#6852. How a Connected Home Supports a Good Night’s Rest

How a wellness content creator uses Hubspace from The Home Depot to curate an environment for better sleep.

As a certified holistic health coach and wellness content creator, Carolina Salazar knows the importance of a peaceful wind-down routine before bed. She trusts Hubspace from The Home Depot that powers many of the smart devices in her home, to help ease her into a good night’s sleep.

Hubspace, The Home Depot’s exclusive smart home platform, powers more than 250 products across select private brands—like Hampton Bay, Husky, and Vissani—as well as national brand partners. It effortlessly connects everyday essentials, including smart security, indoor and outdoor lighting, appliances, switches, watering controls, and more.

Some of the Hubspace app’s most practical features help users personalize lighting and improve home security. Hubspace Connect, for example, lets you control compatible lighting from a single switch, while Vacation Mode is a security feature that turns lights and smart plugs on and off while you’re away. One of Salazar’s favorite features is Circadian Rhythm, which automatically transitions lights from cooler, whiter tones to warmer ones as the day goes on.

“I can set my lights to follow my circadian rhythm, so they shift automatically throughout the day,” says Salazar. “And when they start to turn warm in the evening, that’s my cue to slow it down.” That can mean cozying up with a book, brewing a cup of hot tea, or, in her case, lighting a candle at her bedside and journaling. When Salazar is finished putting her thoughts to paper, she uses the Hubspace app to adjust the color, temperature, and brightness of her lights, dimming them as she gets closer to sleep. This can even be programmed automatically in order to reduce screen time before bed.

“Colder rooms bring better sleep, so I also have Hubspace turn on my AC at night,” she says.

The Hubspace app is free to download and easily syncs with your home’s smart devices. You can enable voice control for screen-free commands—also a plus if you’re trying to reduce your pre-sleep screen time. And the app runs on both WiFi and Bluetooth—so even if the WiFi is down, your nighttime routine can still run seamlessly.

#6849. Sophos Launches Tool for Shadow AI Defense

Unauthorized artificial intelligence tools and agents are creating security risks for organizations as AI adoption increases, according to cybersecurity company Sophos. In a recent product announcement, the company highlighted the growing use of shadow AI, referring to AI tools and agents operating without formal organizational approval or oversight. The security firm cited research indicating that 69% of organizations suspect or have evidence of unauthorized AI use.

Separate research from Gartner and Cloud Security Alliance found that 82% of organizations discovered shadow AI agents in their environments during the previous year.

According to the security firm, organizations have struggled to identify which AI tools employees use, what information those tools access, and whether their use complies with security policies.

"Organizations are adopting AI faster than they can secure it, and most security teams can't answer three basic questions: which AI tools are running, what data is going into them, and whether that activity lines up with policy," said Kyle Falkenhagen, senior vice president of product management at Sophos.

The company also cited an IDC projection that the number of AI agents could reach 1.3 billion by 2028.

Sophos researchers reported observing attackers use AI agents within a customer's network to build and test nearly 80 malware modules.

The cybersecurity firm announced the general availability of Sophos AI Defense, a security offering designed to identify AI tools, monitor their activity, and enforce organizational security policies.

The product operates through Sophos Fusion, the company's cybersecurity platform, and uses information from devices, networks, identity systems, and browsers.

According to the company, AI Defense can inspect prompts, uploads, and other AI interactions to identify potentially risky activity and help prevent sensitive data exposure.

For more information, go to the Sophos site.

California Governor Gavin Newsom is proposing a new model of artificial intelligence regulation that would give independent organizations a formal role in evaluating the safety claims made by AI giants.

Cisco has introduced Privileged Infrastructure Access, an approach to infrastructure administration that gives people and AI agents temporary, task-specific permissions instead of leaving standing privileges available after their work is done.

Avantis Education, the creator of ClassVR, has introduced two new immersive learning libraries for its Eduverse+ platform: EscapeRooms, a math suite for K-8 students, and LingoLab, a Spanish and English language learning suite for students in grades 6-12.

As schools become more dependent on digital platforms for instruction, grading, student services, and family communication, resilience depends not only on preventing attacks, but on how quickly leaders can restore trusted data, recover essential services, and keep disruptions from cascading across the school community.

#6847. 7 Holiday Decor Trends Designers Say We're About to See Everywhere

The pros are leaning into handmade holiday style.

Pictured above: A holiday-ready space by designer Marie Flanigan.

Christmas is just around the corner, and as the holiday spirit makes its way into our homes, it’s a good time to give your seasonal decor a refresh. This year, on-trend doesn’t have to mean brand-new—designers love decorating with heirloom pieces collected over time.

However, if you’re in the mood to try something a little different, here are seven trends on our radar for a chic Christmas.

This winter, our decor is taking a note from European culture—again. Swedish folk art is emerging as a hot winter trend in both holiday and home decor. Defined by bold red hues, tulip motifs, and S-scrolls, the style blends cozy-core with the heritage style craze.

“European folk influences are so big right now,” says designer Johanna Lyle of Jo Lyle & Co. “Think Swedish Dala horses, quilt motifs, and decorative stitching. I think we will see those continue into holiday decor.”

When it comes to her tree decor, interior designer Francesca Grace waves a hand at the carefully contrived, and she’s not alone. “My ornaments are collected from my travels, so the tree is really a collection of memories. I love unpacking them and remembering where each one came from. They don’t all match, and I wouldn’t want them to,” says Grace. “I let the ornaments lead. That mix feels much more personal to me, and it gives the tree something new to say as the collection grows.” This year’s trends hinge on antiqued, inherited style, with one-off pieces that channel bold character and hardly ever come with a matching set.

Interior designer Marie Flanigan likes to add a fresh twist to her seasonal decor with dried oranges and pops of cranberry red.

“We've seen such a beautiful resurgence of dried citrus in holiday decorating over the past few years,” Flanigan explains. “And I think cranberries are ready for their moment to shine. Their rich red hue feels especially festive, and I love the idea of incorporating them into fresh greenery, draping them across a mantel, or pairing them with dried oranges for a more layered look.”

If there’s one classic Christmas element Grace hopes makes a comeback, it's multicolored lights. Inherently nostalgic, these bold-hued bulbs were a ’90s staple.

“I’m ready for a little less restraint on the tree. Those reds, blues, greens, and yellows immediately bring back the feeling of Christmas as a child. They also pull together a collection of ornaments that might otherwise seem completely unrelated. White lights can be beautiful, but I want my Christmas tree to have some mischief,” says Grace.

The classic red-and-green combination may take the title for the most iconic holiday palette, but this year, color trends are going complementary. While base colors stay warm and slightly rusted, patterned pieces are taking on cooler tones. This year, we’re also trading traditional plaid for Scottish tartan.

“Warm crimsons, rich browns, tactile fabrics, and blue-green plaids make a beautiful holiday palette,” says Elizabeth Bennett, cofounder of the interior design firm Kobel + Co.

In a total rejection of mass-manufactured everything, handmade felt pieces are taking holiday homes by storm. The style has even coined a new holiday aesthetic: #Kitschmas, and, according to Lyle, it’s here to stay. “As AI continues to homogenize design, things made by humans will feel more special and important. Handmade and crafted items create stories around decor that feel meaningful and can be treasured year after year,” she says.

Moody browns are steadily emerging as the new neutral as we inch closer to the new year, and lucky for our winter design schemes, the hue really is as versatile as it seems. Paired with powdery blues, bold crimsons, and even minty greens, chocolate brown grounds nearly any palette and matches every metallic hue.

“I’m particularly drawn to chocolate brown as an unexpected foundation for holiday color palettes. It's incredibly rich and versatile. I love the idea of moving beyond traditional red and green and allowing the existing colors of a room to inform the holiday decor. You can create something incredibly festive through tonal layering, subtle metallic accents, and variations in material,” Flanigan explains.

#6843. Cecilie Bahnsen Rethinks the Fleece for Her Second Uniqlo Collaboration

The collection is a Scandi winter balletcore dream.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Cecilie Bahnsen has a cult following for a reason: the Danish designer’s big blooming dresses and dedication to sporty, easy balletcore have been collected in droves by fashion far and wide. So when Uniqlo came calling for her to design a second collaboration, it was time again for the designer to take the reins and merge her playful girlish Scandi style with the utilitarian wearability of the Japanese giant. The collection, which includes womenswear and childrenswear, puts a focus on cozy winter wardrobe dressing and is available now. In it, Bahnsen is rethinking and retooling the classic winter fleece.

For the occasion, Bahnsen spent half a year designing the collection and also collaborated with her friend Fumiko Imano, the artist, to shoot the campaign in Sweden amidst a picturesque landscape. “It was very much about sisterhood, the journey you do of exploring together, and inspiration also from when I’m in the countryside with my son, and we collect flowers, and how we kind of, he sometimes pinned them on his jumper,” Bahnsen tells ELLE of the new collection. “I think it was really kind of that romantic or nostalgic feeling that I wanted to put into the collection. And then there was also this notion of just working with one fabrication and working with the fleece for winter and how you can work this as layering.”

While Bahnsen in particular is known for her flouncy babydoll dresses, when it came to her second collection for Uniqlo, she tells us it was all about “refinement and balance and creating beautiful kind of everyday pieces.” Her personal favorite piece of the lineup is the fleece in the palest shade of soft pastel pink, embroidered with small flowers. “For me, the fleece is such a classic layering piece,” she says. She’s already been wearing it herself for countryside outings. “There’s romance and nostalgia to pink, and I think with this collaboration, it was nice to do the fleece in pink,” she says. “Showing that it could have that femininity to it was special, and it is also that right tone as a super soft pink that I love.”

The entire collection is designed to be layered, and the fleece is at the center of it. There’s a cropped fleece vest, a sheer long T-shirt printed with subtle bouquets of flowers, a floral-embellished fleece skirt and matching socks, gloves, and hats. Bahnsen herself recommends taking a cue from her own runway shows. “I always love a big jumper or a big fleece over a poof-y dress,” she says. “It has both a super boyish vibe to it but also a princess-y, romantic feeling, too, so I love those kinds of contrasts between each other.”

“These are pieces that you want to not just to work for one season, but to be timeless; it’s become about the perfection of these small details that makes it unique and maybe more romantic than what is normally in the Uniqlo collection,” adds Bahnsen. “I feel like the palette has a really Scandinavian winter tone to it, so how do you balance this soft pink into the darker blacks and blues, and kind of build it up, not in a monocomatic way, but really putting the tones and the layering together.” It’s definitely a chicer way to stay warm. The designer’s latest is the ultimate study of form and function.

#6842. Microsoft Expands AI Security Platform to Address Agentic AI Security Risks

Microsoft is expanding the scope of its AI-powered Project Perception security platform to address the growing concern that AI agents may have access to sensitive information and systems, yet don't always behave as expected.

In a company blog post, Hayete Gallot, executive vice president of Microsoft Security, outlined how Microsoft plans to use Project Perception to monitor, manage, and contain what it calls "agentic insiders." These are AI agents deployed within an environment that can access data, use software tools, and make decisions without constant human supervision. While they can improve productivity, Microsoft warns that they also introduce security risks, even when they haven't been compromised by an attacker.

"Agents are unpredictable," Gallot wrote. "They will adapt as the context changes, may not behave as intended, and will collaborate and collude with other agents to reach their objectives, without needing to be compromised."

Project Perception was first introduced in July. The platform, which entered public preview Aug. 3, is designed to help security teams respond to increasingly sophisticated cyber threats using AI-powered automation.

Instead of leaning only on traditional security alerts and manual investigations, Project Perception uses three kinds of specialized AI agents working together. Red team agents hunt for possible vulnerabilities, blue team agents look into suspicious activity, and green team agents step in to fix problems and strengthen defenses.

To spot threats and respond faster, the system pulls in security data from identities, endpoints, cloud environments, applications, and other sources.

Now Microsoft wants to use those same capabilities on the AI agents already running inside enterprise networks. Project Perception would find those agents, give them identities and permissions, and keep an eye on what they do across different systems. It would also keep assessing the risks that come with their actions.

Microsoft also described a plan for "Guardian Agents," specialized AI agents that supervise other agents and step in when their behavior gets risky. That might mean limiting an agent's permissions or blocking certain actions before they cause harm.

"Monitoring and securing those agents must become continuous," Gallot wrote.

The initiative also reflects concerns raised by Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella in an Oct. 10 essay on AI security.

Nadella argued that enterprises should treat powerful AI models much like potential insider security risks, particularly when those models can access confidential information or perform critical business operations.

"We simply can't outsource responsibility for what intelligence does on our behalf," Nadella wrote.

He called for security controls that operate independently of AI models, including restricted permissions, detailed activity logging, and mechanisms that allow administrators to stop an agent's actions.

"In other words, we need to separate the supply of intelligence from the authority over it," Nadella wrote.

Microsoft admits that watching what AI agents do is only part of the answer. Gallot said that future security tools will also need to show why AI models make the choices they do, not just what actions they end up taking.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

Sign-in problems account for up to 53% of help desk tickets at individual enterprises, but network issues can take significantly longer to resolve, according to a recent study from ControlUp.

OpenAI has introduced GPT-6 Astra, its most capable broadly deployed model and the first system the company says has reached the "Critical" cybersecurity capability threshold under its Preparedness Framework.

Organizations are running into problems as they give artificial intelligence more control over critical business applications, according to a new survey from identity security firm Delinea.

AI is moving beyond assisting cybercriminals and beginning to operate within live attacks, according to a recent report from Check Point Research. The technology is making sophisticated capabilities faster and more accessible while creating new security risks for businesses deploying their own AI systems.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

We found items for everyone, from the new hire to the retiree.

A lot of us commiserate about commuting into the office, but there are a few positives to seeing your coworkers regularly, like finding your fellow movie buffs to discuss the latest films with, or bonding with the colleagues you grab drinks with after work. Another upside? Your group may do white elephant gift exchanges, Secret Santa, or at the very least, have an office holiday party. If you are doing a present swap this season, finding creative, affordable gifts for your coworkers could be tricky.

First, there’s determining the budget; then it’s figuring out whether to get them something practical, fun, edible, or just plain silly. Our thoughtful recommendation is to get a gift they can use immediately or leave at work, like a cute tissue box cover, hand warmers (because the office is always cold), or even a small diffuser that makes the air around them smell better. We found 35 such items they’ll love, and if they truly want to, they could bring and use them at home too.

#6836. Azzedine Alaïa Changed Fashion on His Own Terms

A new exhibition showcases 80 garments from the rule-breaking designer.

In the 1980s, Azzedine Alaïa acquired a title that followed him for the rest of his career: the “King of Cling.” The moniker was a fitting one: the Tunisian couturier was renowned for his sculptural, body-centric designs. He experimented with stretch jersey and “anti-stress” fibers to sculpt the body without restricting movement.

But the nickname was also limiting, failing to capture the full scope of his work. The first major U.S. retrospective devoted to Alaïa, “Azzedine Alaïa: Sculpting Fashion,” at San Francisco’s Legion of Honor, brings his broader legacy into focus through 80 garments and selections from his personal collection of historic fashion.

“As my research went on, I came to realize how important the U.S. market was in Alaïa’s launch,” Laura L. Camerlengo, co-curator of the exhibit and curator in charge of Costume and Textile Arts at the Fine Arts Museums of San Francisco, tells ELLE. “By the [brand’s] third season, the U.S. was the second highest market after France, [with interest from] both luxury department stores and small independent retailers.”

The exhibition took shape after Camerlengo reached out to Olivier Saillard, director of the Fondation Azzedine Alaïa in Paris, who became her co-curator. As visitors enter the Legion of Honor’s Rosekrans Court, they encounter a dress designed for Grace Jones: a spring/summer 1986 haute couture look that Jones modeled on the runway and wore to the 1985 Oscars de la Mode. Part of the opening display, titled “The Women Who Made Alaïa,” it sets the tone for a show that recognizes the muses—both famous and private clients—who wore and supported his work.

Also on view are looks made famous by Rihanna—namely, a sheer red halter gown with a flowing train that she wore to the 2013 Grammys—and Tina Turner, whose silk-organza dress embroidered with gold-tone beads from Alaïa’s spring/summer 1989 concludes the exhibition.

The show then turns to Alaïa’s iconography, as Camerlengo puts it: the bandage dress, his work in denim, and zippered dresses, as well as rare, lesser-seen gems. “I was really keen to have early works on view, particularly the pieces from the 1980s, when he was launching his brand,” the curator says. “These are pieces that would have been shown at Bergdorf Goodman when he had his first fashion show in the United States.”

The exhibition also places Alaïa’s designs in conversation with the museum’s permanent collection. Belle Epoque-style ensembles from his spring/summer 1992 collection appear among 18th-century European paintings and furnishings, including a Canaletto view of Venice and a sofa commissioned for Marie Antoinette.

Alaïa’s own collection of historic fashion, previously shown in the 2023 Paris exhibition “Azzedine Alaïa, couturier collectionneur,” is represented here, too. The gallery includes pieces by fashion houses he admired, such as Lanvin, Chanel, Balenciaga, and Schiaparelli.

Though Alaïa, who died in 2017 at age 82, is remembered for the body-conscious designs that earned him that “King of Cling” title, he was also a renegade in an industry built around constant production. He showed collections only when he felt they were ready—a maverick approach that still resonates today—and had his creations worn by a who’s who of the modeling world, from Naomi Campbell to Gisele Bündchen. Many of his designs were featured in the pages of ELLE over the decades.

“He was someone who pioneered a new way of thinking about fashion,” Camerlengo says. “He was one of the first to question the fashion system and seasonal calendars and the need for trends. He was focused on meticulous craftsmanship and intentionality in design, a sort of precedent to what we think about today, where we are critical of overproduction and overconsumption in fashion.”

“Azzedine Alaïa: Sculpting Fashion” is on view at the Legion of Honor Museum from Nov. 21, 2026, to Apr. 11, 2027.

#6835. Survey: Organizations Report Positive ROI on AI Investments, Yet Data Readiness and Oversight Lag Behind

According to a survey by data and AI services provider Further, enterprises are reporting benefits from artificial intelligence even as their data preparation and oversight processes remain incomplete.

The company's recent State of Enterprise AI Maturity research surveyed 515 U.S.-based AI decision-makers. Just 5% believed their organizations' data was largely ready for AI.

The findings suggest that gaining value from individual AI applications and preparing to operate them more broadly are separate challenges. Reported returns alone do not establish that an organization has the foundations to sustain or expand those results.

Further found that 37% of respondents' organizations had paused, scaled back, or abandoned an underperforming AI initiative in the previous 12 months. Among that group, data quality or availability was the most frequently cited main reason, at 24%.

Meanwhile, 29% reported completing a formal data audit and remediation plan. The research also placed 69% of organizations in the exploring, experimenting, or implementing stages of adoption.

Further distinguishes data prepared for conventional reporting from data prepared for AI. Its explanation emphasizes data origins and documentation, along with access controls that support ongoing evaluation and governance.

For readers assessing their own readiness, the practical issue is whether data supports the requirements of a particular application. A collection suitable for a dashboard may need additional preparation before it can reliably support an AI system.

Seventy-seven percent reported a positive return on AI investment. However, only 3% said their organizations had a documented AI-audit process.

The findings measure different things: perceived financial benefit and the existence of a repeatable oversight process. Neither substitutes for the other.

"In reality, confidence without a documented process is exposure," said Kierra Dotson, associate director of AI strategy and governance at Further. "Closing that gap doesn't take new technology; it requires an auditable governance process."

For the full report, visit the Further site (registration required).

California Governor Gavin Newsom is proposing a new model of artificial intelligence regulation that would give independent organizations a formal role in evaluating the safety claims made by AI giants.

Cisco has introduced Privileged Infrastructure Access, an approach to infrastructure administration that gives people and AI agents temporary, task-specific permissions instead of leaving standing privileges available after their work is done.

Avantis Education, the creator of ClassVR, has introduced two new immersive learning libraries for its Eduverse+ platform: EscapeRooms, a math suite for K-8 students, and LingoLab, a Spanish and English language learning suite for students in grades 6-12.

As schools become more dependent on digital platforms for instruction, grading, student services, and family communication, resilience depends not only on preventing attacks, but on how quickly leaders can restore trusted data, recover essential services, and keep disruptions from cascading across the school community.

#6831. Safe Word: The Best Jewelry Launches of November 2026

Safe Word is a series highlighting the latest trends in the jewelry market, keeping you up to date on all things that sparkle and shine.

November’s jewelry launches are looking both backward and forward, with designers revisiting their roots, reworking archival ideas, and stepping into entirely new territory. First up, Quentin Jones is taking her surrealist art off the wall and onto the body with “Nocturne,” her first jewelry collection; Pomellato reaches back to 1997 to revive “Moneta Orientale” as a colorful modern talisman; David Yurman celebrates the magic of a New York holiday with Michael B. Jordan, Yerin Ha, and Inde Navarrette; Vhernier revisits its iconic “Trottola” design with new proportions and contrasting materials; Di Volpe reinterprets the classic tennis bracelet with customizable gemstones in “DiSegno”; Brent Neale transforms the classic bow into sculptural fine jewelry with “Wrap It Up”; and Kendra Scott launches the new “Kendra Scott Collection,” reimagining her signature Spike motif as a personal talisman. From archival revivals and meaningful motifs to innovative designs and new creative directions, November’s launches prove that sometimes the best way forward starts with looking back. For the rest of the month, we’ve got you covered on all the jewelry-related happenings worth knowing about.

Quentin Jones is taking her art off the wall and onto the body with “Nocturne,” the first jewelry collection from House of Quentin Jones. Following the multidisciplinary artist’s debut of surrealist ceramics and tapestries last year, the new collection transforms recurring gestures and symbols from her work into sculptural collars, cuffs, armbands, pendants, chains, and earrings. Eyes, hands, stars, moons, tears, and knots take shape in silver and gold vermeil alongside freshwater pearls, onyx, opals, jade, glass, and silk tassels, giving the pieces a dreamlike, talismanic quality. Beginning as drawings and hand collages in Jones’s Brooklyn studio, each design carries her surrealist visual language into wearable form, further blurring the line between art, design, and fashion.

Piotrek Panszczyk Makes His Debut at Ippolita

Ippolita is entering a new chapter under creative director Piotrek Panszczyk, who makes his debut at the house with “Ancient Bohemia,” a collection inspired by its archives and the organic forms of nature. Taking a sculptural approach to jewelry, Panszczyk reimagines familiar signatures with an emphasis on texture, craftsmanship, and unconventional layering. The house’s iconic Wavy Disk becomes the Wavy Coin, featuring Hippolyta, Queen of the Amazons and the brand’s namesake, as a symbol of feminine strength. Elsewhere, hand-sculpted Stardust pavé is set entirely with diamonds, pearls make their first appearance, and lightweight, hand-hammered silver Roma links offer multiple ways to wear, from necklaces to belts. Lapis lazuli, mother-of-pearl, and quartz introduce color through Ippolita’s signature layered doublets and triplets, mixed rather than matched for an eclectic finish. The result is a fresh interpretation of the house’s heritage that feels both ancient and distinctly modern.

Jade Trau and Luis Morais Combine Gemstones and Diamonds

Jade Trau and Luis Morais are joining forces for a limited-edition collection that brings together their different approaches to fine jewelry. Comprised of 16 one-of-a-kind pieces, including 12 bracelets and four necklaces, the collaboration pairs Morais’s colorful gemstone beads with Trau’s signature 18-karat gold and diamond elements. The designs feature a vibrant mix of stones, from chrysoprase and amazonite to lapis lazuli, turquoise, carnelian, and tiger’s-eye, accented by recognizable Jade Trau details like the Frankie rondelle, Sophisticate station, and Georgie diamond lock. Designed with everyday wear in mind, the bracelets lend themselves to layering, while the necklaces incorporate Trau’s diamond-set closures. The result is a playful meeting of color and craftsmanship that brings a fresh perspective to both designers’ signatures.

Lagos Looks to the Stars With Its Latest Collection

Lagos references the night sky in its latest fine jewelry collection, taking inspiration from the North Star and its centuries-old symbolism of guidance and possibility. Designed to celebrate milestones, new beginnings, and meaningful connections, the “North Star” collection features sculptural star motifs crafted in 18-karat gold and illuminated by natural diamonds. Cascading silhouettes and light-catching details bring a sense of movement to the celestial designs, balancing statement-making shapes with delicate craftsmanship. Both symbolic and sophisticated, the assortment offers a modern interpretation of a timeless emblem, making each piece a reminder of the people and moments that guide us.

Kendra Scott Launches an Eponymous Collection

Kendra Scott is offering a more personal look into her design world with the launch of the “Kendra Scott Collection,” a new line spanning fashion and fine jewelry. Its first edition, “The Spike,” revisits a silhouette the designer has worn throughout her career as a personal symbol of strength, confidence, and conviction. Inspired by nature and the radiance of the night sky, the collection translates the pointed motif into sculptural necklaces, earrings, bracelets, and charms, balancing bold proportions with intricate craftsmanship. Fashion jewelry incorporates tiger’s eye, malachite, and turquoise, while fine jewelry introduces lab-grown diamonds, pavé detailing, labradorite, and lapis. From statement-making links to delicate diamond charms, the collection offers a new interpretation of Scott’s longtime signature, inviting wearers to make the symbol their own.

Alison Lou’s ‘New Wave’ Collection Experiments With New Materials

Alison Lou is entering a new chapter with “New Wave,” a fine jewelry collection that introduces unexpected materials and a fresh approach to the designer’s signature playful aesthetic. Exploring the relationship between movement and form, the collection pairs lightweight titanium and ceramic with lab-grown diamonds, creating fluid, sculptural silhouettes that curve and catch the light. Available in Fig and Denim Blue colorways, the designs include wavy rings, diamond-accented signets, delicate thread necklaces, and statement drop earrings. With its unconventional materials and easy-to-wear designs, “New Wave” brings a new sense of movement to Alison Lou’s everyday jewelry.

Jenny Bird and Emese Gormley Partner on the Anyu Ring

Jenny Bird is expanding her JB FINE collection with a new collaboration featuring lifestyle creator and Lipstick on the Rim podcast host Emese Gormley. Named in honor of Gormley’s mother, the Anyu Ring offers a personal take on the classic signet, featuring three lab-grown baguette diamonds set in 14-karat yellow or white gold. The understated design combines the timeless appeal of a signet ring with a sentimental tribute to family, bringing a new perspective to Bird’s growing fine jewelry offering. Available exclusively on Jenny Bird’s website, the collaboration makes a case for jewelry that feels as meaningful as it looks.

Pomellato Revives Its 1997 Moneta Orientale Pendant as a Colorful Talisman

Pomellato is looking to its archives for its latest good-luck charm. For the newest chapter of “Memorie d’Archivio,” the maison revives Moneta Orientale, a 1997 pendant inspired by the ancient symbolism of a circle surrounding a square, representing the harmony between heaven and earth. Made in 33 stone-and-pavé combinations, the design pairs colorful hard stones including black jade, amazonite, sodalite, rhodochrosite, and labradorite with Pomellato’s signature irregular pavé of diamonds, sapphires, rubies, and more. Offered in two proportions, each pendant transforms the archival motif into a modern talisman for prosperity and protection.

Bibi van der Velden Makes Her High Jewelry Debut With ‘Eclipse’

Bibi van der Velden is entering a new era with “Eclipse,” the designer’s first high jewelry collection and most refined exploration of diamonds to date. Inspired by the fleeting moment when light gives way to darkness, the collection includes two six-piece suites: one in white diamonds and another defined by an ombré of diamonds into gray spinels deepening into black. Van der Velden’s signature alligator motif is abstracted into fluid, sculptural silhouettes, with scales engraved into the white gold on the reverse of each piece. “With Eclipse, I wanted to take those familiar elements and transform them into something more abstract and sculptural,” she says. “It’s a collection about transformation, which has always been at the heart of my work.”

Aflalo and Leandra Medine Cohen Turn Everyday Indulgences Into Fine Jewelry

Aflalo is finding the humor in our obsession with wellness with “The Vanities,” a new two-piece fine jewelry capsule designed exclusively by Leandra Medine Cohen. Playing with the contradiction between longevity and life’s little indulgences, the collection transforms two unlikely everyday accessories into precious objects. The Memento Mori is a 14-karat yellow gold cigar band set with more than four carats of diamonds, designed to slip over an Oura Ring, while The Libertine is a sterling silver pendant case adorned with diamonds and a wild-haired woman outlined in gold, designed to hold nicotine pouches or other small essentials. Equal parts extravagant and irreverent, the collection makes a playful case for enjoying life’s pleasures without sacrificing personal style.

Julia Berolzheimer’s Hart Jewelry Collection Draws on Childhood Memories

Hart is teaming up with Julia Berolzheimer for a new jewelry collection that celebrates the art of the stack. Inspired by Berolzheimer’s childhood memories of her grandmother’s California ranch and her collection of Art Deco minaudières, the collaboration brings together whimsical charms, colorful gemstones, and meaningful talismans designed to be mixed, layered, and restyled. Botanical motifs take shape in Tree of Life coins, daisies, bees, and fruit-inspired charms, while carnelian, turquoise, and pink opal add playful pops of color. One standout is the Perfume Vesta Case, a modern interpretation of a Victorian-era accessory that can hold a sample-sized fragrance and hang alongside other charms. With interchangeable chains, cords, and pendants, the collection makes a case for jewelry that feels just as personal as the memories behind it.

David Yurman Captures the Magic of a New York Holiday

David Yurman is celebrating the magic of the holidays in New York City with a new campaign featuring global brand ambassador Michael B. Jordan alongside actresses Yerin Ha and Inde Navarrette. Set against a snow-covered Washington Square Park, the campaign captures a series of quintessential New York moments, from Jordan and Ha facing off in a spirited card game to Navarrette hailing a cab in search of the perfect holiday gift. Throughout the campaign, the cast wears some of the house’s most recognizable designs, including 18-karat gold Cable bracelets and pieces from the “Starburst” and “Armory” collections. Directed by Bradford Young and photographed by Drew Vickers, the campaign brings together the spirit of the city, the joy of togetherness, and the timeless appeal of David Yurman’s jewelry.

Vhernier Reintroduces Its Iconic ‘Trottola’ Design With New Proportions

Vhernier is revisiting one of its most recognizable designs with a new interpretation of “Trottola,” the sculptural collection originally introduced in 2003. Inspired by the continuous motion of a spinning top, the latest designs play with proportions, contrasting materials, and asymmetrical shapes across rings, bracelets, necklaces, and earrings. Polished rose gold meets glossy black jet, with select pieces featuring diamond pavé for an added contrast of light and dark. The collection also focuses on versatility, from rings designed for stacking to convertible earclips that can be worn as studs or dangling earrings. With its fluid forms and unexpected material combinations, “Trottola” brings a fresh perspective to Vhernier’s signature sculptural approach to jewelry.

Di Volpe is rethinking the classic tennis bracelet with “DiSegno,” a new customizable fine jewelry system designed to change with its wearer. Using concealed, patent-pending connections, individual cultured diamonds and gemstones, called Elements, can be removed, rearranged, or added by hand, allowing wearers to transform bracelets into necklaces or earrings without compromising the seamless look of traditional tennis jewelry. The collection launches with 28 stone-and-cut combinations, including rubies, emeralds, sapphires, and diamonds, alongside a limited-edition collaboration with Lebanese artist Ali Cha’aban featuring five sculptural designs. With new cuts, colors, and artist collaborations planned for future releases, “DiSegno” makes the case for fine jewelry that’s never quite finished.

Brent Neale Riffs on the Bow in Fine Jewelry

Brent Neale is putting a bow on the holiday season with “Wrap It Up,” a new fine jewelry collection that transforms the familiar ribbon motif into sculptural works of art. Drawing inspiration from Old Hollywood glamour and the femininity of the 1960s, the designer plays with the contrast between rigid precious metals and the soft movement of fabric, crafting bows that twist, curl, and unfurl in 18-karat gold and platinum. Colored gemstones sit nestled within the ribbon-like forms, while pavé diamonds accentuate every curve and fold. Equal parts playful and precious, the collection brings a little gift-wrapped glamour to the jewelry box.

Grace Winfrey’s ‘Poppy Fortuna’ Collection Reimagines Good-Luck Charms

Grace Winfrey is bringing a little extra luck to the jewelry box with “Poppy Fortuna,” a new fine jewelry collection inspired by the natural beauty of the Italian landscape and symbols of good fortune. Scarlet poppies, sunflowers, bees, four-leaf clovers, horseshoes, and ladybugs come to life through vibrant gemstones and intricate pavé, creating miniature mosaics of color and texture. Highlights include a Lucky Bangle combining three good-luck charms, sculptural sunflower earrings with tiny ladybugs tucked into their petals, and layered charm necklaces designed to feel like modern keepsakes. Handcrafted in New York City, each piece transforms familiar symbols of prosperity, protection, and happiness into playful, colorful works of art.

Robinson Pelham Channels ’90s Glamour for Its Anniversary

Robinson Pelham is closing out its 30th anniversary celebrations with “Suite 96,” a new campaign and collection launch inspired by the glamour of late-’90s London. Set inside a private suite at St. James’s Hotel & Club in Mayfair, the campaign considers jewelry the ultimate room service, with diamonds arriving on trays, gold spilling out of wardrobes, and plenty of pieces to layer before a night out. At the center is “Studio 30,” a four-piece anniversary collection featuring diamond necklaces and statement earrings, including the sculptural Via and Hira necklaces, cascading diamond drops, and the bold Solstice Supreme. Alongside the anniversary designs, the house introduces new collections and additions to existing favorites, from chunky gold links and graphic heart rings to colorful enamel and gemstone combinations. Together, the launches celebrate three decades of Robinson Pelham’s bold, playful approach to fine jewelry.

Le Vian and Jared Revive Georgian-Era Romance

Le Vian is bringing a little old-world romance to Jared Jewelers with its new exclusive “Georgian Chocolate & Nude Diamonds” collection. Inspired by the candlelit jewels of the Georgian era, when jewelry was designed to capture and reflect the warmth of natural light, the collection rethinks historic buttercup settings in polished Honey Gold, with eight rounded prongs framing each natural diamond. The designs pair Le Vian’s signature Chocolate and Nude Diamonds with warm gold detailing across solitaire rings, anniversary bands, tennis bracelets, necklaces, and earrings. Select styles introduce colorful gemstones including Chocolate Quartz, Green Apple Peridot, and Grape Amethyst, giving the vintage-inspired silhouettes a contemporary update. With its sculptural settings and warm-toned stones, the collection brings the romance of antique jewelry into the present.

Wempe’s ‘Au:thentic’ Celebrates the Art of Goldsmithing

Wempe’s new gender-inclusive fine jewelry collection, “Au:thentic,” puts craftsmanship front and center, finding inspiration in the foundational skills taught to aspiring goldsmiths. The collection takes its shape from the jump ring, one of the first techniques Patrick Sieroslawski learned as an apprentice more than 22 years ago, translating its form to necklaces, bracelets, rings, earrings, and pendants. Handcrafted at Wempe’s atelier in Germany, the pieces come in 18-karat yellow, white, and rose gold, with select styles set with diamonds. “Au:thentic” also marks the beginning of a multiyear rollout, with three additional chapters set to expand on its design language through twists, wraps, and interlocking links.

#6829. Holiday Travel With Babies and Pets Just Got a Whole Lot Easier

Smart travel essentials for the whole family—four-legged members included.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Seasonal travel is exciting, but getting everyone from point A to point B can be a production—especially when babies and pets are involved. Between navigating crowded airports, packing the car with what feels like half your house, and keeping your four-legged family members calm amid all the chaos, there’s a ton to think about. And while you can’t control flight delays, holiday traffic, or the timing of a much-needed diaper change, having the right travel gear can make a world of difference.

Enter Nuna and Tavo, two brands that bring thoughtful design, innovative safety features, and a beautiful aesthetic to (often hectic) family travel. Nuna’s premium baby gear helps take some of the stress out of everything from airport security to car-seat installations, while Tavo focuses the same level of attention on the safety and comfort of travel essentials designed specifically for pets. Think lightweight strollers that fold with one hand (because your other one is probably occupied), car seats that don’t require an online tutorial to install, and comfortable carriers that give your furry companions a safe spot of their own.

Whether you’re flying across the country to visit the grandparents, packing up for a holiday road trip, or exploring a new destination together, these six Nuna and Tavo essentials, available at Bloomingdale’s, can help make the journey a little more joyful for everyone involved.

Given crowded security lines, blowouts at the worst possible moment, and the sheer amount of stuff you have to tote, a lightweight stroller is a genuine lifesaver at the airport. At just 16.3 pounds, the Nuna VIAA cabn is designed to meet IATA cabin-size standards when folded with the armbar removed—though you’ll still want to double-check your airline’s requirements. One-handed steering makes navigating crowded terminals easier (especially when you’re juggling a much-needed iced coffee), while a roomy storage basket holds up to 22 pounds of diaper bags, coats, backup outfits, bottles, snacks, and approximately 47 things to keep your baby entertained. It even comes with a carrier bag for when it’s time to board.

This Nuna duo pairs the TRIV lx stroller, which folds with one hand and stands on its own, with the ultralight PIPA urbn infant car seat. And if you’ve ever broken a sweat or found yourself YouTubing car seat installation instructions for the 100th time, you’ll appreciate that this one clicks into place without a base, using its built-in pipaFIX rigid latch installation. Large rear wheels and all-wheel suspension pair with a spacious storage basket and handy tracking-device pocket to make the stroller ready for everyday outings.

The Nuna SWIV’s standout feature is button-activated 360-degree rotation on all four wheels, allowing it to move sideways and pivot through tight spaces without the struggle of squeezing through crowded airport lines and store aisles that clearly weren’t designed with strollers in mind. A one-handed recline with three positions, including a near-flat option, makes adjustments easy, and the stroller is travel-system-ready with any Nuna PIPA series infant car seat, allowing you to move baby from the car to the stroller.

The Tavo Crispin gives your pet a roomy, comfortable spot of their own, with mesh panels for ventilation and visibility so they can still keep eyes on their humans. It’s impact-tested and secures to your vehicle using lower anchors and a top tether, with a protective memory-foam guard for added peace of mind. And when the holiday travels are over, it collapses for easy storage without taking up precious space at home.

This Tavo travel system might be your new favorite on-the-road companion. The Maeve car seat attaches to the Roscoe Trek stroller frame, so you can go from the car to a festive outing without having to transfer your pup to a different carrier. All-terrain rear wheels and suspension make bumpy sidewalks easier to navigate, while one-touch brakes and a compact stroller fold come in handy when it’s time for a coffee stop or to load everything back into the car.

There’s a very particular type of heart-rate spike that comes with flying with a pet. You have to stand in the airline’s special-assistance line with your (probably nervous) dog or cat, only to finally reach the counter and hold your breath as the agent (and sometimes a supervisor) decides whether their carrier fulfills the rules. The Tavo Dupree II has a chic carry-on design that’s also crash-tested, complete with built-in vehicle connectors and a protective memory-foam guard. Mesh panels keep air circulating, while a trolley sleeve lets you slip it over your suitcase handle instead of carrying everything separately. You’ll still want to double-check your airline’s requirements before flying.

#6827. Researchers Find AI Is Accelerating Vulnerability Discovery

AI-powered vulnerability research is contributing to a major increase in security updates, raising concerns that IT teams could fall behind as patching demands grow, according to cybersecurity company ThreatDown.

In a recent blog post, the company stated that in September 2026, vendors addressed 998 common vulnerabilities and exposures (CVEs) around Patch Tuesday, compared with 96 during the same period last year. In addition, the three-month rolling average of patched Linux kernel vulnerabilities tripled year over year, while Google's Chrome browser experienced a 36-fold increase.

The researchers point to advanced AI models, including Anthropic's Claude Mythos Preview, as a major factor behind the increase in vulnerability discovery.

Introduced in April 2026, Mythos Preview is being used through Project Glasswing, an initiative involving Microsoft, the Linux Foundation and other organizations to identify vulnerabilities in critical software.

While AI could ultimately make software more secure, the cybersecurity company warns that vulnerability discovery is accelerating faster than organizations can deploy fixes.

IT teams must still evaluate patches, test compatibility and schedule updates without disrupting critical business operations.

The challenge is particularly significant for organizations already struggling with patch management.

The cybersecurity vendor cites Verizon's 2026 Data Breach Investigations Report, which found that 35% of known actively exploited vulnerabilities remained unpatched after 28 days in 2025.

Ryan Powell, vice president of IT and security at ThreatDown, said automated patching systems can help organizations manage the growing workload. However, additional patches still require more testing and validation, increasing operational overhead and the risks associated with frequent system changes.

The report also highlights the risks of vulnerabilities in widely used software components such as OpenSSL, embedded in applications, network devices, and security products.

Unlike standalone applications, these components often depend on downstream vendors to release updates before organizations can deploy fixes.

The security firm's analysis points to CVE-2022-0778, an OpenSSL vulnerability patched in March 2022, as an example. Some affected products did not receive updates until several months later.

As AI accelerates vulnerability discovery, these dependencies could delay remediation and extend organizations' exposure to known security flaws.

ThreatDown's findings illustrate a growing challenge for enterprise cybersecurity: AI is making vulnerabilities easier to discover but fixing them remains a complex and time-consuming process.

For the full report, visit the ThreatDown blog here.

California Governor Gavin Newsom is proposing a new model of artificial intelligence regulation that would give independent organizations a formal role in evaluating the safety claims made by AI giants.

Cisco has introduced Privileged Infrastructure Access, an approach to infrastructure administration that gives people and AI agents temporary, task-specific permissions instead of leaving standing privileges available after their work is done.

Avantis Education, the creator of ClassVR, has introduced two new immersive learning libraries for its Eduverse+ platform: EscapeRooms, a math suite for K-8 students, and LingoLab, a Spanish and English language learning suite for students in grades 6-12.

As schools become more dependent on digital platforms for instruction, grading, student services, and family communication, resilience depends not only on preventing attacks, but on how quickly leaders can restore trusted data, recover essential services, and keep disruptions from cascading across the school community.

#6825. ELLE’s Guide to the Best Black Friday Fashion Sales

Save on designer bags, holiday party looks, jewelry gifts, and cold-weather staples.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

It’s a great time to love fashion. Hundreds of Black Friday sales have already begun—but with so many places to shop, it can be hard to know where to start (and when to stop). To help you maximize your budget, we’ve detailed the places ELLE editors are shopping this year. From major department stores to designer labels, these are the 20 best sites to browse for Black Friday deals ahead of the holiday weekend, organized alphabetically for your convenience.

Aligne is one of ELLE editors’ go-to fashion labels, so we’ve been monitoring the brand’s site for a Black Friday sale. A handful of early markdowns are already live, but we’re more impressed by the Aligne deals we’re seeing at Nordstrom.

We can confirm Alo will be hosting a Black Friday sale. In years past, the brand has offered a sitewide discount, excluding cashmere and Atelier items. It’s worth browsing Alo’s site now to prepare your cart, as popular workout leggings, sports bras, and jackets will sell out fast.

Amazon’s massive Black Friday sale includes thousands of fashion items, including modern cult classics like Ugg boots, Rag & Bone sweatpant jeans, and Ray-Ban sunglasses. The retailer typically matches prices with department stores and DTC websites, so you can do all your sale shopping in one fell swoop.

Banana Republic shoppers can take an extra 20 percent off existing sale items now through November 22. Hold off on purchasing full-priced products until next week—we expect the brand to offer additional deals closer to the holiday.

Coach has markdowns on all kinds of handbags to gift—and boots, loafers, and leather jackets worth keeping for yourself. Beyond the label’s website, expect Nordstrom, Revolve, and Amazon to have Coach deals, too.

ELLE readers can use the promo code ELLE20 to score 20 percent off all DeMellier bags through November 30. Before you shop, check out our guide to celebrity-worn styles included in the sale.

Dôen fans will have to wait for a Black Friday sale on the celebrity-worn label’s site, but we can confirm the brand will be having one. Until then, you can shop markdowns at Net-a-Porter and Saks Fifth Avenue.

J.Crew has hundreds of markdowns on its site, but we wouldn’t be surprised if the brand offered a sale-on-sale promotion closer to Black Friday. Browse now to get ideas, but wait until after the holiday to finalize your cart.

Lululemon already kicked off its Black Friday event—but the brand rarely hosts sales, so deals will sell out fast (especially on neutral colors like black). Expect new markdowns to be added during the holiday weekend.

#6824. Where Does Sunrise on the Reaping Rank Among the 6 Hunger Games Movies?

Warning: Spoilers for all six Hunger Games movies.

With the release of Sunrise on the Reaping, Suzanne Collins continues to expand on The Hunger Games movie franchise. The latest installment tells the story of Haymitch Abernathy’s Hunger Games—which doubles as the Second Quarter Quell, where twice the number of tributes are reaped—and details his journey from a humble member of District 12 to one of the most important forces in the Second Rebellion by the time Katniss Everdeen’s story is told. But how does Sunrise on the Reaping stack up against the other five films? Read on for ELLE’s ranking below.

Catching Fire ends with a major cliffhanger. When Katniss shoots the arrow that breaks the 75th Hunger Games arena, it kick-starts the true beginning of the revolution. It also provides one of the most heartbreaking plots in the series—when Peeta gets left behind during Katniss’s rescue from the arena, she’s forced to continue her rebel duties without having Peeta by her side.

The Ballad of Songbirds & Snakes tells the story of Lucy Gray Baird, the first District 12 winner of the Hunger Games, whose Games have been erased from history. The film also provides much-needed insight into President Snow’s background, setting the stage for his years as a ruthless authoritarian. To restore his family’s reputation, Snow (Tom Blyth) mentors Lucy Gray (Rachel Zegler) in the 10th Hunger Games, but ends up forming an attachment to her that turns romantic. After Lucy wins the Hunger Games, she runs away with Snow so they can have a future without Capitol rule—but when she catches him in a lie that hints at his dangerous nature, she flees. The betrayal haunts Snow for the rest of his life and inspires him to make the Hunger Games even more vicious.

Before Katniss, there was Haymitch Abernathy’s Hunger Games. The second of the prequel movies, Sunrise on the Reaping shows Haymitch (Joseph Zada) before he became the jaded, embittered drunk that we meet in the first Hunger Games film. Haymitch isn’t entirely disillusioned by the government’s treatment of the district, but being forced to enter the games under such intense circumstances radicalizes him so much he’s inspired to take action. His hopes to spearhead the rebellion don’t pan out the way he had hoped, but that failure paves the way for a future where another rebel could be successful.

The dramatic conclusion to the entire franchise. In Mockingjay: Part 2, the conflict between President Snow (Donald Sutherland), the Capitol, and the outlying districts comes to a head, with the rebels working towards ending his authoritarian reign. Katniss, backed by her own personal army, successfully infiltrates the Capitol and finds it turned into a massive Hunger Games arena before she can successfully capture and kill Snow. The final film isn’t afraid to deliver surprising punches, with Prim dying while helping children in the Capitol and Katniss killing Alma Coin when Snow was meant to be her original target.

The movie that starts it all. Viewers are introduced to the world of Panem and its poorest district, District 12, on the day of the reaping ceremony, in which one girl and one boy are selected to participate (and likely die) in the Hunger Games. Katniss’s sister, Prim, worries about being selected to fight in the Games in her first year of eligibility—when Prim is selected, Katniss bravely volunteers in her sister’s place and rekindles the fire of the rebellion by nearly eating poisonous berries with Peeta while under the impression only one of them could win.

Devoted fans and critics agree—Catching Fire is easily the best film of the Hunger Games franchise. After Katniss survives the 74th Hunger Games and returns home alongside Peeta, the stakes are higher and the challenges are more complex than they were during her first trip to the arena. After being reaped for the Third Quarter Quell, Katniss finds herself fighting among skilled victors while unknowingly being at the center of a plot to overthrow the government. What follows is the true beginning of the rebellion and the battle against the Capitol and President Snow.

#6822. Ransomware Attacks Move Beyond Enterprise Systems to Theaten Employees' Families

Attackers are exploiting enterprises' own artificial intelligence systems and extending pressure beyond internal networks to employees and their families, according to a new Semperis survey of organizations that suffered disruptive ransomware attacks.

In a recent announcement, the company highlighted findings from its 2026 Ransomware Risk Report, which examines AI-related attack tactics, personal intimidation, and recovery preparedness. Censuswide surveyed 1,200 information technology and security professionals across nine countries and eight industry sectors in the first half of 2026.

The sample has an important limit: This year's study included only organizations where at least one ransomware attack caused business disruption. The results describe those victims' experiences, rather than the likelihood that an organization will suffer an attack. The report says that sampling approach changed from previous studies.

Semperis sells identity security, recovery and crisis management products.

The report says 84% of respondents experienced attacks that exploited AI in some way. Its breakdown distinguishes AI-based attacks, reported by 40%, from attacks that manipulated victims' own AI agents or systems, reported by 44%. That distinction matters: The broader figure covers both attackers using AI and attackers exploiting AI deployed inside an organization.

Defensive use of AI also produced mixed responses. Nearly half of respondents, 49%, said they successfully used AI for detection or response, while 23% said their AI tools failed to detect or respond successfully. Those are survey responses about victimized organizations, rather than a controlled comparison of security products.

Chris Inglis, former U.S. national cyber director and a Semperis strategic adviser, described the difficulty of recognizing AI's involvement. "Many attacks are not perceived as being aided and abetted by AI because they're just too slick, too sophisticated," he said in the report.

Data erasure, destruction, or release remained the most frequently reported threat, at 56%. Blackmail, extortion or public shaming followed at 41%, with system lockouts or operational sabotage at 40%. The report also recorded psychological intimidation at 23% and physical threats at 17%.

Sign-in problems account for up to 53% of help desk tickets at individual enterprises, but network issues can take significantly longer to resolve, according to a recent study from ControlUp.

OpenAI has introduced GPT-6 Astra, its most capable broadly deployed model and the first system the company says has reached the "Critical" cybersecurity capability threshold under its Preparedness Framework.

Organizations are running into problems as they give artificial intelligence more control over critical business applications, according to a new survey from identity security firm Delinea.

AI is moving beyond assisting cybercriminals and beginning to operate within live attacks, according to a recent report from Check Point Research. The technology is making sophisticated capabilities faster and more accessible while creating new security risks for businesses deploying their own AI systems.

#6818. IDC Report Finds Significant Gap Between AI Spending and Measurable Financial Returns

Nearly 70% of organizations report a significant gap between artificial intelligence spending and measurable financial returns, according to a recent survey by IDC.

The research firm's 2026 Worldwide AI Economics and Cost of Ownership Survey found that 69% reported such a gap, while only 15% had achieved an AI return on investment exceeding 100%.

In a separate May 2026 survey, 73% of respondents identified excessive AI costs as a significant risk.

IDC highlighted the findings in an October 20 analysis examining what enterprise customers expect from AI technology vendors.

Enterprise customers are placing greater emphasis on measurable business outcomes when evaluating AI products, according to IDC.

The firm identified three priorities for vendors: demonstrating financial value, addressing governance and security requirements, and building support beyond IT departments.

The analysis also highlighted the gap between initial agentic AI deployments and broader adoption. Although 80% of organizations have started deploying agentic AI, only 17% are using it across the enterprise.

IDC said evaluating AI investments can be difficult when the technology changes how employees work and how business processes operate.

The firm expects AI infrastructure capacity to catch up with demand around 2029, potentially easing costs. Until then, vendors face pressure to demonstrate the long-term financial value of their products.

AI funding decisions involve a broad range of executives. IDC's September 2026 C-Suite Tech Survey found that CIOs and CTOs were involved in 63% of those decisions, business-function executives in 62%, and chief financial officers in 61%.

A separate IDC survey found that 42% of CEOs planned to hire a chief AI officer within the next 12 months.

Buyers also reported concerns about the products and terms vendors offer. Limited integration with existing systems, insufficient model and data transparency, and concerns about vendor lock-in were each cited by 34% of respondents.

Overpromising and underdelivering, along with unclear pricing models, were each cited by 31%.

IDC said vendors need to substantiate financial returns and address security and governance requirements while working with decision-makers across IT, finance, and other business departments.

For more information, go to the IDC blog.

#6814. Report: Global Ransomware Attacks Rise 23% in Q3

Global ransomware activity increased 23% in the third quarter of 2026, with organizations in North America and Europe accounting for the largest share of attacks, according to cybersecurity firm NCC Group.

The company's Cyber Threat Intelligence Report Review of Q3 2026 recorded 2,865 ransomware victim claims, up from 2,325 in the previous quarter.

Despite the quarterly increase, attacks declined 26% month over month in September to 821. North America recorded the most attacks that month with 390, followed by Europe with 211.

The industrial sector accounted for 29% of quarterly attacks, while Qilin remained the most active ransomware group for the sixth consecutive quarter, recording 365 victims. The Gentlemen followed with 356, and INC Ransom ranked third with 109.

The report highlighted a July cyber attack against a small UK power generation facility that forced operations to stop for approximately four days.

Although the incident did not affect the national electricity grid, it demonstrated how cyber attacks can disrupt physical infrastructure.

The security researchers said the incident was consistent with techniques previously associated with Iranian-affiliated campaigns, including attacks against internet-exposed industrial controllers.

However, the UK government has not formally attributed the attack, and the techniques used remain unconfirmed.

The report also identified growing security challenges as organizations connect traditional IT networks with operational technology (OT) systems for remote maintenance and centralized management.

The firm recommended network segmentation, stronger privileged-access controls, and continuous monitoring of OT environments.

The research found that more than 40 vulnerabilities affecting enterprise applications, VPN appliances, e-mail gateways, and management platforms were added to CISA's Known Exploited Vulnerabilities Catalog during the quarter.

The report examined Gunra, a ransomware-as-a-service operation that has targeted 53 victims across multiple industries as of Q3.

According to the cybersecurity company, Gunra exploited vulnerabilities CVE-2024-55591 and CVE-2025-24472 in internet-facing Fortinet devices to gain access to corporate networks.

The group also used stolen credentials, session hijacking, and administrative tools to move between systems before deploying ransomware.

Other emerging ransomware groups included Deadlock and Storm, with the latter claiming 78 victims by the end of September.

Researchers also observed initial access brokers advertising compromised VPN, Remote Desktop Protocol, and SSH access, alongside access to cloud identities, source code repositories, and security management platforms.

The quarterly review also highlighted BraZetsu, an AI-enhanced Windows malware framework that evolved into a specialized initial access toolkit.

According to the report, BraZetsu uses AI models to automate victim profiling, analyze compromised environments, and identify valuable corporate systems.

The framework searches for enterprise software, financial data, digital certificates, and cloud credentials, helping operators identify compromised access that can be sold to other cyber criminals.

The cybersecurity firm also identified risks involving legitimate AI agents, including prompt injection, excessive permissions, and unintended access to external systems.

The report cited incidents involving U.S. and Australian government systems as examples of agents accessing resources beyond their intended scope.

Researchers warned that these incidents do not necessarily involve malicious intent but can create consequences resembling conventional cyber intrusions.

For the full report, visit the NCC Group site.

California Governor Gavin Newsom is proposing a new model of artificial intelligence regulation that would give independent organizations a formal role in evaluating the safety claims made by AI giants.

Cisco has introduced Privileged Infrastructure Access, an approach to infrastructure administration that gives people and AI agents temporary, task-specific permissions instead of leaving standing privileges available after their work is done.

Avantis Education, the creator of ClassVR, has introduced two new immersive learning libraries for its Eduverse+ platform: EscapeRooms, a math suite for K-8 students, and LingoLab, a Spanish and English language learning suite for students in grades 6-12.

As schools become more dependent on digital platforms for instruction, grading, student services, and family communication, resilience depends not only on preventing attacks, but on how quickly leaders can restore trusted data, recover essential services, and keep disruptions from cascading across the school community.

The film is moving forward, at least on the page. During a Nov. 19, 2026 appearance on Watch What Happens Live with Andy Cohen, Lawrence offered a rare update on the project when a fan asked about its progress. “I don’t know, I always get in trouble for saying stuff,” she joked, before revealing, “We have a first draft!”

During a November 2025 appearance on Las Culturistas, Lawrence revealed that she and Stone are developing a Miss Piggy movie for Disney, with Oh, Mary! playwright and Tony Award winner Escola attached as the screenwriter. It marks the first collaboration between the two Oscar-winning actresses and the first time Miss Piggy will take center stage in her own feature.

“I don’t know if I can announce this, but I’m just going to,” Lawrence said on the podcast. “Emma Stone and I are producing a Miss Piggy movie and Cole is writing it.” When asked if she and Stone plan to appear on-screen, she added, “I think so. We have to.”

Plot details haven’t been shared yet, but the project is in early development at Disney, which owns the Muppets franchise.

While the storyline is still under wraps, the pairing of Escola’s wit with Miss Piggy’s flair for dramatics suggests a distinctly self-aware take on the beloved character could be coming.

Lawrence and Stone will produce the film, marking the first time the two have worked together professionally.

#6809. Microsoft Expands Protections and Controls for Sovereign Cloud

Microsoft is introducing new tools within its sovereign cloud offerings designed to give organizations tighter control over where their data resides, who can access it, and how critical systems operate, especially in light of AI becoming a bigger part of enterprise infrastructure.

In a recent announcement, Douglas Phillips, president and chief technology officer of Microsoft Specialized Clouds, outlined updates spanning public cloud, private cloud, and partner environments. The changes target governments, regulated industries, and organizations that need tighter control over sensitive workloads without abandoning cloud-based AI capabilities.

"Digital sovereignty should give organizations greater control and security over the technology they depend on, while continuing to innovate," Phillips wrote.

One of the biggest additions is Microsoft's Sovereign Control Panel, which will enter public preview at Microsoft Ignite. Available through the Azure portal, the tool will give IT administrators one place to manage data sovereignty settings, spot compliance issues, and identify potential risks that could disrupt operations.

Microsoft is also tightening controls over who can access sensitive systems. Its Data Guardian feature will require oversight by personnel based in Europe when Microsoft engineers access certain production systems in the European Union and European Free Trade Association. Another tool, Production Touch Ledger, will keep tamper-resistant records of that access, giving organizations a clearer picture of who accessed their systems and what they did.

Microsoft is also giving customers more control over encryption. External key management with Azure Managed HSM is now generally available, letting organizations keep their main encryption keys on hardware they own or manage outside Microsoft's cloud.

The company is also expanding its Sovereign Private Cloud offerings, giving organizations more options to run systems with or without an internet connection. New capabilities include GitHub Enterprise Local, GitHub Copilot with locally hosted AI models, and additional data services running on Azure Local.

The push for greater operational control comes as Microsoft is also reconsidering how enterprises should secure increasingly autonomous AI systems.

As reported by Redmond magazine, Microsoft is expanding Project Perception to address risks posed by what the company calls "agentic insiders," AI agents capable of accessing sensitive information and performing actions without constant human supervision.

That initiative introduces plans for Guardian Agents, specialized security agents designed to monitor other AI systems, evaluate their behavior and intervene when their actions become risky.

"Monitoring and securing those agents must become continuous," said Hayete Gallot, executive vice president of Microsoft Security.

Project Perception is about limiting what AI agents can do. Microsoft's sovereign cloud expansion tackles a different set of questions: where those systems run, who gets to administer them, and how the data stays protected.

Taken together, the two efforts point to a shift in Microsoft's enterprise AI strategy. The company is putting more weight on oversight, not just automation.

Phillips said AI is making these questions more pressing as organizations roll out intelligent systems across sensitive business processes and critical infrastructure.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

#6808. The Launch: November’s Must-Know Fashion News

Fall is in full swing, and with it, so is the fashion industry. As the leaves continue to turn and preparations for the holiday season get serious, brands are finding new ways to celebrate with exciting new capsules, collaborations, and stocking-worthy releases. Plus, there’s plenty of reason to get out and shop, with the likes of L/Uniform, Danielle Frankel, and Bulgari all opening new storefronts. Below, all the fashion news to know this month.

Where: Online at gap.com and in select Gap stores

Why: Zac Posen is keeping things simple this winter season—and the latest GapStudio wardrobe is edited to just 12 pieces. The designer’s capsule gives familiar staples a sharper feel through sleek silhouettes, precise tailoring, and touches of unexpected texture like a playful teddy bear coat rendered in a cozy caramel fabric. Other highlights include elevated essentials that Posen has prioritized at the brand: a turtleneck maxi dress, a sleek poplin shirt, and high-rise seamed stirrup jeans. “For winter 2026, we wanted to move beyond traditional occasion wear with versatile pieces designed to be worn from day to night,” Posen said of the collection, which came to life on singer EJAE in the accompanying campaign, in a statement. “There’s still that level of drama with thoughtful tailoring and exaggerated lines, but it’s balanced by a focused palette that feels elegant and wearable.”

Why: Parisian leather goods label L/Uniform is making its New York debut with a flagship on the Upper East Side. Following boutiques in Paris and Tokyo, the store marks the brand’s first American address with founder Jeanne Signoles working with architect Jakob Sprenger to create a space that balances the polish of a boutique with the practical spirit at the heart of the label. “Opening a store in New York was a priority for me from the very beginning,” Signoles says. “I always felt that L/Uniform naturally belonged here, with the city’s energy, its independence, and its very strong connection to everyday life. Our bags are made to be used—to work, to travel, to accompany daily life.”

Why: A historic Tribeca landmark is the setting for Danielle Frankel’s new flagship. At 6 Harrison Street, the bridal house has created a 12,000-square-foot space where original architectural character meets a considered, contemporary interior by Augusta Hoffman Studio. #6804. 6 Stylish Ways to Wear a Blanket Coat This Fall and Winter

Basically a blanket you can wear outside—and make it fashion.

Beating the cold has gotten that much easier with the rise of blanket coats. This winter, we’re staying bundled up, not only with stylish layering tricks like wraps, shawls, and capes, but by blending the two.

Blanket coats have become the cold-weather item fashion people are loving, and it was all over a lot of the fall/winter 2026 runways, too. So, it’s clear that the style is making the rounds, and there’s no need to wait for next winter to finally be tuned in. Blanket coats provide the coziness of at-home comfort, snuggled beneath a good quilt, to your everyday outfits.

And most serve a two-in-one purpose, also providing some neck protection, so you can opt out of scarves sometimes, or double up if you’re dealing with some really cold days. Below, I’ve gathered some stylish ways to lean into the popular blanket coat trend this fall and winter.

If you’re looking for something that feels a bit elevated, try a blanket coat similar to this style with draping that bunches and gathers flawlessly, giving an almost regal vibe.

If you’re not big on layering, let the coat do the layering for you. Leaning into a blanket jacket like this one that’s split open at the front makes it look like you’re wearing more pieces than you actually are.

The blanket coat serves as a literal wrap-around sweater that keeps you cozy and stylish. This black with brown trim one is perfect for your every day.

While some blanket coats wrap, others fall like trenches, but carry a bit thicker material like the one above. Pairing a simple neutral-colored piece with a monotone outfit is the easy way in.

The best part of a good blanket style coat is that it keeps you covered without having to deal with a zipper. If you want a more relaxed-fit coat then a style like this is great.

For those days when you wish you could carry around a big blanket with you, try instead pulling out your thickest coat that gives all the vibes.
