#6803. Cisco Intros Privileged Infrastructure Access for People and AI Agents

Cisco has introduced Privileged Infrastructure Access, an approach to infrastructure administration that gives people and AI agents temporary, task-specific permissions instead of leaving standing privileges available after their work is done.

In a recent blog post, Cisco's Matt Caulfield described a system that uses cryptographic identity to connect an administrator or agent to the infrastructure needed for a particular task. Access is governed by short-lived privileges, with session recording providing a record of the work performed.

"If there is no work being done, no privilege exists," Caulfield wrote. Cisco positions the offering as a way to bring identity controls directly into infrastructure operations while reducing dependence on static credentials, shared secrets, and broad access.

The announcement follows Cisco's previously announced Teleport partnership and its Infrastructure Identity concept, which connects the identity of a person or machine with the resources it can access, the actions it performs, and the permissions governing those actions.

Caulfield described a gap between the controls organizations apply to workforce applications and those used to administer network infrastructure. Companies have invested in single sign-on, multifactor authentication, and risk-based controls for application access, he wrote, but those protections often do not follow an engineer logging into a router or switch.

Instead, infrastructure access may depend on long-lived credentials, shared accounts, or separate policy systems. According to Cisco, that separation makes it harder to enforce least privilege and establish which person or system performed a particular action.

The company illustrates the problem with an administrator updating hundreds of switches during a maintenance window. If some updates fail, the administrator needs to troubleshoot before the window closes. The permissions available to handle that work, however, may cover more infrastructure and remain active longer than the task requires.

Traditional privileged access management can reduce risk through credential vaulting and rotation, Caulfield wrote. But he argued that operational friction can undermine those controls when teams create exceptions to complete urgent work. Those exceptions can leave credentials and privileges usable after the immediate need has passed.

Cisco's stated objective is to give infrastructure teams a consistent route to the devices they manage while providing security teams with policy enforcement, temporary permissions, and a record of activity. The company describes operational convenience as part of the design, rather than treating access governance solely as a credential-management problem.

Privileged Infrastructure Access makes access an on-demand action, according to the announcement. Cryptographic identity establishes the actor, and the resulting connection provides only the privileges needed to complete the task. Those privileges expire when the task concludes.

For customers using Cisco Identity Services Engine (ISE) and Terminal Access Controller Access-Control System Plus (TACACS+), the offering can draw on existing policy and device context to govern access to Cisco routers and switches. Cisco says customers can extend the same access model to other infrastructure, including servers, Kubernetes clusters, and cloud resources.

Sign-in problems account for up to 53% of help desk tickets at individual enterprises, but network issues can take significantly longer to resolve, according to a recent study from ControlUp.

OpenAI has introduced GPT-6 Astra, its most capable broadly deployed model and the first system the company says has reached the "Critical" cybersecurity capability threshold under its Preparedness Framework.

Organizations are running into problems as they give artificial intelligence more control over critical business applications, according to a new survey from identity security firm Delinea.

AI is moving beyond assisting cybercriminals and beginning to operate within live attacks, according to a recent report from Check Point Research. The technology is making sophisticated capabilities faster and more accessible while creating new security risks for businesses deploying their own AI systems.

#6798. Survey Finds 84% of Organizations Reported at Least One AI-Related Incident in the Past Year

Companies are running into problems as they give artificial intelligence more control over critical business applications, with 84% reporting at least one AI-related incident in the past year, according to a new survey from identity security firm Delinea.

The company's 2026 Application Access Governance report found that some organizations have experienced incorrect financial transactions and AI actions they couldn't trace or explain. The findings raise concerns about whether enterprises have enough oversight of AI systems that can make decisions and take actions on their own

The findings are based on an independent survey of 1,000 senior finance and finance technology leaders in the United States and United Kingdom. The AI-specific analysis included 879 respondents whose organizations use the technology in critical business applications.

The report found that 35% of surveyed organizations have deployed AI agents capable of taking actions within business applications. However, only 11% of organizations using AI said their governance and security controls were keeping pace with deployment.

Delinea also found gaps in centralized visibility into agent deployment and permissions, including who owns agents and who approved their use.

Some respondents reported segregation-of-duties conflicts involving AI, while others identified agents that continued operating after the employees who deployed them had left the organization.

The research also highlighted gaps in monitoring access across business systems. Only 46% of respondents said they continuously monitor and manage cumulative access risks across applications.

Those gaps can make it difficult to identify segregation-of-duties conflicts, in which a person or agent has permissions across systems that should be kept separate.

Despite those issues, 95% of respondents said they were confident their access controls would withstand scrutiny in an external audit.

However, 26% reported access-related audit findings that had recurred in consecutive years, while 25% said some findings had remained unresolved for three or more years.

Only 22% of organizations had a single named owner for each application, with ownership tracked in one system of record.

Meanwhile, 60% reported an increase over the past two years in the number of critical applications operating outside the scope of access governance or Sarbanes-Oxley Act compliance.

The findings highlight the oversight challenges associated with giving AI agents authority to act within business systems, particularly when organizations cannot consistently identify who owns an agent or trace its actions.

Frank Vukovits, chief security scientist at Delinea, said organizations remain responsible for the actions AI agents take within their systems.

"When an agent can approve a transaction or change permissions, the business owns the outcome. You need to know what every agent can do, who owns it, and who approved it, before an auditor asks or your financials are misstated," Vukovits said.

For the full report, go to the Delinea site.

Sign-in problems account for up to 53% of help desk tickets at individual enterprises, but network issues can take significantly longer to resolve, according to a recent study from ControlUp.

#6796. Your First Car Comes with Questions

You’ve manifested several milestones—job, apartment, a squad of ride-or-dies. Now, you need a way to get from one to the other. This is where State Farm comes in as your trusted partner to help you make informed decisions. Here’s what you’ll need to know before you enter your car owner era.

As images of weekend getaways and road trips with your besties fill your mind, so, too, should these pre-purchase considerations.

At the dealership, put on your negotiation face. Buying a car is a little like dating—you do need to be observant, but you shouldn’t put all your cards (in this case: your budget) on the table. Finally, don’t be afraid to walk away. Follow these tips to make sure you're setting yourself up for success.

Before you turn up your favorite song and hit the open road, make sure your new baby is protected. It can be a lot to take in, so let’s demystify insurance. Here’s what you need to know.

Rate: This is the baseline estimated price that would cover the potential cost of future claims. It’s determined using shared group factors such as age group; if you’re in your twenties, it would take into account the statistical driving information for other 25-year-olds in a region.

Premium: This is the adjusted individual insurance price that you’ll pay over a six- or 12-month period. It’s created by starting with the rate, then adding more specific factors that affect your claim risk level. These include your driving record, claims history, mileage usage, and vehicle type.

Overwhelmed? Don’t sweat it—State Farm can get you an insurance quote before you buy.

Ah, the eternal question. Are you commitment-minded or more of a free agent?

To answer that when it comes to car ownership, consider how much you drive annually (to avoid leasing mileage overages if you exceed the stated mileage), how long you plan to keep your vehicle, and your financial situation.

Then, know that there are insurance differences between leasing and buying, too. Leasing companies adhere to stricter requirements than those that most states set, which could translate into higher monthly premiums.

If you buy to avoid the mileage caps and wear-and-tear of leasing, but still want the benefits of a fresh ride every few years, there is a moment during your payment plan when you can get a new car. Your initial loan payments largely go to interest (what lenders charge for giving you money), but there’s a break-even point when your payments begin going to principal (the actual amount you’ve borrowed). When that happens, and the outstanding balance is equal to the value of the car, you can trade it in for that amount, which is a budget-friendly way to upgrade.

Whatever you choose, a little strategy will keep you in the driver’s seat from dreaming about your new car to signing on the dotted line.

State Farm is there to protect you and your car.

#6794. A Complete Timeline of the Cyrus Family Drama, From Tish and Billy Ray’s Divorce to Miley’s Wedding

Here's where Miley stands with her parents and siblings.

Miley Cyrus’s family has had so much drama going on over the last four years, I can only assume producers over at E! are currently trying to sign them up for a reality show. And tbh, we’d tune in. But if you haven’t been Keeping Up With the Cyruses, gather round because it’s equal parts complicated and messy.

#6793. Report: Cyber Criminals Move Toward More Targeted Phishing Attacks

Cyber criminals are moving away from mass phishing scams and focusing on more targeted attacks, increasingly using trusted websites and services to trick victims, according to a new report from KnowBe4. The security firm found that spear-phishing attacks increased 41% over the past year.

The company's Phishing Threat Trends Report, Vol. 8 found that overall phishing volume increased 29.8% year over year. Organizations received an average of one dangerous e-mail for every 733 messages, but the bigger concern is how attackers are changing their approach.

Rather than sending the same fraudulent message to thousands of people, criminals are spending more time researching their targets and creating convincing messages that appear to come from trusted coworkers, businesses, and technology platforms.

The findings are based on threat data from KnowBe4 Defend, the company's inbound e-mail security platform, which processes approximately 1.15 billion event signals daily.

KnowBe4 found that several traditional phishing methods declined significantly compared with 2025. Mobile-focused scams dropped 47.3%, advance-fee fraud fell 31.3%, and missed-message scams declined 11.3%.

Meanwhile, spear phishing increased 41.1%, pre-attack reconnaissance rose 33.7%, and company impersonation climbed 28%.

The shift suggests that attackers are finding more value in carefully crafted scams that exploit existing business relationships and workplace routines.

Business e-mail compromise (BEC) remains particularly common, accounting for 51.2% of phishing e-mails analyzed in the report. Human resources, finance departments, and executive leadership are among the most frequently targeted groups.

Attackers also appear to be timing their campaigns around normal business activity. KnowBe4 found that 90.4% of phishing messages arrive Monday through Friday, with Monday accounting for the largest share at 20.6%.

One of the report's more troubling findings involves the growing use of legitimate technology platforms to deliver malicious content.

KnowBe4 found that phishing attacks were twice as likely to originate from legitimate infrastructure in the third quarter of 2026 compared with the previous quarter.

That includes AI services, where attackers are taking advantage of trusted domains to host fake developer consoles, login prompts, and credential-stealing pages. The report also identified a 228% increase in AI-brand impersonation attacks between March and August.

Because these malicious pages can operate on legitimate domains with valid security certificates, users may not see the usual warning signs associated with suspicious websites.

"For years, security advice has focused on a predictable checklist: Check the sender address, hover over the link, and look for the padlock," said Jack Chapman, senior vice president of threat intelligence at KnowBe4.

"While still foundational, that guidance is becoming less useful by the day."

The findings echo a recent report about management software and AI e-mail assistants. Research from Fortra and Barracuda showed how criminals are using legitimate administrative tools and hidden AI instructions to manipulate users and compromise systems.

California Governor Gavin Newsom is proposing a new model of artificial intelligence regulation that would give independent organizations a formal role in evaluating the safety claims made by AI giants.

Cisco has introduced Privileged Infrastructure Access, an approach to infrastructure administration that gives people and AI agents temporary, task-specific permissions instead of leaving standing privileges available after their work is done.

Avantis Education, the creator of ClassVR, has introduced two new immersive learning libraries for its Eduverse+ platform: EscapeRooms, a math suite for K-8 students, and LingoLab, a Spanish and English language learning suite for students in grades 6-12.

As schools become more dependent on digital platforms for instruction, grading, student services, and family communication, resilience depends not only on preventing attacks, but on how quickly leaders can restore trusted data, recover essential services, and keep disruptions from cascading across the school community.

#6790. Study: Sign-In Problems Are Primary Drivers of Help Desk Tickets

Sign-in problems account for up to 53% of help desk tickets at individual enterprises, but network issues can take significantly longer to resolve, according to a new study from ControlUp.

Analyzing 1,069,110 help desk tickets, the IT operations company identified password resets, account lockouts, and multifactor authentication (MFA) issues as major drivers of support demand.

At one large enterprise, account-related tickets took a median of 11 minutes to resolve, compared with 50 hours for network problems. Meanwhile, 87% of network and phone tickets were reassigned between teams, compared with 20% of account tickets.

MFA enrollment and re-enrollment problems appeared at 40% of organizations, with one in five linking these issues to employees replacing or upgrading their phones. Virtual desktop problems appeared at 55% of organizations analyzed.

Traditional phone support remained a major channel for IT assistance.

Among organizations that recorded how support requests were submitted, 57% reported that phone calls accounted for approximately two-thirds or more of contacts. At one organization, that figure reached 99%.

Machine-generated tickets from monitoring, security, backup, and patching systems appeared at half of the organizations studied. At 15%, automated tickets represented between 25% and 50% of all requests.

The research covered enterprises across retail, healthcare, financial services, manufacturing, construction, energy, and IT services.

ControlUp said the findings highlight the need to evaluate recurring problems and their operational impact rather than relying solely on ticket volume and resolution speed.

"IT organizations have traditionally measured support performance by how quickly they close tickets, but that doesn't tell the whole story," said Simon Townsend, senior vice president of marketing and office of the CTO at ControlUp.

"A password reset that takes a few minutes and an infrastructure failure that disrupts an entire location shouldn't carry the same operational weight."

For more information, go to the ControlUp site.

Sign-in problems account for up to 53% of help desk tickets at individual enterprises, but network issues can take significantly longer to resolve, according to a recent study from ControlUp.

Organizations are running into problems as they give artificial intelligence more control over critical business applications, according to a new survey from identity security firm Delinea.

AI is moving beyond assisting cybercriminals and beginning to operate within live attacks, according to a recent report from Check Point Research. The technology is making sophisticated capabilities faster and more accessible while creating new security risks for businesses deploying their own AI systems.

#6784. Enterprise AI Spending Outpaces Adoption Growth, Report Finds

Enterprise AI spending is growing faster than the number of organizations paying for it, according to a new Menlo Ventures report that points to a shift from buying access for employees to financing work performed by agents.

The venture capital firm's 2026 State of Enterprise AI report estimates deployment spending reached a $176.8 billion annualized run rate by September, up from $33 billion in 2025. The share of enterprises paying for AI rose from 55% to 65%.

A larger bill can reflect broader use inside an organization, but it does not independently establish that the additional work is useful or profitable.

Menlo's methodology describes revenue figures as annualized run rates. The $176.8 billion estimate therefore should not be read as $176.8 billion already spent during the first nine months of 2026.

The report also distinguishes unusually heavy spenders from the typical respondent. Average reported spending reached 4.7 times the prior year's level, while the median reached 2.2 times. That gap suggests a smaller group of large spenders drove the average substantially.

The practical implication is that an industry growth figure may be a poor benchmark for an individual organization's budget. Buyers need to understand their own workload growth and the results it produces.

"Agents are changing the economics of software," Menlo principal Derek Xiao said in the firm's announcement.

Menlo reports that agents account for 80% of tokens on OpenRouter, a service providing access to multiple models. That measures activity on one platform, rather than all enterprise AI use. Token consumption also measures processing volume, rather than completed business outcomes.

An agent that makes repeated attempts can consume more resources without delivering proportionately more value. For procurement teams, the relevant comparison is consequently the cost of completing acceptable work, including the review it requires.

Menlo estimates that Anthropic, OpenAI, and Google capture 69% of enterprise AI deployment spending. That measure is distinct from their shares of enterprise language-model API spending, a narrower category. The figures should not be used interchangeably.

The report draws on a Morning Consult survey of nearly 400 U.S. enterprise decision-makers responsible for more than $4 billion in AI spending. Market totals are modeled estimates informed by surveys, public filings, and third-party reports. Menlo discloses investments in Anthropic and OpenRouter.

A separate announcement from Information Services Group describes buyers putting more emphasis on the economics and reliability of production deployments. ISG says ongoing security and observability work remains necessary as organizations scale.

That provides a useful distinction between market demand and business value. Suppliers can generate growing revenue while customers still determine which deployments justify the cost.

For enterprise leaders, the new spending estimate strengthens the case for examining usage beyond license counts. It leaves the harder purchasing question with the customer: How much worthwhile work did the increased spending actually buy?

Menlo's State of Enterprise AI report is available here.

John K. Waters is the editor in chief of a number of Converge360.com sites, with a focus on high-end development, AI and future tech. He's been writing about cutting-edge technologies and culture of Silicon Valley for more than two decades, and he's written more than a dozen books. He also co-scripted the documentary film Silicon Valley: A 100 Year Renaissance, which aired on PBS.  He can be reached at [email protected].

California Governor Gavin Newsom is proposing a new model of artificial intelligence regulation that would give independent organizations a formal role in evaluating the safety claims made by AI giants.

Cisco has introduced Privileged Infrastructure Access, an approach to infrastructure administration that gives people and AI agents temporary, task-specific permissions instead of leaving standing privileges available after their work is done.

Avantis Education, the creator of ClassVR, has introduced two new immersive learning libraries for its Eduverse+ platform: EscapeRooms, a math suite for K-8 students, and LingoLab, a Spanish and English language learning suite for students in grades 6-12.

As schools become more dependent on digital platforms for instruction, grading, student services, and family communication, resilience depends not only on preventing attacks, but on how quickly leaders can restore trusted data, recover essential services, and keep disruptions from cascading across the school community.

#6782. Phishing Threats Totaled 7.3 Billion in Q3 2026

E-mail phishing threats reached 7.3 billion in Q3 2026, with September activity increasing 41% from August, according to Microsoft Threat Intelligence.

According to a report from Microsoft Threat Intelligence and the Microsoft Defender Security Research Team, phishing detections reached nearly 3 billion in September alone.

The findings also highlighted changes in attacker techniques, including business-service impersonation, credential theft, and social engineering through Microsoft Teams.

The Redmond-based firm recognized approximately 129 million malicious messages spoofing the sender domains of tracked business services during Q3.

DocuSign was the most frequently spoofed brand in September, accounting for 29% of the month's tracked spoofing activity. Adobe, SurveyMonkey, and Barracuda also experienced increases toward the end of the quarter.

The research found that some malicious messages were delivered despite failing e-mail authentication checks and being identified as threats.

In September, tenant- or user-level security overrides applied to 63% of these messages with a recorded security outcome — most involved tenant-level exceptions.

Microsoft warned that overly broad exceptions intended to support legitimate business communications can weaken protection against impersonation attacks.

The company emphasized that these findings do not establish whether recipients opened the messages or whether accounts were compromised.

Microsoft also recorded approximately 141,000 malicious Teams call attempts during Q3.

Attackers increasingly used ordinary personal names instead of explicitly impersonating IT support personnel. Such display names accounted for 46% of an observed sample in July, rising to 88% in September.

Meanwhile, QR code phishing declined 67% compared to Q2, totaling roughly 12.1 million attacks.

CAPTCHA-gated phishing activity increased toward the end of the quarter, reaching 3.1 million detected attacks in September. SVG attachments played a significant role in the increase.

Credential theft remained the primary objective of malicious attachment campaigns, with approximately 94% to 96% of payload-based attacks directing users to phishing sites or fraudulent sign-in pages.

Microsoft recommended reviewing Exchange Online Protection and Defender for Office 365 configurations, enabling Safe Links and Safe Attachments, and strengthening authentication protections.

The company also advised organizations to review security exceptions and verify the identities of external Teams contacts before responding to suspicious requests.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog post.

Sign-in problems account for up to 53% of help desk tickets at individual enterprises, but network issues can take significantly longer to resolve, according to a recent study from ControlUp.

OpenAI has introduced GPT-6 Astra, its most capable broadly deployed model and the first system the company says has reached the "Critical" cybersecurity capability threshold under its Preparedness Framework.

Organizations are running into problems as they give artificial intelligence more control over critical business applications, according to a new survey from identity security firm Delinea.

AI is moving beyond assisting cybercriminals and beginning to operate within live attacks, according to a recent report from Check Point Research. The technology is making sophisticated capabilities faster and more accessible while creating new security risks for businesses deploying their own AI systems.

#6780. Decoding the Over-the-Top Outfits in All’s Fair Season 2

Kim Kardashian, Glenn Close, and more live out a fabulous fashion fantasy in All’s Fair season 2.

No further questions: All’s Fair season 2 is one of the most fashionable series you can stream right now. When the show debuted just over a year ago, it made headlines for being Hulu’s biggest scripted launch in three years. Is it any wonder? It combines drama, style, and female powerhouses with major personality.

The show follows stylish lawyers played by Kim Kardashian, Naomi Watts, Sarah Paulson, Niecy Nash-Betts, Teyana Taylor, and Glenn Close, each with their own distinct aesthetics that range from extremely rare archival ’90s vintage to quiet luxury glam. All’s Fair season 2 debuted a few weeks ago and is well underway, with the finale airing on Wednesday, Dec. 2, 2026. While we wait for the next episodes, we caught up with Paula Bradley, the costume designer behind it all.

When we started out with the new season, we did have a note from Ryan Murphy just to tone it down a little, not a lot, but just a little, in regard to that they are lawyers, not to do so many hats on Niecy—it’s hard for the eyeline. Then it was really just about combing through the season’s collections and seeing who did what, and which silhouettes go where, because at this point we’ve kind of developed our characters. The big difference for season 2 was we really upped our jewelry game.

So, it started with Glenn. She had asked if she could have some real pearls that she just wore all the time. And so we wrote to Tiffany’s, and then we wrote to everybody, we found jewelers that we’d never heard of that did fantastical things. Tiffany loaned us three incredible brooches that were so ridiculously valuable, and some pearls and diamonds and earrings for Glenn. Boucheron did jewelry for everybody for the Scorpio Ball. We had to have new safes installed in the office and in the truck.

Yes. We bought a lot of vintage for Teyana Taylor. We had vintage Alaïa and Gaultier, and we bought an incredible vintage outfit that—I can’t say who wears it in the end—we bought this Galliano runway piece that I had them split into two invoices so that I could put it in the budget. There was a sweater set that was really, really stunning. I don’t dress Kim, Soki Mak dresses Kim. She starts with just going to the auction houses and the vintage places around Europe and Paris, and just collecting everything that she can for Kim.

So, if we start with Sarah Paulson, who plays Carr, she’s the most edgy of all of the characters. She always has a shoulder, sometimes it’s the softer shoulder, but mostly it’s a very sharp shoulder. It was based on the old Saint Laurent. She always has a feminine moment, so the bows have become her—big bows, big earrings, and moments of softness just to soften it up.

Then Naomi Watts (Liberty Ronson) is our quiet luxury. She walks into everything, and she’s monochromatic, although this season we started to pull her a little bit more into the dark side.

Niecy Nash-Betts (Emerald)—it was hard to lose the hats for Niecy, so we went into the belts because her waist is so snatched. And Niecy, for me, represents her own culture incredibly well. That body is so Jessica Rabbit, and it’s so for real. And so she’s always about color, sass, attitude, and glamour. I would say she’s the most glamorous of my characters.

Glenn Close (Dina) is our classic. She’s our classic WASP. So her clothes usually have embroidery or a little bit of extra texture, or she always has a lace edge. She’s a little bit more heirloom, you know? Old Hollywood.

I can. So, I can’t give away the ending because it hasn’t landed yet, and it’s quite a shocker. But obviously the opening, and re-creating the scorpion cape (for Kim) was amazing, because there still are a few ladies who do that beading, who did it in the ’50s and ’60s for Judy Garland and Liza Minnelli, and worked with Bob Mackie. They dip those beads into different solutions of bleach to age them appropriately. They pulled threads to get the edges a little bit frayed. And I worry that that talent and that skill set will soon have disappeared. So that was amazing.

The other thing that I love is the Balenciaga bright yellow highlighter coat that Sarah wears, because she always wears black or gray. And that moment of her just walking through the hallways in that giant oversize cocoon coat, and the glasses and the shoes—we did it just like the runway—was a real joy.

And Naomi, we got her in a couple of Saint Laurent pieces with stronger shoulders, and that was great, too. But the opening—that was supposed to be the opening, but it’s actually the second scene—is also my favorite. So we made Sarah a leather shirt with a giant leather bow, with the shoulders, and Glenn had been at the Erdem show in London, and so we put Glenn in that fabulous denim suit with all the white embroidery. I think it just set the season off.

Sarah has the Fendi that came off the runway this season. And Sarah’s really the only one with a bag game, because she comes in and out of the law firm. So she still carried the Prada from last season into this season, but she also has a really great Balenciaga bag.

I think it’s more the totes—when they have them, it’s the tote bags, because that’s their professional, legal side. They all have a set of work totes that you believe that they could pull documents out of. I think that’s probably the most relatable. I think we all, even though it’s not good for our shoulders, it’s not good for our backs, most women I know, we all have a set of really great tote bags that we put our very heavy computers in and carry all our stuff in. We like a good tote, and I think that’s probably the most relatable part of it.

This interview has been edited and condensed for clarity.

#6779. The Holiday Party Etiquette Rules That Actually Matter, According to Lizzie Post

An expert shares how to celebrate the festive season with polish.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Welcome to In Good Company, where we ring in the festive season with fashion-insider entertaining tips, expert etiquette advice, and the perfect editor-approved hostess gifts.

Holiday entertaining brings with it countless conundrums—from uninvited guests to the cousin who won’t put down their phone—for both hosts and guests. Agility is the key word; being able to elegantly pivot and respond to unexpected situations is what makes people feel comfortable and appreciated. Just in time for festive gatherings, ELLE spoke with Lizzie Post, author, co-president of the Emily Post Institute, and co-host of the Awesome Etiquette podcast, about all the things to keep in mind for a kind and gracious holiday. Read on for Post’s refreshingly down-to-earth tips.

No one can keep up with the incredible party décor shown on influencers’ posts, and you don’t have to try. Elegance can be found in any space, so host within the realities of your home. “You are not meant to be entertaining at the level of someone with a million-dollar budget and a gigantic home. You get to entertain at exactly your level, whatever that is,” Post says. “All the little touches and the things you do to make people feel comfortable and welcome in your home are what make for a great host.”

Bringing the wrong item can be an error on the part of the guest, but hosts should always make sure to minimize discomfort. “You want to take the etiquette high road and let it not be a problem. If [someone brings] an extra guest, quickly enlist someone from your household or a trusted guest to help you zhuzh the arrangements in a discreet way so that the extra person does not feel like they were not meant to be there… [We have] the nearest-and-dearest to the host take smaller portions, so that everyone can have a decent portion.”

Part of smartphone etiquette is letting others make their own choices. “You don’t overly control someone’s phone usage. Their rudeness will show on its own and will be noticed by more than just you… [Saying] ‘Could you make sure your phone is off, so you’re not tempted to use it throughout the evening?’ That is not a polite thing, even though it sounds gentle and so reasonable. That’s micromanagement.”

Bringing a thoughtful gift is always appreciated, but truly engaging with the event is what really matters. “There is no burden for hosting you. You’ve been invited and should feel welcome and like you belong in this space. You are not there to repay the efforts of the host. We are greeting our host. We are engaging with the other people. We are helping ourselves to the refreshments that are offered. If there are games or entertainment, we’re participating in that. If there’s a theme, we’ve showed up in the theme. That’s really how we’re a good guest.”

Always say goodbye. “At the end of the night, say thank you and goodbye and seek out the host, whether it’s a big open house party or it’s a small dinner party to make that connection and express gratitude before we go.”

Serving an item that a guest brought is at the host’s discretion. “A hostess gift is a hostess gift. It is not meant to be used that night with the people who brought it. It can be, but it does not have to be.”

Whether you mail a card or not, make sure to share your appreciation with your host. “Thank-you notes are a wonderful idea and they allow you an extra level of expression, a little more thought. It doesn’t mean that a text message the next day would not also be appreciated. Any version of thanking your host after a party is a great version.”

This interview has been edited and condensed for clarity.

#6775. The Only Apple Deals Worth Your Dollar This Black Friday

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

AirPods, MacBooks, and Watches for up to 28% off—this year’s sale is hot.

If it feels like life is one long string of sales—most of them being mediocre—you’re not wrong. Despite the near-constant deluge of Prime Days, anniversary events, and the like, some sales do legitimately stand above the rest. You know the one: Black Friday. It tends to bring some of the best deals of the year, including record-low prices on Apple tech.

Apple is notorious for not marking down its own products—on the contrary, the tech giant actually raised prices on Macs and iPads earlier this year—so we turn to retailers like Amazon when looking to save on everything from AirPods to Apple Watches. This year, Amazon’s Black Friday deals span 12 days (the sale is called Turkey 12 if you’d like to know too much), which means plenty of Apple discounts are already live. In the event that you’ve spilled coffee on your MacBook or lost one of your AirPods, you needn’t wait until Black Friday proper to shop.

Sure, there’s always the chance these deals could slightly deepen, but we’d wager we’re only looking at marginal price cuts at this point. Consider this: Shop now, shave a couple hundred bucks off of those tech upgrades you’ve been avoiding, then spend your holiday weekend watching football and reheating Thanksgiving leftovers instead of scrolling through Amazon. Your move.

AirPods reliably go on deep discount during Amazon sale events, and Black Friday has proven no different. The third-generation AirPods Pro have dropped to just $179, only $10 shy of their record-low price.

Although the new AirPods 5 aren’t marked down right now, they start at just $129, which is still lower than the discounted Pros. We’re hoping that these will go on sale for the first time on Black Friday. For those who prefer over-ear headphones, the AirPods Max 2 are $120 off (not quite their lowest price, but nothing to scoff at). You can also save on Beats wireless headphones and earbuds, including $150 off the ultra-popular Beats Studio Pro, which have Apple’s chip under the hood.

No Apple product experienced higher price hikes than laptops. The MacBook Air jumped $200 to $1,299 and the MacBook Pro climbed $400 to $1,999, driven by rising RAM and storage costs. To ease the pain, both the 13- and 15-inch MacBook Air models are up to 19% off. We haven’t seen deals this deep on Macs in quite some time.

If you prefer the Pro, those aren’t quite as discounted, but you can still shave up to $250 off of a souped-up model. Plus, Apple’s most affordable laptop, the MacBook Neo, just went on sale for the first time. Sure, it’s only a $50 discount, but it’s still the cheapest model by far—and fits the bill for most basic needs like watching Netflix and renewing your lease.

Although Apple Watches haven’t fallen victim to price increases this year, deals have been few and far between, which means these Black Friday discounts are liable to sell out fast.

The brand-new Apple Watch Series 12—which ushered in Apple’s first stab at readiness scores—is $70 off, dropping this to its lowest price ever. If you’re looking for a budget-friendly Apple Watch, opt for the SE 3, which has dipped to under $200—a verifiable steal given Apple’s current pricing dynamics. While the rugged Apple Watch Ultra 4 isn’t on sale right now, we’ll keep you posted should any surprise sales arise.

Apple Watch Series 12 (GPS+ Cellular, 42mm)

Back in June, Apple bumped the price of its entire iPad range by up to $200, which makes scoring one on sale all the more crucial. As predicted, previous-gen iPad Minis (an editor favorite) are $100 off after the revamped A20 Pro model was released earlier this month. Should you prefer a full-sized tablet, entry-level iPads, iPad Airs, and iPad Pros are all on sale, so it’s mostly a matter of use case in determining which to buy. If you’re going to watch TV on it while traveling, opt for the $399 model, while the Pro is better suited for getting creative work done.

Accessories are reliably among the easiest Apple products to save on during major sales. If you want to stick with Apple-branded gear, the second-generation AirTag, complete with improved Precision Finding, is sitting at a record-low $23, in addition to discounts on Apple Pencils, Magic Keyboards, and the like. You can also save on plenty of third-party power banks, wall adapters, and wireless chargers, which tend to see steeper discounts than Apple’s own gear.

#6773. Has Zegna Cracked the Code to Modern Tailoring?

With its latest jacket, the Falcon, the brand brings some sporty utility to its collection of mix-and-matchable clothing. Artistic director Alessandro Sartori explains how it’s all part of a bigger project to help men dress their best.

Pay attention to the world of menswear long enough, and you’ll notice that certain quandaries come up time and again. A favorite among traditionalists and avant-garde types alike is the fate of the suit. It’s been incorrectly declared dead enough times that the handwringing over its demise has largely shifted to questions of its evolving role in the modern guy’s wardrobe: Who wears a suit, and when, and why?

According to Alessandro Sartori, the artistic director of Zegna for a little more than a decade now, there’s another question we should all be asking ourselves: What exactly is a suit these days?

“Everything we do here is grounded in tailoring techniques and in tailoring fabrics,” Sartori tells me over Zoom from his home in Milan. “But I'm a big believer that a suit doesn't necessarily have to be a combination of the classic top and the classic bottom in the same fabric and color.” It can be comprised of those things, he stresses. But the “modern suit,” as he calls it, can also be an intentional pairing of a different kind of top—a chore coat, an overshirt—with a trouser with a different fabrication. The materials and colors should feel complementary and cohesive but needn’t be perfectly matched.

“This is not only a way to give a new life to suiting,” he explains, “but to dress men differently—and, at the same time, keep the quality, the craft, and the beautiful fit that a tailored piece has.”

The idea is to adapt classic techniques to the realities of getting dressed today. “Many fashion brands offer a nice quality, but is not the handmade quality that we do,” Sartori says. “And there are a lot of brands that are offering beautiful, beautiful quality, but they are quite classic.” Since he started in 2016, Sartori’s overarching project has been to populate the gulf between those two modes of production, making clothes that feel contemporary but are rooted in the top-tier fabrics and handwork that helped Zegna become the powerhouse of Italian fashion that it is today.

The latest addition to Sartori’s lineup is a new jacket, the Falcon. It pays homage to the sort of utilitarian jackets Zegna founder Ermenegildo Zegna might have worn while walking the grounds of what is now the Oasi Zegna nature reserve in northern Italy, at the base of the Biellese Alps. (According to company lore, Ermenegildo imagined those walks as being accompanied by the flight of a falcon.)

Sartori acknowledges that the use case, in 2026, might be a little different. “I don't think that people are necessarily using the jacket walking up in the mountains,” he says. The Falcon is more about embracing a “sporty-chic attitude,” whether you’re stomping around the country or the city, and working within the modular framework that the designer has created at Zegna. You can mix and match it with other pieces—both old and new—from the brand, and you can wear it as part of a “modern suit” or on its own.

The new design is cut from Zegna’s Oasi Cashmere for lightweight warmth, and Sartori calls it “a great addition to our vocabulary.” Buttoned patch pockets give you somewhere to stash your stuff, a bowling collar can be flipped up to shield against stiff winds, and an internal drawstring can be cinched at the midsection for a little extra protection against the cold. Ultimately, it’s a familiar-looking piece. But it’s exceptional in its construction and materials and its design—a streamlined take on the hunting jackets of yore—is as considered as how it’s made.

“Long-lasting life is a key factor for what we're doing, and I don't mean only quality-wise,” Sartori says. “I also mean design-wise.”

The designer, for his part, hopes that the Falcon—alongside other modern classics from the brand—will be the sort of thing that guys wear and reinterpret for years before passing it on to the next generation. And as the brand continues to grow and evolve alongside its customer base, one of his biggest aims is to ensure that each item he introduces remains relevant throughout its (ideally long) lifespan.

“It’s like writing a book, and every season I add a chapter,” he says. “Because the story is going on, is moving with our clients, our community of people, our brand, our family. So many layers are … I don’t want to say ready, but cooking. I see a bright future for the brand, following this principle of walking with our customers and building an indelible aesthetic. I like this approach.”

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

If you’ve been eyeing a new pair, now is the time to buy.

While the Black Fridays of yore involved a sea of people rushing into a big-box store to claw each other’s eyes out over a new TV or gaming console, the biggest shopping holiday of the year looks a bit different these days. Every time Apple gadgets go on deep discount, they tend to sell out quickly. It’s just like those Black Friday mad dashes, just from the comfort of your own couch, so it would behoove you to shop sooner rather than later.

Right now, both the AirPods Pro 3 and the AirPods Max 2 are hovering close to their lowest prices ever. If you’re looking for the cheapest possible pair, the brand-new AirPods 5 are still at full price, but considering that’s just $129, it’s still a solid deal, especially because they’re Apple’s first standard AirPods to include noise cancellation. That said, we wouldn’t be mad if they go on sale for the first time and will keep you posted if they do. Amazon, your move.

AirPods Pro 3 are Apple’s most advanced earbuds yet. We called them “pretty damn cool” back when they first dropped in September 2025, and more than a year later, we stand by it.

Not only do they come armed with the noise cancellation Apple is famous for, but they also boast both adaptive and transparency audio features that adjust to the environment around you. Krista Jones, senior commerce director, is partial to the adaptive mode, which she says is “slightly less noise cancelling and always tunes into my surroundings to turn things up or down accordingly.”

While a fourth generation of AirPods Pro were rumored to drop in fall 2026, there’s been no sign of them in Apple’s most recent slate of releases, so if you’re looking for the most sophisticated AirPods on the market to date, these are it. Right now at $179, they’re just $10 shy of their lowest price ever.

Should you prefer over-ear headphones, the AirPods Max 2 are $120 off as part of Amazon’s Black Friday sale. Back when the first generation of these dropped in 2022, editor-at-large Dave Holmes called them “Apple’s best product in years.” Four years later, Apple’s second stab has only improved the superb noise cancellation and sound quality we already loved, and added in a slate of beloved features from the AirPods Pro (think Live Translation, Conversation Awareness, and Personalized Volume), without sacrificing the sleek build.

Since the second generation was released in March 2026, the lowest we’ve seen them go is $398. That said, a $120 price cut is nothing to scoff at, especially in an age where our favorite tech has fallen prey to price increases. Note that these tend to sell out pretty quickly when they’re more than $100 off.

#6769. The Brand New Apple Watch Series 12 Is on Sale Just in Time for the Holidays

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Score 18% off Apple’s latest and greatest.

It’s no secret that Apple has been dominating tech headlines this year. But between the news of its recent price hikes on MacBooks and iPads and the release of the iPhone Duo (the tech giant’s first foldable), not much fanfare has been made about the new generations of the Apple Watch. As far as we’re concerned, that’s a damn shame, because they’re well worth the upgrade—especially if you can find one on discount.

Right on cue, Amazon’s Black Friday sale is already here, and with it comes with a record-low deal on the brand-new Apple Watch Series 12 and a sub-$200 price on the entry-level Apple Watch SE 3.

Below, we’re breaking down the differences between each model—and peeling back the curtain on our price-tracking prowess—so you’re all set to shop. After all, when Apple gadgets are this cheap, they tend to go like hotcakes, so don’t say we didn’t warn you when they inevitably sell out.

Commerce editor Luke Guillory argued that compared to other fitness trackers on the market, Apple was lagging behind with its lack of readiness score functionality—until now. The Apple Watch Series 12 marks the debut of the brand’s very own readiness score thanks to a revamped tracking system. Plus, the watch now measures your heart rate more frequently to deliver Heart Rate Variability (HRV) readings every five minutes. If you’re trying to pay closer attention to your health this year, it’s a smart buy, made sweeter when it’s on sale for Black Friday.

For October Prime Day, this brand-new watch surprisingly dropped to just $350. We would have been pleased with that same discount for Black Friday, so this $70 discount is all the more impressive.

If you’re looking for Apple’s most affordable watch, we suggest turning your attention to the entry-level Apple Watch SE 3. It was actually the watch that converted Gelbart to Apple wearables in the first place, arguing that its beauty is in its simplicity.

“It's an accurate and predictive workout tracker. It counts my steps and activity and connects to my phone to play music and receive messages and calls,” he says. “If you’re new to the Apple Watch and want to dip your toes, this is where I recommend you start.” It has the same sleep tracking features, like nightly sleep score and sleep apnea notifications, but lacks the ECG app or hypertension notifications of its newer siblings. It also has an 18-hour battery life so you’ll need to charge most nights.

This Black Friday discount brings the SE 3 back down to the record-low $199 we first saw during October Prime Day, making it by far the most affordable Apple Watch on the market.

Our philosophy when it comes to shopping for Apple tech during Black Friday? If you find the watch with the features you need at a price you can stomach, pick it up. It’s incredibly likely that the deals you see here are the best ones you’ll see for the rest of the year.

At a Senate Judiciary Committee hearing on Wednesday, seven fools who could soon have federal seats refused to admit that Joe Biden won in 2020. Yikes.

The Republicans in Congress are scrambling to fill as many government positions with unqualified replicants as they can find before the clock runs out on their majorities. The giddyap on these nominations has impressed Democrats as being unseemly. From Nomination Notes on Substack:

This has resulted in some hilarious testimonial follies and at least one near hooley between the two senators from Texas. On Tuesday, it was a panel full of judicial nominees, and there were some real lulus in there. The Democrats on the committee decided to test the nominees’ independence. This is just equal parts silly and insulting by now. Just say it, people. Joe Biden won the 2020 election.

The real dustup came over the nomination of one T. Elliot Gaiser, the head of the DOJ’s Office of Legal Counsel, to a seat on a federal district court in Texas. I will grant you that the draining of Congress’s war powers to the executive branch is a horse that left the barn decades ago; in fact, on Thursday, the Senate killed a war-powers resolution as regards the Iran war. But sooner or later there has to be a test case, and it might as well be Gaiser.

#6761. Jack O’Connell on McQueen, Ink, and the “Heavy Hitters” of Sinners

He may not be James Bond, but the actor is still dressed to kill.

Jack O’Connell may not be James Bond, but he’s still dressed to kill. At the 17th annual Governors Awards, hosted by the Academy of Motion Picture Arts and Sciences, the Sinners and 28 Years Later: The Bone Temple star rolled up in a mono-black suit sourced from McQueen’s Spring/Summer 2027 pre-collection: a tailored jacket with ultrawide lapels, black wool barathea trousers, and polished Jermyn brogues. If anyone at the awards exuded main-character energy, it was O’Connell.

“It’s a real lovely, beautiful chance to congregate and celebrate,” O’Connell tells me days later. “You come away from them feeling really inspired. There’s a lot of love in that room.”

He adds, “It is my mission now to binge Glenn Close [movies].” (The 79-year-old legend received an Academy Honorary Award for her many decades of contributions to the industry.)

But O’Connell’s all-black eveningwear, courtesy of McQueen, is award worthy on its own. No stranger to events like these, “sometimes you don’t wanna look uniform,” O’Connell says. The now grown-up James Cook from Skins opted for a double-breasted jacket with distinctly large lapels that he says were “almost a winged tip.” He felt it was something Alexander McQueen himself might have imagined. “Just a dapper bit of Lee McQueen—it’s nice, isn’t it? To push the boat out a little bit.”

He credits his stylist, Luke Day, for pointing him in the right direction. “We pick our moments. Sometimes we want to toe the line; sometimes we don’t. You don’t always feel like slapping a dicky bow tie on. It’s great. It mixes it up.”

When O’Connell isn’t mixing it up in front of flashing cameras, he likes to dress to his surroundings—as any man ought to. “If I’m in the countryside, it’s boots. If I’m in the city, it’s kicks. I really like a lot of Axel Arigatos on my feet. But Reebok was always the staple. I love a Reebok.”

The man could use a pair of Reeboks, because he’s had quite the year running ahead. In 2025, he was a most memorable menace in Ryan Coogler’s vampire scorcher Sinners. He kicked off 2026 in 28 Years Later: The Bone Temple as Jimmy Crystal, the bleach-blond cult leader of butt-kicking devil worshippers in the zombie apocalypse. He will soon star in Ink (which hits select theaters December 11 before it lands on Netflix on January 8), Danny Boyle’s screen adaptation of the stage play about the British tabloid The Sun. If it bleeds, it leads, indeed.

All the while, his name got thrown into the mix as the next James Bond. Though O’Connell had public support from friends like Boyle, he downplayed how much he was actually in the running (as in an October interview with Entertainment Weekly). Now that Robert Pattinson is 007, he doesn’t have to answer those questions anymore. “He’s one of the best in the biz, isn’t he? I love his work,” O’Connell says of R-Patz. “He’s a great set of hands. Anything he turns to, he just kinda nails. Really interesting actor, makes very unobvious choices. I’ve been a fan of his for a long time.”

Without Bond looming over him, O’Connell is free to focus on what he’s actually cooking up. Lately, he’s been hard at work on two big blockbusters for 2027: Godzilla x Kong: Supernova and A Quiet Place Part III. “It’s been one of my favorites for sure,” O’Connell says, reflecting on the past year. “Tied for going to the Oscars and watching my friends win trophies, it is up there as one of the best experiences of my life. I hope we keep pushing.”

So what can audiences expect to see in Supernova? Besides monster wrestling, of course. “We follow Kaitlyn [Dever]’s character up the ranks,” he explains. “She’s studying these monsters. Her brother’s part of a new organization. I can’t give too much away about it. And Trapper’s just being Trapper. You know how Trapper is.”

The project, with its green screens and CGI kaiju, is the biggest O’Connell has undertaken yet. But the large-scale endeavor brings him back to his childhood. “I remember kids running around the playground pretending to be Godzilla,” he says. “It’s omnipresent, isn’t it?”

He adds, “It’s not every job that I’m doing that type of thing. It was great to explore it to the maximum. You know who the main characters are. It’s not the people. It’s great supporting these pixelated legends.”

O’Connell can say even less about A Quiet Place Part III. “It’s in the aftermath of this horrific apocalyptic event,” he says. “It’s a tale of trauma, loss. It’s stuntier than any of us anticipated. But at the center of it is this very beautiful human story where we’re saying, What is the cost of all this fucking chaos and carnage, you know?”

But in some ways, O’Connell is still riding the high of Sinners. “People responded to it in such a way,” he says about the Oscar-winning thriller in retrospect. “It was great to see people unite at the cinema en masse. It’s great when one film can do that. I put it down to Ryan Coogler, our cast, and our entire team across the board. Everywhere you looked, it was packed with heavy hitters.”

Next time you catch O’Connell, bet he’s dressed in McQueen. “I really love what Alexander McQueen, as a person and as a creative, stood for,” he says. “We’re from similar backgrounds, which is rare for me in this world. It makes it meaningful. I celebrate his story, so any opportunity to work with them is a really heartfelt one.”

He swears you’ve seen nothing yet. “I don’t feel like I’ve done my best work yet. Far from it.”

#6760. “It Was Never About Fashion”: Emily in Paris’s Costume Designer Reveals the Method to Her Madness

“Emily in Paris: Fashion Without Rules,” a new show at SCAD Fashion in Atlanta, is devoted to the series’s unique style

Emily in Paris premiered when audiences needed it most. The Netflix original, created by Sex and the City producer Darren Star, offered an escapist fantasy: a young girl trying to find love and success in Paris in October 2020. The show functioned as a reprieve from the pandemic, a dreamy alternative in which we could vicariously live through Emily (Lily Collins) as she gallivanted around the City of Light. It was and still is delectably digestible television.

The love for Emily in Paris was anything but unanimous. Critics wasted no time tearing the show apart, with some targeting its depictions of French culture or claiming the script lacked the bite of Star’s previous television shows. The criticism extended to Emily’s idiosyncratic wardrobe, with some writing that the clothing leaned hard on American stereotypes of French fashion or looked cartoonish, with clashing patterns and bright colors. Costume designer Marylin Fitoussi took these criticisms head-on and doubled down. Emily’s outfits weren’t meant to be couture or designer; they were meant to dress a girl finding her sense of self, expressing her desire to explore the unknown.

For six seasons, Fitoussi curated a wardrobe fit for Emily, a bright-eyed young woman unafraid to try something new, to break the rules. This mirrors Fitoussi’s personal philosophy on clothing: that fashion is meant to break rules, to push boundaries, to provide a space of unrelenting experimentation. Ahead of the sixth season, SCAD FASH Museum of Fashion + Film compiles six seasons’ worth of outfits in “Emily in Paris: Fashion Without Rules,” on view through March 14, 2027. The exhibition lives up to its name; for the designer, clothing is a mode of expression that lets anyone feel free, wherever they are. It offers a character study, tracing Emily’s evolution from a doe-eyed expat to a well-versed Parisian resident. (And worry not, there are also looks from Emily’s chic boss Sylvie—played with devastating elegance by Philippine Leroy-Beaulieu.)

Ahead of season 6, Bazaar sat down with Fitoussi at SCAD Fashion in Atlanta to discuss working with Lily Collins, her swan song for Emily in Paris, and how everyone has the potential for creativity.

Marylin Fitoussi: I don’t recall a contemporary TV show being in the spotlight in this way. Generally, when you think of an exhibition, you think of period pieces or couture pieces. The show is really part of pop culture now!

MF: I was surprised when fashion magazines [criticized the show] at the beginning. I’m not a fashion designer, [but] I love fashion and I love clothes. This show was not about fashion at the beginning. [Emily] is a bubbly character with a very strong personality from Chicago; she couldn’t have come dressed in Comme des Garçons or in Yamamoto. I needed to have this cultural shock. She’s a fish-out-of-water character, and we needed something to catch the audience’s eyes. When you want to show people that you love the culture but you can’t express it because you don’t know the codes, you don’t speak the language, the only way you find it is through clothes. It was never about “fashion.” Sometimes, it’s about creativity, individuality, making something not trendy at all. The audience got that, and fashion magazines didn’t get it, so they killed me.

MF: Many times, I’ve asked myself, “Why does this little gingham suit have so much influence?” It’s quite simple. It was the most duplicated, copied, reposted, fan-arted, and used as a Halloween costume. It touched people because it was for them. The French can have their own version of it, their own concept, but for the entire world, it was this one little girly, timeless outfit with the red beret, the red lips, the red soul.

MF: She read the bad press after the release of season 1, and we had a talk. I said they hated it in France; they find it hideous. [I asked her] “Do you want me to change [it]?” She was smiling and said, “No, we can’t, and we won’t please everybody. We need to keep this strong DNA. We need to continue to have this unexpected silhouette because it’s what catches the audience’s eye.” It’s a guilty pleasure. We can say, I love it.

It opens a way for the audience to see fashion in a different way, where you can mix in an awkward way and free people from this law where you shouldn’t [do that]. Coco Chanel used to say, “Before leaving a room, look at yourself in the mirror and take something out.” I always say, “Put something more on.”

MF: I wanted to present a more elevated version, more mature, more evolved, but still true to what she was and what she is, with an eclectic combination and a little bit awkward. She knows the French rules, but as I do, she finds French fashion so boring. In general, I find that fashion is less and less interesting. Why are fashion and celebrity stylists sourcing more and more from archives? What it’s showing is that now everything is the same. Everything is boring, and I understand why. They need to sell the show! We don’t have to sell anything. We have to sell dreams, illusions, craziness, boldness. People watch the show in groups. Some people say, “I love it,” and some say, “I hate it,” and they have a dialogue and fight and have a good [time]. We love that.

MF: A young designer wants to be famous right away. Fame doesn’t happen, and it doesn’t matter. You need to be happy with what you’re doing. With costume design versus fashion design, you can be much freer. You don’t have anything to sell, and you don’t have to design the “It bag.” If your show is successful, it’s a win-win for everybody. To see that, you need to be true to your vision. You need to be true to your gut feeling, to your vision, to your DNA. Talent is not enough; you need to be at the right place at the right time. Being discovered by Darren Star … he changed my life. At some point, you find the right project for you.

You need to work a lot to take care of every detail. You need to be strong and push the boundaries. You need to try to show a different way to see costumes and clothes, to see that everything is possible…. Style begins when fear ends. You don’t want to please everybody, and I’m always attracted to people who dress in a different way, and I’m never judgmental. To create something is to expose yourself. It’s very vulnerable. It’s brave.

This interview has been condensed and edited for clarity.

#6758. One Martini to Rule Them All

The case for ordering a vodka martini, always.

At a dinner a few years ago, my fiancé’s mother, an elegant former Wall Street banker, ordered a vodka martini, dry, with three olives. I grew up in New Orleans, where I was weaned on sickeningly sweet, sticky cocktails, but I was always fascinated by the martini’s sophistication. I’d seen the drink in the hands of characters like Roger Sterling from Mad Men, who would expertly balance his V-shaped glass as he dropped a cynical witticism. So that night, I told the waiter to make it two. Ever since then, I have favored vodka martinis over gin, the base spirit for the by-the-books recipe.

I’m not the only one. Bartenders in New York have started to notice a trend away from gin. Chris Amirault of Bar Chimera in Manhattan says that he makes four vodka martinis for every one gin martini. “Vodka pays the bills, and it’s what a lot of people prefer,” says Brian Evans of the Lobby Bar at the Hotel Chelsea, also in Manhattan.

For those who insist that a true martini must only be made with gin, Tristan Stephenson, host of the Curious Bartender podcast, says, “History isn’t on the side of purists. The martini has been a moving target since the 1880s. It started sweeter, with maraschino, orange curaçao, and sugar syrup making appearances. Then it dried out over decades, then had the vermouth all but exorcised from it. Then came olive brine and everything since.”

Why do many martini drinkers prefer vodka? Ti Adelaide Martin, coproprietor of New Orleans staple Commander’s Palace, succinctly explains one reason: “Vodka is the more approachable spirit.” Unlike gin, whose botanicals impart noticeable flavors to a martini, vodka doesn’t overpower other ingredients. It’s more of a blank canvas, which is why it’s used for variations like the espresso martini, which is now the fifth-most-ordered drink at bars, according to Drinks International. Instead of strong flavor, vodka provides “texture, weight, and a kind of icy austerity that can be genuinely beautiful when the vodka is good and the drink is properly cold,” says Stephenson.

And, let’s be honest, we also drink vodka martinis for the booze. “Vodka tastes bracing,” says Esquire editor in chief Michael Sebastian. “I want to take a sip of my martini and get the feeling that it’s strong and it’s cold, and it goes through your whole body and you feel better and loose immediately.”

Now I order my vodka martini with a splash of vermouth, just dirty enough to make me thirsty, and shaken so that thin ice shards float on top. You might prefer yours vermouth forward, citrus focused, or dirty as swamp water. It’s all fair game. And that’s the beauty of a vodka martini: It’s a personal drink. My future mother-in-law has her order, and I have mine. What’s yours going to be?

#6756. Why Don’t You … Take Fashion Inspiration from the World’s Great Performers?

Diana Vreeland had a singular take on not only fashion but life. Her eccentricity has become folklore and was best exemplified in an illustrated column of irreverent advice for chic living she called “Why Don’t You?” Resurrected and reimagined for modern life, the column, now written by Bazaar’s executive digital director, Lynette Nylander, will dispense tips on how to live stylishly, decadently, and daringly.

Here’s a thought: Don’t get dressed. Mount a production. Put on a show. Go on tour. And if the reviews are lukewarm, don’t change the costume; blame the critic! For the performance issue, Lynette Nylander takes cues from the stage’s most stylish to arm you with tips for stepping out.

that the “effortless” look took 40 minutes. The “I just threw it on” leather jacket, the smudged eye makeup, the shirt that looks slept in: All of it is stagecraft. Miley Cyrus’s new rock-star look personifies this. She’s not someone to shy away from an “era,” and this one is all tousled hair, loads of lashes, and leather and lace from the likes of Tom Ford, Roberto Cavalli, and Saint Laurent. Effortless? No. Fabulous? Absolutely!

some noise. Taffeta rustles, silk whispers, embellishments jangle, and leather creaks with intent. Performance is not only visual. The swish of a skirt on a staircase is part of the entrance, and the weight of crepe hugging the hips is part of the walk. The late, great Tina Turner was a master of clothes that created a symphony as she writhed around the stage; often, they were the creations of the late, great Bob Mackie. Björk went viral earlier this year at the Venice Biennale in a neon-pink Bottega Veneta dress made of fiberglass shards that jingle-jangled together as she fist-pumped. The lesson? Dress for the ear as well as the eye.

the precision of a uniform. The chef’s whites, the lawyer’s suit, the professor’s tweeds, the tailored black of the gallery assistant: Each is a costume designed with a very particular audience in mind. Even people who wear the same thing every day are making a statement, and the strongest of them curate that repetition with real rigor. Consistency, done well, is a kind of theater. Just don’t make it boring! Consider Steve Jobs, who famously wore only Issey Miyake turtlenecks and Levi’s. Truly the only tech titan to ever have taste!

the art of quick change. For Céline Dion’s fall residency at the Plénitude Arena in Paris, underneath the spotlight wasn’t the only site where a performance took place. Like all true divas, Dion knows that every moment of every day is an opportunity to perform. Outside of the Royal Monceau hotel, her home in Paris, where her faithful fans lined up to greet her, she treated onlookers to a dance, a song, an air-kiss, and always a fashion show: off-the-runway looks from Jacquemus, Loewe, and Phoebe Philo. As they say, all the world’s a stage.

the ordinary garment its moment. Not every act needs sequins. A perfect white tee, a pair of jeans that have taken on the shape of a life, a forever leather jacket worn with confidence: these are the understudies who steal the show. Performance in fashion is not always about drama. Sometimes it is the quiet authority of a simple thing worn exactly right. A disciple of this way of dressing? Kim Gordon, whose effortless style, both onstage and offstage, has made her your favorite celebrity’s favorite celebrity for the past 40 years.
