#6755. The 27 Best Video Games of 2026 (So Far)

With Grand Theft Auto VI finally in gamers’ hands, 2026 might go down as one of the greatest years in the medium’s history.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Will 2026 go down as one of the best years in video games? There just simply aren’t enough hours in the calendar year to play through all of the amazing games that came out before Thursday, November 19, and now we have to add on hundreds of hours of Grand Theft Auto VI.

Of course, the industry’s economic woes cannot be overstated. It’s a dark time when even prolific studios downsize, slash budgets, and lay off talented developers with invaluable institutional knowledge. Even the GTA VI designers—whose game is already heralded as a masterpiece—are still fighting for better working conditions. So it’s hard to actually call 2026 a great year in gaming when it’s also been a year of heartbreak and uncertainty for the future.

But the games are still great. Even just a few hours into GTA VI, it’s obvious that it’s a massive achievement for Rockstar Games that’s well worth the wait. And just this past month, Nintendo dropped a remake of The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time that features beautifully upgraded graphics from the original 1998 classic. Elsewhere, Resident Evil Requiem nailed the franchise’s decades-long identity split with a game that’s half third-person action and half first-person survival horror. Plus, action games like 007 First Light, Control Resonant, and Onimusha: Way of the Sword let loose the power fantasies we’ve only dreamed of.

But it’s not all blood and guts and guns and swords. Earlier this year, the coming-of-age Mixtape caused a ruckus among gaming circles, though anyone who’s played it and loved it will tell you just how much it’s a love letter to bygone summer days. Oh, and a spin-off Pokémon title about building adorable towns and protecting the environment is currently the highest-rated game in the franchise’s thirty-year history.

So, unless you’re already dedicated to spending all of Thanksgiving and Christmas in GTA VI’s Vice City, some of the best games Esquire’s editors have played through in 2026 so far.

GTA VI hadn’t even been out for twenty-four hours yet and critics were already calling it a masterpiece. To be fair, video game critics call a lot of titles masterpieces, but it was clear from everyone’s immediate reaction to Vice City that Rockstar Games spent the last decade making something truly special. Sure, there are hundreds of hours of side quests and town-foolery to get lost in, but GTA VI also features a Bonnie & Clyde-esque love story between its two playable protagonists—Jason and Lucia—which breaks up the beloved smash-and-grab chaos of the Grand Theft Auto franchise and makes the gamer question just where the point between a video game and real life begins.

At its most basic, Nintendo’s The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time remake isn’t all that different from the classic, 1998 original. The game certainly looks a lot better on the Nintendo Switch 2 than it did on the Nintendo 64, now that the polygonal characters and sharp outdoors have been converted to more living and breathing graphics. But at the end of the day, you’re still playing the same game that you played in 1998. Don’t take that as a knock, though! Ocarina of Time still tops “Best Video Games of All Time” lists for a reason.

There are times playing Star Wars: Galactic Racer when you will feel like you are going too fast. And yes, the rate at which it will feel like that will shock you. But that’s pod racing! And better than any racing sim, let alone a Star Wars video game with voice acting as good as Galactic Racer, it truly feels like you’re speeding around in a hunk-a-junk that you just cobbled together with space garbage and jet engines.

Following up Fire Emblem: Three Houses was already a tall task, let alone having to follow Fire Emblem: Engage. Both titles combined the best of Japanese RPGs with the new slice-of-life school relationship activities introduced in Three Houses. And now Fortune’s Weave, something of a trilogy-capper for the series, is the most maximalist entry yet. With dozens of characters, choices, a vast map, and four unique story quests that all overlap, there’s so much to do in the latest Fire Emblem game that they could wait another five years before the next entry.

The Blood of Dawnwalker is the debut game from Rebel Wolves, a new studio started by former creatives from The Witcher 3: Blood Hunt. So if Dawnwalker feels eerily like a Witcher game, you’re not crazy. That being said, the vampire game’s controversial timer—which forces players to make quick decisions with big impacts on gameplay—fully separated the RPG and proved that the new studio was ready to try out exciting ideas all its own.

Remedy’s Alan Wake 2 topped Esquire’s Best Video Games of 2023 list three years ago, so you can bet we were excited for the studio’s next game, Control Resonant. The latest is a sequel to 2019’s Control, which now finds its former protagonist Jesse Faden missing. So, support character Dylan Faden, now in the spotlight, takes control to deal with the foreign entities and save his estranged sister.

Can video games have too much of a good thing? Apparently not. Phantom Blade Zero, the umpteenth samurai RPG since Sekiro: Shadows Die Twice and Ghost of Tsushima, dazzled gamers with its combat and fluid martial arts combos. And while it firmly isn’t a soulslike—the developer explicitly rejects the genre, actually—that still doesn’t mean Phantom Blade Zero is a walk in the park either.

How about a game that’s a little less stressful? If you need a break from the fast-paced action, check out Big Walk, a co-operative puzzle game about, well, going on a long walk. The charming game allows players to communicate only if they’re close to one another on Big Walk’s big map, providing a bit of a challenge as you progress on your journey while also adding some intimacy to the digital experience. Plus, the characters are cute as hell.

Star Wars fans ate good this year. If racing isn’t your thing, check out Zero Company, a turn-based tactics game that falls somewhere in between Fire Emblem and XCOM. Though the game isn’t all that challenging on easier difficulties, players have found a lot of fun and repeat plays with the game’s permadeath mode that doesn’t allow characters to return after they die.

Rock out with your claws out. Insomniac applies its winning formula for triple-A superhero games to flesh out the most popular X-Men member of all time. The five-foot-four berserker stars in his own solo adventure in a universe where mutants live in secret. While piecing together his own memories, Wolverine meets a telepath named Jean Grey. Together they must stop a powerful industrialist from unleashing a weapon that could kill all mutants. A hyper-violent tour through some of its title antihero’s classic comics, Wolverine doesn’t quite reach the same heights as Spider-Man, but the way it lets players truly feel like “The Best There Is” is gold.

If you could rewind to your last day of school, what would it look like? Who are you with? And most importantly, what’s on the stereo? At the start of summer, developers Beethoven & Dinosaur released the gorgeous narrative game Mixtape, a loving homage to coming-of-age cinema with a tastefully curated soundtrack. (We’re talking Joy Division, Silverchair, B.J. Thomas, and more.) Set in the summer of 1995, the game sees three California teens relish their final hours together before going their separate ways. While Mixtape kicked up a storm among gamers over what defines a “game”—you can’t die or fail, nor are any of the things you do in Mixtape remotely challenging—it’s still a special experience that I argue illuminates how special games can be as a storytelling medium. Your adolescence probably didn’t look exactly like Mixtape, but the game can nonetheless trigger old memories.

As the world waits with bated breath to see who will play the next James Bond on the big screen, video games have a Bond to call their own in actor Patrick Gibson. From developer IO Interactive, of the smash Hitman series, comes 007 First Light, a high-octane espionage action game that follows a new James Bond in the early days of his career at MI6. Unlike the Hitman games, which are mired in the darkness of Agent 47’s deadly profession, 007 First Light revels in the fantasy of Bond’s perilous and pleasurable lifestyle. It’s basically the closest any of us will get to feeling like James Bond short of renting an Aston Martin for a test drive.

It’s been 12 years since Yacht Club Games released Shovel Knight, which was deemed by many to be one of the leading retro-inspired indie hits of the 2010s. Now Yacht Club has done it again with Mina the Hollower, which mines plenty of inspiration from gothic dungeon crawlers like Castlevania and Game Boy–era Legend of Zelda games, with modern flourishes à la Dark Souls. Players control Mina, a genius 19th-century mouse who must slay monsters and restore power to her home. Don’t let its cute looks fool you: Mina the Hollower is a challenging game that will have you sweating and smiling all the way through.

Remember dropping quarters into Marvel vs. Capcom 2 back in the day? The legacy of Marvel’s 2D-fighting heritage lives on in Marvel Tōkon: Fighting Souls, a shiny new team-based fighter with an anime-inspired take on the Marvel universe. Heroes like Captain America, Iron Man, Spider-Man, Storm, Deadpool, and Blade—and even villains like Doctor Doom—team up to save the world from an alien menace. The game is developed by Arc System Works, arguably the modern masters of the genre, boasting titles like Guilty Gear, BlazBlue, and Dragon Ball FighterZ, and the studio applies its decades of expertise to one of the finest and most nuanced fighting games we’ve ever had the pleasure of throwing our controllers at.

There’s no shortage of killer samurai games nowadays. Which is perhaps why Capcom saw fit to resurrect its dormant Onimusha franchise with the exceptional Onimusha: Way of the Sword. Essentially a reboot of the series (whose last title was released all the way back in 2006 for the PlayStation 2), Way of the Sword follows legendary swordsman Miyamoto Musashi as he comes into possession of the Oni Gauntlet and must cleanse the world of a supernatural evil. While not quite a Souls-like, the game offers an adequately complex combat system that ensures sword slashing never gets stale. And slicing demons in half feels oh so satisfying.

If you, like me, have fond memories of Initial D, Forza Horizon 6 is an invitation to awaken the sleeping gearhead within. The open-world racing series finally makes its way to Japan, the birthplace of drift racing, to let players roam dense city streets and wide-open mountain roads in tricked-out Hondas, Nissans, and exotic supercars. The Forza Horizon series has reached its peak with this sixth installment, with photorealistic graphics that can fool you into believing you’re actually speeding through traffic halfway around the world.

After almost 30 years of Resident Evil games, it’s no surprise that the ninth installment is another polished thrill ride. Capcom plays the hits to great success in Resident Evil Requiem, a survival-horror game that hits the right notes even when it’s serving up Leon S. Kennedy fan service. But in the roughly 10-to-18-hour campaign (a great length!), the gameplay of FBI newbie Grace Ashcroft is more horror focused and full of fresh twists on the Resident Evil formula. Though we won’t spoil it here, Esquire’s review in February also teased that the latest Resident Evil game contains “one major set piece in particular that rivals some of the best action-movie moments in recent years.”

Don’t let Pokémon Pokopia’s cozy gameplay fool you—the latest spin-off of the cutesy monster-collecting phenomenon is actually one of the deepest games in the franchise’s nearly thirty-year history. Sure, you will spend most of your time in Pokopia building houses for your Pokémon to live in or meticulously gardening. But underneath the casual novelties of building a perfect little Pokémon town lives a surprisingly emotional story about climate-change disasters, grief, and the importance of taking care of Earth’s natural environment.

Saros is a gameplay-first, story-second action game that feels incredible. Much as with the developer’s previous game, Returnal, the third-person shooting is inspired by arcade shooters where bullets fill your screen nonstop. Unlike Returnal, it’s not brutally difficult. Gameplay is fast and exhilarating—and sometimes overwhelming—but it doesn’t take long before you know exactly which projectiles to absorb, which to dodge, and which to parry. The PlayStation-exclusive title also has one of the best gameplay loops of the year, and it does more with the DualSense controller’s advanced haptic feedback than any other game on the Sony system. Merging the run-based progression of Hades with a more traditional level-based structure to enormous success, Saros is difficult yet approachable, rewarding players with a ton of resources to invest in upgrades as it slowly unfurls its mystery-box story bit by bit.

With the release of No More Heroes III in 2021, Grasshopper Manufacture finally put the saga of Travis Touchdown to rest and kicked off its next era with Romeo Is a Dead Man. The studio’s latest game retells Shakespeare’s famous love story mashed up with 1980s sci-fi, Japanese tokusatsu, and grind-house cinema. The game puts players in control of Romeo Stargazer, a small-town deputy who falls in love with an amnesiac named Juliet. But when Romeo dies, he is reborn as a half-man, half-machine superhero who embarks on a quest to find his missing love. Romeo Is a Dead Man feels like immersing oneself in an obscure Troma film, and it’s as madcap and magnificent as it sounds.

Remastered video games are always a tough sell—though with every Nintendo fan having no issue coughing up $30 to play Pokémon FireRed & LeafGreen on the Switch 2 this year, it seems times have changed. Luckily Dragon Quest VII Reimagined is much more than a port to a newer system. The remake, which includes many quality-of-life improvements, was rebuilt from the ground up with glorious 3D animation. If you’ve never played the classic Japanese RPG before, the charm of this latest remake is enough to make you a Dragon Quest fan for life.

If you’re too scared to play horror games alone, let Reanimal’s multiplayer gameplay bring people together. Set in an island town overrun with monsters and mysterious creatures, Reanimal follows a nameless brother and sister who must survive the island’s horrors if they want to save their friends. With a story as cryptic as some of its puzzles, Reanimal carries the baton of other memorable platformers like Limbo and Little Nightmares to deliver an otherworldly experience. Although Reanimal can be played alone, it is a journey best taken with someone by your side.

Video games are all about rewarding the player for progression, and there may be no better equivalent to this feeling in the real world than rock climbing. Cairn, a new indie title that focuses on this extreme sport, captures the similarities beautifully. As the player works their way up the mountain, juggling the character’s stamina and gauging the next best foothold, it is amazing how intense and immersive Cairn feels compared with its relatively beautiful visuals. But as with any good climb, the surprisingly difficult journey is more than worth it when you finally reach the top.

If you thought Doom had too many pixelated demons and not enough rubber-hose whimsy, Mouse: P.I. for Hire is your game. Heavily inspired by classic Disney, Fleischer, and MGM cartoons—and clearly taking some cues from the 2017 hit Cuphead—Mouse: P.I. for Hire puts players in control of an anthropomorphic mouse detective on his latest case in Mouseburg. The game’s fast-paced first-person-shooter action is delightfully complemented by its (literally) cartoonish antics. Hand-drawn pistol and shotgun blasts and reloading animations are as much a treat to watch as the game is fun to play. If gaming today feels more complicated than ever, Mouse: P.I. for Hire brings it back to basics with a novelty coat of paint.

Nintendo should have called the new Mario Tennis game Mario Tennis Chaos, because the player needs to understand right up front that skills on the court can carry them only so far in Mario Tennis Fever. Much like the 24 drivers per race in Mario Kart World, a calamity of new power-ups renders tennis into a zany, uncontrollable farce of a sport that becomes one of the best Mario sports titles in Nintendo’s history—once you learn to let go and embrace the nonsense.

The decades-old Marvel vs. Capcom series still reigns supreme as the best superhero tag fighter in video games, but Invincible VS is set on taking the throne. Based on the hit Amazon series (with some of its stars reprising their voice roles, like Oscar-winner J.K. Simmons as Omni-Man), Invincible VS is a 3v3 tag-team fighting game featuring playable characters from Robert Kirkman’s comic-book universe. The game tells a stand-alone story adjacent to season 3 in which Mark must stand with his allies to save Earth from the Viltrumites. (Hence the fighting.) Although its mechanical controls are relatively simple compared with those of other fighting games, it’s no less elegant and fun, and the game features some gnarly cel-shaded 3D animation that’s simply gorgeous. Besides, it’s another way to pummel your buddies to a bloody pulp. Talk about threatening us with a good time.

Dead Space? More like Dad Space. Pragmata is Capcom’s latest original game and a potential franchise in the making, its story centered around a systems engineer named Hugh and his foster-father relationship with an adorable android nicknamed Diana. With a rogue AI overtaking a research station on the moon, Hugh and Diana must work together to take the AI offline and make it back to Earth. Between its breakneck gameplay involving grid-like puzzles (which must be solved on the fly) and its endearing story about parenthood, Pragmata is what you might get if you asked ChatGPT, “What if Alien and Marvel’s Logan could be a single video game?”

#6754. 27 Charming Christmas Towns That Look Like a Real-Life Holiday Movie

Think twinkling lights, towering trees, and Santa-themed bike rides.

The holiday season is in full swing, with many halls and houses already decked to impress in garland and fairy lights. But as you wrap gifts, prepare to host, and begin marathoning holiday movies, it can feel like the joyous season is coming to an end much too soon. We can't blame you if you're still wanting more from this merry time of year, and we've come up with a solution. There are plenty of beautiful Christmas towns across the country to fill your heart with peace, joy, and nostalgia—you just have to know where to look.

These towns are more than just beautifully decorated—they’re truly immersive, offering experiences from Santa-themed bike rides to orchestrated light shows and winter bar crawls. Below, we've rounded up the 27 most charming Christmas towns from all over the country, so no matter where you live, you'll be close enough to find a bit of extra cheer this year.

This picturesque mountain town serves as the perfect holiday backdrop for more reasons than just the ever-present blanket of fluffy white snow. Each year, the valley is transformed into a Christmas town with seasonal theatrical performances, Gunnison’s Night of Lights, a tree lighting ceremony, and even an ice sculpting event.

Ever since the formation of this city on Christmas Eve in 1741, Bethlehem, Pennsylvania, has been rooted in the holiday spirit. From Christmas markets to historical tours to horse-drawn carriage rides, there's no doubt this town won't feel magical. And make sure to stop by the historic Sayre Mansion, pictured here, to check out their elegant holiday decor.

Annapolis is known as one of the best cities during the holiday season thanks to its winter weather and historic downtown. If you're lucky enough to visit this Christmas town, make sure you spend time bargain shopping until midnight, checking out the spectacular light display in Sandy Point State Park, and viewing the Yacht Club Light Parade.

Chicago is exceptionally beautiful during the Christmas time. From the seemingly endless strings of lights to the Christkindlmarket, which is free to enter and so charming, the Windy City has many offerings during the holiday season, making it one of the best Christmas towns to visit. Plus, you don't want to miss ice skating or any Christmas-themed museum exhibits.

If you want to do something Christmas-y almost every single day of December, then Newport, Rhode Island, might just be the place to go. To create the perfect coastal Christmas, this town offers a month-long celebration that's been going strong for 54 years. From holiday bike rides to Medieval festivals to winter afternoon teas, there's so much to do in this Christmas town.

You'll feel as if you've traveled back to Charles Dickens's time when you walk through Franklin's historic Christmas festival. Characters from A Christmas Carol and Oliver Twist come to life throughout the weekend-long festivities. Roam the Victorian Village and try Victorian-era Christmas treats like sugar plums while listening to carolers.

A classic ski town, Park City offers Christmas markets, festive decorations, and a Christmas Eve parade lit by torches—plus spectacular views of the mountains are around every corner.

In the heart of Virginia horse country, you'll find one of the most festive Christmas towns. Cheer on the town's hunting hounds while sipping hard cider and wander the streets in search of vintage Christmas ornaments.

During Kennebunkport's annual Christmas Prelude, the town puts on a very New England celebration: You can watch Santa Claus arrive by lobster boat, go on seashore holiday trolley rides, and enjoy wine tastings and lobster bakes. Not to mention, the town is covered in Christmas lights and garlands that make it the perfect festive photo backdrop.

For a dreamy and unique experience, head to Santa Fe. One of the most anticipated celebrations is the Canyon Road Farolito Walk, which is when thousands of farolitos—luminaries made of paper bags, sand, and tea candles—cast a glow on the gardens and old buildings that line the road. You also can get your white Christmas fix by visiting Ski Santa Fe.

As one of the oldest towns in the country, Portsmouth is a charming New England destination that celebrates the holidays with a Vintage Christmas Celebration. Visit the historic village of Strawberry Banke or wander around the charming shopping districts in this historic Christmas town.

Get a Hallmark movie feel while experiencing a taste of Denmark in California's Solvang village, located in Santa Ynez Valley wine country. The destination hosts Julefest, a month-long holiday celebration with live entertainment, a parade, and a night market.

Koziar's Christmas Village in Bernville is one of the top attractions in Pennsylvania. With more than a million lights adorning buildings, barns, and the landscape—and a reflective lake that doubles the beauty—it's hard to beat the town's display.

This Bavarian-style village is charming any time of year! December festivities include a display of Santa costumes from all around the world, a gingerbread house competition, carolers, and plenty of photo ops.

Of course, New York City isn't a small Christmas town, but it is extra magical during the holidays. From the massive tree at Rockefeller Center to the various Christmas markets in the city parks with ice skating, shops, and more, you can't beat the Big Apple's holiday spirit.

Nicknamed the Lighted Poinsettia Capital of Texas, this town brings major holiday spirit every year. Visit during December when the lighted trail in Comanche Trail Park is aglow all month long. Check out Hotel Settles for its elegant Art Deco lodging.

During the holiday season, each home in Historic Williamsburg lights one candle, a tradition that dates back to the Revolutionary War era. The cobblestone streets and colonial holiday traditions such as caroling by torchlight make this destination a must, especially for history buffs.

This Christmas town stays decked out year-round, so if you're craving holiday spirit in July, you'll still find candy-cane posts, boughs of holly, and a Santa Claus house with live reindeer!

Christmas without the cold? We've got you covered! In addition to abundant sunshine and festive holiday decor, you'll also have plenty of history to explore in St. Augustine, America's oldest city.

Want to take a ride on the Polar Express? Visit Durango during the holidays. The historic mining town offers a Polar Express–inspired trip that your kiddos will flip over.

#6752. Is The Rings of Power Coming Back for Season 4?

There’s One Ring to rule them all, but two seasons still to go.

There is One Ring to rule them all, but there are still two more seasons to go. The Lord of the Rings: The Rings of Power on Prime Video is fast approaching the end of its third season, which means some fans are now asking what the future has in store for the prequel series. While there aren’t definitive answers just yet, Tolkien fans can rest assured in knowing that a fourth season is more likely to happen than not. Here’s what we know so far.

On November 25, The Rings of Power will release the final two episodes of season 3. So, is season 4 a shoo-in? Almost. Per The Hollywood Reporter in a May 2026 story, preproduction was, at the time, “eyeing a schedule that includes preproduction on a fourth season this fall, with shooting to begin in early 2027.” As far as anyone outside the production knows, this work for season 4 has been underway.

In 2017, Amazon secured the rights to produce a Lord of the Rings television series for an eye-watering price tag of $250 million. In 2018, The Hollywood Reporter wrote that negotiations between Amazon and the Tolkien estate included a five-season commitment, while Amazon maintains authority to greenlight or cancel the series at any time. In October 2025, The Ankler reported that Tolkien has a $20 million per season “kill fee” if Amazon ends the show before its fifth season.

As of right now, Amazon has not formally greenlit The Rings of Power for season 4. But some signs point to it as inevitable. Amazon has not released viewership numbers for the current season, so only Amazon knows how the show is performing on Prime Video. Whether the audience is strong enough to keep the show going or Amazon simply wants to avoid kill fees (and a negative reputation in the industry for failing to finish a pricey Lord of the Rings show), will decide the future. For now, fans should enjoy the sunrise in Rivendell while there is still one.

#6747. Quilted Liner Jackets Are the Swerve Your Wardrobe Needs. Here Are 10 Stellar Picks.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Layering is the name of the game in fall and winter. For the next few months, you are not a person; you’re a living, breathing Russian nesting doll. You are encased in strategic layers of t-shirts, sweaters, scarves, light jackets, and big jackets that you divest yourself of over the course of a day (or night). Like a knight donning his armor, you will spend this season dutifully putting on and taking off each of these individual pieces as you fight the seemingly endless battle against winter.

A few pieces are a given. A good tee certainly isn’t going anywhere, nor is a sweater. The specific kind of Big Coat you use varies depending on the guy and his style, but it’s nevertheless a guaranteed winter staple. Those middle layers can be a bit of a toss up, though, which is how you might encounter something like a quilted liner jacket.

Once upon a time, these padded jackets served as the interior protective shell worn under World War II era flight jackets before they expanded into more mainstream outerwear. The quilted padding traps body heat, but doesn’t make the jacket so heavy that it just feels like another cumbersome layer. You can still wear it as a liner jacket—but lowkey, if it’s the right temperature, they also work pretty well on their own. So if you’re in the market for a new versatile transitional weather jacket, you’ve come to the right place.

If we had to pick one from this entire group, it would be this jacket from Buck Mason ... And it’s really not that close. Inspired by old-school mountaineering outfits, the nylon is woven in Japan, and the down fill has an exceptional warmth rating. Add a elastic cuffs and a fit that works layered underneath or overtop, it’s perfect.

Look, layering’s great and all—but no one talks about how annoying and awkward it is when you get hot enough to take off one layer, but then are stuck just kind of holding onto it until you get the chance to put it down or hang it up. That’s not a problem with this packable Paul Smith shirt jacket. Let it do its magic to keep you warm. When you’re ready to take it off, simply collapse it down, zip it up, and stuff it away into your pocket until you need it again.

Rains has a well-established reputation of balancing fashion and function with their outerwear, whether it’s their Esquire-editor favorite Fishtail Parka or this bomber liner jacket. Nothing beats a classic leather bomber...except maybe the rain (leather + rain = no bueno). That’s not an issue with Rains’ Banja bomber liner. You get that same edgy look, but with a slightly more practical construction. Plus there’s something sleekly minimal about the channel quilting. Layer it beneath your actual bomber or heavy coat of choice for optimal warmth all season long.

Look, we love a good Dickies moment. They make some of our favorite pants of all time, not least of which because they’re mad affordable—though that’s a pretty big element to it. So naturally, if someone comes up to us asking for a high-quality and stylish quilted liner jacket at well under $100, we’ve got one word for ya: Dickies.

Barbour’s been making sturdy outerwear that can withstand the rainy U.K. weather since the 1800s. And while they don’t strictly make a liner jacket, this lightweight—or “Flyweight,” as Barbour calls it—quilted jacket gets pretty close.

With a sleek, collarless design, this Fieldworth jacket can easily slide under any heavy coat. It falls more into “middleweight jacket” territory, meaning it’ll probably keep you toastier than some of the other options on this list—both on its own and when layered up.

If you’re looking for more of a typical fall/spring jacket, J.Crew’s PrimaLoft® jacket is where it’s at. The quintessentially Americana brand uses the down alternative to create a well-insulated transitional weather jacket you can layer with, or wear on its own.

Look, all these other brands and designers are great and all—but Alpha Industries is the real deal. No like seriously. When the company was first founded in Tennessee in the 1950s, it was as a clothing contractor for the United States Department of Defense. These were the guys making those sexy flight jackets long before Tom Cruise shrugged one on in Top Gun. Their Deco Liner nods to the OG liners of yore, featuring a slim, collarless design that will integrate with additional layers. Wear the jacket by itself when you start feeling warmer, and let the whole world see the Alpha embroidery on the back so everyone knows this is some top of line shit.

If you’re in the market for something lighter and less padded, opt for this cotton hunting jacket. The silhouette takes inspiration from vintage Czech military liners and traditional hunting gear to create a layer of outerwear breezy but still warm.

Get two jackets for the price of one with this reversible number from Cos. You get all the benefits of a quilted coat, with the versatile sleekness of a classic collarless jacket.

#6746. What’s in Her Pockets? Gemma Chan Kept Snacks in Her Pink Valentino Dress at the Oscars

The star of Josephine answer Bazaar’s “First Now Next” questionnaire

Gemma Chan first gained our attention in Crazy Rich Asians in 2018, stealing the screen as the glamorous, composed socialite Astrid. Chan imbued this character with a compelling emotional depth beneath the stoic exterior, a skill she honed during years of stage performance and her starring role in the British sci-fi show Humans.

Now Chan wants to produce films, to spend more time in the creative process leading up to filming. Her first film, Josephine, which she also stars in, is out on November 20. Directed by Beth de Araújo, the movie orbits around the titular Josephine, an eight-year-old girl played by the scene-stealing poppet Mason Reeves, and her parents, Claire (Chan) and Damien (Channing Tatum). One morning, when Josephine is running through the woods with her father, she witnesses a rape in the park before her father manages to intervene and save the woman. Josephine grapples with emotions and trauma she doesn’t understand, as her parents desperately try to provide care in opposing ways. Chan delivers a deeply intimate performance, drawing inspiration from the inner strength of the women in her family.

This summer, Chan also starred in Peacock’s show of the summer, Five Star Weekend, based on the novel by Elin Hilderbrand. She joined an all-star cast including Chloë Sevigny, Regina Hall, Jennifer Garner, and D’Arcy Carden. It tells the story of Hollis Shaw (Garner), a recent widow who decides to invite four friends from different stages of her life to her home in Nantucket for a luxurious, healing weekend.

Harper’s Bazaar’s questionnaire series “First, Now, Next” dives into the past, present, and future of some of our favorite creatives, spotlighting the moments and people that have influenced them. We touched base with Chan ahead of Jospehine’s release to talk about working with Channing Tatum, her time in Los Angeles unsuccessfully auditioning for pilots, and her dreams of running a cat sanctuary.

I would say the first film that I watched multiple times was The Sound of Music. We first watched it as a family when I was four years old. I would be imagining myself as one of the Von Trapp children and thought Christopher Plummer was amazing. He’s this terrifying but sophisticated guy who’s standing up to fascism. What’s not to like about that? Later in life, I would say the film that really spoke to me and made me want to become a storyteller was Secrets and Lies by Mike Leigh. What he managed to do there, and the emotional truth that his performers gave, was just extraordinary.

I was really fortunate that one of my first jobs was Secret Diary of a Call Girl. I met some people who are still my very good friends—Billie Piper and Lily James, as well as the director, Wayne Yip, British-Chinese like myself. The four of us became really fast friends, and we’re close to this day. It’s been a solace to have really solid friends from such an early stage in the industry.

A key one was in the early 2010s, when I first started going to Los Angeles for pilot season and auditioning for a whole bunch of American network shows. I got pretty close on a few, and I tested for some, but I never landed one. The whole trajectory of my life would have been different if I booked one of those jobs. I went back to the U.K., and I did more theater and more British TV. I ended up doing a show called Humans, which I absolutely loved, and I would say that was a pretty key point.

Later in my career, it’s been a little harder to find roles since turning 40. It’s part of the reason why I have loved starting to produce and being part of the creative process much earlier and finding the stories and getting to work on something like Josephine, which I also produced.

Some of them are really, really fun. They ran the whole gamut, from outright multi-cam sitcom to procedural drama. It was crazy, doing six auditions a day, driving all over town, having to memorize 10 pages for each. It was a real task. You get to the room and look around, and you’re one of however many actors sitting there, and everyone’s eyeing each other up. There was one audition that I went to and was waiting. I’d been up all night learning these lines, and I could literally hear the casting director offering the job on the phone to another actor who had been there earlier that day. I thought, “Oh my God, what am I doing?”

When I was 16, success meant losing my virginity, finding a way to stay out all night without my parents knowing, and getting a bit of distance from them. Nowadays, success looks like managing to have a good night’s sleep—getting eight hours—staying hydrated, and finding a way to spend as much time as possible with my parents. I want to spend every moment when I’m back at home in London.

There are a couple of things. They would constantly be on me about the importance of education and, eye roll, “I get it.” I am now the person who does that to my nephews, my little cousins: “Stay in school, kids!” Then, my dad had this particular way of loading the dishwasher. I’d say, “What are you talking about? There is no correct way!” I’ve discovered there is a correct way.

I was first sent the script back in 2019, and from the moment that I read it, I couldn’t get it out of my head. The truth sang from every page. I had a meeting with Beth, and she articulated her vision and what she wanted to do. I thought it was so unusual and daring and audacious. I believed in her vision. Then the pandemic happened. We were going to shoot it, and then the strikes happened. It’s been a really winding road, and it feels like a small miracle that we finally managed to get it made.

For me, the character of Claire really resonated. I’ve been fortunate enough to do so many different things in my career, but I’ve never gotten to play a role like hers. I saw a lot of myself in her. I see a lot of my own mother in her; my sister too. She has this real emotional intuition, and she’s not the loudest voice in the room, but she has the inner core of strength. She’s a survivor. Getting to play her was an amazing challenge of stripping everything back and getting to the truth of the story and the script.

It’s interesting because Claire and Damien have been raised in different ways, and they’re carrying their pasts with them. It changes how they approach parenting their daughter. I would say at the beginning of the film, they’re a unified front and haven’t really been tested. Then this thing happens where they both deeply love their daughter and want the best for her, but they don’t agree on how to do that. The first cracks start to appear. With Claire, it was about going into her background. She speaks a bit about her own relationship with her mother, about how they never spoke about difficult things and she felt a real distance from her. I’d say Claire is someone whose instincts are right for the most part, but she doesn’t always follow through on them. I had points where I wanted to shake both parents and go, “What are you doing?!” But then, I’m empathizing with them because they’re just two flawed human beings trying to do their best for their child. There is no handbook for it.

I’ve grown up and been a fan of Channing’s for a long time. If you think you know him from movies like Magic Mike and 21 Jump Street, you really don’t. What he brings to the role—the humor and the complete naturalness and spontaneity. He also brings such a level of vulnerability, and people will be really surprised by it. Before we started filming, Beth, he, and I spoke a lot about our own upbringings and our parents. He has a daughter, and that’s part of the reason why he was drawn to the script. We talked a lot about that and spent some time with Mason, who plays Josephine. We didn’t have to try; we could ease with each other. We hung out in the park. Channing and Mason got their nails done at the salon together. We went for dim sum. We did regular things, so she was comfortable. Hopefully, you believe in us as a family unit.

It’s not in my closet, sadly, but if I had to wear one outfit, it would be the pink Valentino dress I wore to the Oscars in 2019. It had pockets. It had volume. It was comfortable. I kept snacks in the pockets for the ceremony. Bury me in that dress. It’s dreamy, and I don’t know where it is.

The last thing I splurged on was a Steinway piano for myself. That’s amazing. Best decision I’ve ever made. Why didn’t I buy myself a good piano sooner? Something I will never spend money on is expensive mascara because you go through it at such a rate. You might as well just get a good drugstore mascara.

That was a dream getting to work with those women. None of us had met each other before; we had a blast between every take. We’d be in our little huddle of chairs, sharing the hot goss, going right in. It felt really, really special. They’re a brilliant bunch of women. Regina Hall made me laugh so much that I peed myself a little bit on set.

People really responded to this show about women and female friendship. There’s no murder. There’s nothing to solve in that sense, but it’s just about their lives and the day-to-day. People really relate to that, and obviously you have the aspirational element of this amazing house in Nantucket and the food porn.

Gigi is diabolical. Let’s be honest about that. Obviously, you’re not meant to judge your character, and when I read the scripts and approached it, obviously I wanted to empathize with her. Hopefully, you understand a bit of where she’s coming from. Then I watched a little bit of the show and was like, “Oh my God, she is insane for going on this weekend!” Absolutely insane.

She felt on the defensive. She did fall in love. She never wanted to. She tried to break it off. She was pursued. It takes two to tango and, sadly, the other party is deceased. She has to take the entire blame for it. I hope that she’s not villainized completely. I feel that Becca, the writer, and Erin, the author, both wanted her to be a fully dimensional human being. Although there is that secret, she has a genuine connection with these women. She genuinely grows to care about all of them, including Hollis. That is not an act. She is a complicated woman.

Celine Song, Denis Villeneuve, Chloe Dumont. They’re all so talented. I’d absolutely die to work with any of them.

That’s an easy question! I’d open a cat sanctuary and rescue all the cats that I meet. I’m about to adopt another cat from Puglia. He’s five years old, and we’re actually rescuing another kitten that my sister found abandoned. She’s coming from France next month, and we’re going to introduce them, and hopefully they’ll get along.

#6742. Plestia Alaqad Sees the World

The photojournalist talks about the making of her latest book, The Eyes of Gaza

“The hardest part was having something as intimate and personal as a diary become public,” says Plestia Alaqad of her decision to publish her diary-memoir, The Eyes of Gaza. The book details the harrowing first weeks after October 7 in Gaza as a young Palestinian journalist. Alaqad studied new media and journalism at Eastern Mediterranean University in Cyprus before returning to her home in 2022. Intending to educate the world about her city, she did not know she would end up reporting the first stages of the genocide.

Alaqad’s reporting situated herself as a voice for the ordinary. In the first few days, she spoke to her neighbors and local children. Her reporting appeared mostly on her Instagram account, which grew to a little over 2 million followers, with many of her images republished by the BBC, The New York Times, PBS NewsHour, and The Washington Post. Even through the chaos and the expulsion of over 100,000 Gazans in the first few days, according to UNHCR, which Alaqad also recounts in her memoir, there are moments of tenderness caught by her and everyday Gazans she would meet and talk to. Her style of reporting is grounded in a sense of familiarity, narrating the everyday in extraordinary circumstances. She made connections with people in a way that makes you feel like you were alongside her, drinking tea with the local tetas and jiddos, watching the violence and the world collapse around you.

Alaqad left Gaza for Lebanon, where she is completing her master’s degree. During the course of our interview, she spoke openly with me about the loneliness of the isolation of being a witness, forever changed by the devastation.

One of my favorite things about Gaza is the people and the sense of community. For example, the relationship between my neighbors and I. I’ve been living in the Al -Waleed Building since I was almost four years old, and I know all my neighbors. Like Dana, who lived next door next to me—it was like having a sister and best friend nearby at all times. Whenever I needed anything, I just knocked at Dana’s door. If I needed help picking out an outfit, if I needed help replying to messages, even if I was just bored and I wanted someone to hang out with, it was always Dana who I turned to. There’s something about Gaza that just is home to me. There is simplicity and a love for life in Gaza that I haven’t seen elsewhere in the world. So being born and raised there always meant having loving and supportive people around me.

There’s more to us than being occupied. We’re full of talent and art we never got the time to make, because we’re too busy worrying if the people we love will still be alive tomorrow. That’s what pushes me to keep writing.

I’m interested in writing and telling stories of people. I recently interviewed Renad Atallah and wrote a piece about her for The New Arab. Also, one of my favorite pieces I wrote was an interview with Alaa Abu Diab, a one-of-a-kind comedian, for The National.

As of recently, I started my newsletter on Substack, where I share a personal essay every week on Gaza, girlhood, friendship, and everything in between. My ultimate hope would be to tell stories about a free Palestine one day. And as a journalist, I’ll always see my work as incomplete without going back to Gaza and continuing to tell Palestinian stories from the ground. And hopefully work on an international level and tell stories of people who suffered because of war and conflicts in other countries.

I enjoy reading nonfiction books and memoirs because I love learning about people and their experiences. I remember reading Anne Frank’s diary in middle school.… I remember how it made me feel and the impact it had on me.

I have also always been drawn to personal writings, like Franz Kafka’s letters and the letters between Ghassan Kanafani and Ghada al-Samman. I think I’m a very curious person by nature, and I’m interested in understanding what is happening in people’s minds, and if I get the chance to read strangers’ diaries, I wouldn't say no. That curiosity is something that inspired me.

Growing up, I always had a diary. It was me and my purple diary against the world.… For me, journaling has always been my safe space in the world. It’s the one place where I can write whatever I want without feeling judged. That’s honestly why I wanted to create Neya [Alaqad’s creative brand], which is an Arabic word that means intention. The first product will be a journal, of course, and it will come in four different colors. Each journal has a small, kind reminder on the hardcover and little sweet quotes throughout the pages because I wanted to create something that is personal and safe, something you would want to come back to and share your thoughts in. For me, journaling has always been where I can reconnect with myself. I hope Neya can become that for other people too: a small space where they can pause, reflect, and reconnect with themselves.

The hardest part was having something as intimate and personal as a diary become public. Something that was once only mine suddenly was in the hands of people from all around the world. I always try to think about the impact it could have on others, and I’m grateful that this time we get to tell our own stories rather than having our stories told on our behalf. I’m also grateful for the readers around the world who have read The Eyes of Gaza and are making the effort to educate themselves about Palestine.

I often feel like I am living in two worlds. On one hand, I have the opportunity to do things that many people back home cannot access freely, such as pursuing my education and continuing my master’s degree. On the other hand, I carry the weight of knowing what is happening to my people and my land, without having any superpower to stop it.

I always try to stay honest and true to myself, and I try to take people through this journey with me online. I share the contradictions, the pain, and the moments of hope. What it’s like trying to process the experience of having your homeland bombed, your city erased, and your people murdered, while also surviving and trying to make sense of the world outside. It is something that is difficult to put into words.

It feels like time has frozen in Gaza, while the rest of the world continues moving forward. And for those of us away from home, we are constantly trying to live between these two realities.

Gaza is a place where contradictions exist side by side. You see unimaginable grief, but you also see people trying to find meaning and strength in the middle of that suffering.

The Eyes of Gaza captures these contradictions. On one page, sadness may take over, while on another, hope emerges. Ultimately, I believe that when people experience powerful emotions, they are often moved to take action.

#6741. From the Archive: Ina Garten Tapped a Few Famous Friends for Her '90s Farmhouse Renovation

Take a look inside the sunny, five-room farmhouse.

Welcome to Archive Dive—my new excuse to obsess over the absolute treasures hiding in House Beautiful’s 130-year-old archives. Every month, we’ll dig up a favorite story from the past (epic room makeovers, wildly bold design swings, and style moments that were way ahead of their time) and talk about why they still resonate today. It’s part history lesson, part design deep-dive, and 100 percent a love letter to the iconic taste that’s defined HB for over a century.—Jo Saltz, House Beautiful Editor in Chief

After Ina Garten moved the now-shuttered Barefoot Contessa specialty store from Westhampton to East Hampton, she and her husband, Jeffrey, decided to put down roots in the neighborhood. But rather than buy a new-build home, Garten transformed an old farmhouse into a light-filled, five-room retreat designed for hosting—and feeding—guests.

In our latest Archive Dive, we revisit the 1994 renovation, which includes Garten’s search for the right paint color, her no-nonsense decorating approach, and her love of the property’s grounds. She took each project in stride with help from famous friends like Martha Stewart and the late interior designer Robert Currie—and Jeffrey, of course. The result was a relaxed home with an entertaining-ready atmosphere and a garden that would make any green-thumbed foodie proud. Read on for the original story.

With the help of Robert Currie, the owner of a fabulous food emporium in the Hamptons planned her nearby house for the casual ease of the legions of guests she feeds in her spare time.

Are you full of self-doubt in the dark of the night? Should you buy that old house? Should you change your career? Then follow in Ina Garten’s (bare) footsteps. She has done both with such breathtaking speed and success that the average worrier blinks in awe. She bought a Westhampton, New York, specialty store (oddly shaped breads, ravishing vegetables, seafood salad to go) from a business opportunities ad practically on the spot.

“I didn’t know how to slice smoked salmon or tell whether the Brie was ripe,” she confesses. But in no time, she turned her emporium, Barefoot Contessa, into a foodie’s fairyland.

After Garten moved the store to East Hampton she converted a cramped, run-down old farmhouse in the village into an airy five-room refuge and party venue for herself and her husband. “I just trusted that if the house had good ‘bones,’ I could figure it all out,” she laughs. No insomnia. No agonies. And, incredibly, no mistakes.

She’s a nurturer, too, a definite Earth Mother, which is certainly why she fled her career in nuclear energy policy planning for the far more congenial field of food. Her personal policy is summed up simply: lots of entertaining.

When her husband—a former Columbia professor, author, and investment banker—worked in New York, she encouraged his uptight fellow bankers to loosen their ties and shed their jackets by dishing up platters of fried chicken and other finger foods. In East Hampton she gives Sunday afternoon parties with plenty of easy food “like crabcake sandwiches,” and leaves all the doors open so guests will feel free to wander out and eat on the porch, or anywhere else that seems inviting.

She often serves in her sunny kitchen, which contains the requisite serious fridge and stove, but lacks the usual array of self-important utensils. This is a kitchen with nothing to prove.

The toughest decisions of her fix-up were the wall colors. She thought at first that she might like the whole house vanilla (a true foodie’s choice), but test patches were too white or yellow.

“I had samples all over the walls, and the painter was yelling ‘Stop already’,” but finally she hit on a toasty khaki for her bedroom. She was still stuck for the first floor when she spotted the bedroom primer paint: khaki halved with white. A mental lightbulb turned on, and the painter went to work. The house is now two values of that changeable shade with browny greens in the upholstery derived from the tapestry-covered living room sofa.

There is not a stick of unnecessary furniture in the farmhouse either, just several old oversize tables, a few pieces of portly upholstery, and in the bedroom, a brass bed (of which a friend said, “That’s not a bed, it’s a temple”), plus two chairs. No windows are curtained.

“Everything,” Garten feels, “should do something, have a function, not just sit there. I like things to be spare.” And bare as well, like her new floors, which are crafted of newly purchased old wood. Are the original floors still underneath? Nope. With a sanding and a coat of polyurethane, they are reincarnated as a kitchen countertop.

Garten actually bought the house for its grounds, because she yearned to garden. She had lived for a time in rented houses, but when she found herself planting expensive trees in rented lawns, she realized it was time to own some land.

Outside her kitchen doors she has cut square beds for tomatoes, which mix quite cheerfully with dahlias, and for roses knee-deep in catmint. She likes to combine “mess with structure,” but her structures are always imaginative. Instead of a single stake for each tomato plant, for instance, she does a row of tepees. Or she lines her disheveled strawberry beds with a spine of iris.

A Manhattan florist friend, Tom Pritchard of Pure Mädderlake, helped her with the garden design and did a very clever thing. He took two chairs out into the garden and walked around with her, scoping out the best seating spots. That’s how she decided which vistas to emphasize and which had too fine a view of the neighbor’s patio. Gardening has become a passion since she moved here. Every surface in her house is piled with well-thumbed horticultural tomes; for the time being, her food banks are relegated to the bottom of the pile.

Garten acknowledges that she has had a lot of help. Her good friend, the late Robert Currie, designed all the seating, shopped with her, and arranged the furniture. He also taught her to avoid like mad “anything that was too trendy.” Stephen Scanniello, the rose dean at the Brooklyn Botanic Garden, chose a hundred great roses for her, and Martha Stewart passed judgment on her new shower stall. No less helpful was her very supportive husband.

But husbands don’t ponder that midnight ceiling with you. And talented friends aren’t around at 2 a.m., when the real bullets are bitten. Some lucky people, though, do it all with ease—Ina Garten for one. Warm and comfy, like her own new house, she just turns over and goes back to sleep.

#6736. We Really Need to Talk About the MobLand Season 2 Finale

A certain character’s fate hangs in the balance.

Nothing is simple in MobLand. Two seasons in, the plot has racked up more lines than Eddie put up his nose the night he stabbed Tommy Stevenson in season 1. Actually, that’s not strictly true. One thing is simple in MobLand: Eddie. He is a simpleton of truly Homeric (as in Simpson) proportions: a smarmy, sneering, simpering Freudian mess, channelled with gimlet-eyed precision by Anson Boon. All he wants is his God-given right to be respected.

Trouble is, when you’re an anti-Midas who turns everything he touches to shit, respect is a dish that never leaves the kitchen. Eddie is, in Kevin’s words in season 1, “a knifey little twerp.” That stabbing set the whole bloody saga in motion, so it’s only right that two seasons later we end with Eddie the Incompetent. Full circle, then, like a roundabout made of corpses.

MobLand opened with Eddie turning a stabbing into a gang war, so it seems fitting that we end with Eddie turning an assassination into an own goal. But we’ll get to that.

It seems Conrad’s chickens are coming home to roost, and his marbles are jangling around his head like loose change. We open the episode with his card being declined at a petrol station. In frustration, he shoots the till boy dead. But he knows what it means.

“We’re getting lifted,” he tells Maeve in the car. Police are raiding the entire business: bank accounts are frozen, warrants are out, and Tattersall is telling reporters the Harrigans have links to terror groups across the globe. The family convenes at a dingy old safe house out of town.

“We’re terrorists,” says Seraphina. “We’re looking at life in Guantánamo. It’s now or never.”

Her plan has been bubbling for a while. She calls it Harrigan 2.0: going global.

Remember the diplomatic suitcase of diamonds Seraphina collected from her billionaire stepdad Charlton in the last episode? It was meant to fund her and Bella’s takeover of the Harrigan operation. Now, it seems, Harry has it. He takes her aside to let her know he knows. “How did you know?” she asks, incredulous.

Then he says the three words he says more than any others in MobLand: “It’s my job.”

Inside the safe house, the family are all there, and everyone has a plan to pitch. Kevin wants to crawl back to Kat and hand over Conrad, Maeve, and Eddie. “Clean the house,” he growls to Harry, out of earshot of the others.

Conrad, meanwhile, isn’t sure what to do. “What are our options?” he asks Harry.

“We don’t have any,” comes the terse reply. “Either we run seven ways and it’s every man and woman for themselves, or we go back to Kat and try to strike some kind of deal.”

Kevin proposes offering her 50 percent and first working out who’s a liability. “And right now I’m seeing a lot of liabilities. Right, Harry?”

Bella has had it up to ’ere with Kevin’s pusillanimity. “Oh, you nearly got to the end of a sentence without saying ‘Harry,’” she taunts. “I know he saved you from a bumming once but… Jesus.”

Kevin pulls a pistol and fires, missing her ear by millimeters. Conrad draws. “Put the gun down, boy,” he hisses. Bella steps forward. “Go on then, big boy.”

You could cut the tension with piano wire.

“Fuck this, I’m getting us out of this mess,” says Seraphina, defusing the standoff by fetching a fistful of diamonds from the car. “Fifteen Type IIa brilliants. North of ten million euros,” she tells Conrad, placing them in his hand.

The plan: offer Kat a cut of the empire. “This time tomorrow we’re back in business.”

Remember that glorious, long-winded speech Conrad gave in Spain about the good old days, how he came from nothing, and how Maeve has been his compass through the carnage of his life? “We were born outlaws and we’ll die outlaws,” he bellowed.

Well, now he knows the jig is up. Looking balefully at the floor, he grunts: “Do it.”

So how will Harry play this? He slips out to his 4x4 and rings Kat. “At 5 p.m. I will meet your diplomatic courier. I will pass to said courier €10 million in untraceable diamonds. Upon authentication, I will give you the location of the Harrigan family.”

A double cross? It must be a ruse. He simply MUST have a plan… Then, hanging up, he mutters: “Fuck this, I’m done.”

He returns to the family and lies: “Kat will take the ten million and 50 percent.” Too easy by half.

As Frankie put it two episodes ago: “Is that it? Unless you’ve got some brilliant plans, that is actually a bit shit. What are you up to, Harry?”

We’re more than halfway through and haven’t heard a peep from Frankie, our laconic, crocodilian villain. Ah, but here he is, getting the news of Harry’s volte-face from Tattersall. “How do we know it’s not a bluff?”

“He’s not stupid,” says Tattersall. “He knows when the game’s up.”

“Well, you know me,” leers Frankie. “I can’t resist a gamble.” Guns are grabbed from car boots, and off he pootles with a gaggle of tooled-up goons to send the Harrigans back to Hell.

It’s all unfolding very quickly now, as denouements always should. Back at the safe house, Conrad is still seeing the dead, including the petrol station lad he just murdered. Maeve says he’s been saying their names in his sleep.

The old loon’s marbles are now departing his head like passengers from a cancelled flight. But there’s one thing that’ll straighten any man out: a black pearl of Maeve wisdom. “We’ve been stuck up tighter holes than Kat McAllister’s,” she reassures him. “But when you’re stuck up someone’s hole, there’s at least two ways out.”

Quite so. And who better to lead the way than Harry? As ever, he picks the exit that leads straight back to where he came in. Unbeknownst to us, he has gone up Kat’s backside, rummaged about, and come out the other end holding her entire career. The “betrayal” was a ruse from the start.

So when Frankie’s goons arrive armed to the teeth, it’s a trap: flash grenades, smoke grenades, poison gas, and Zosia on the exit with a machine gun for anyone daft enough to run. “Oh, Harry,” concedes Frankie, before a mighty gunfight erupts and Harry tears through the house like Keyser Söze in a gas mask. “So this isn’t the beginning of a beautiful friendship,” smiles Frankie, choking in the corner. Harry shoots him in the knee.

Then Harry rings Kat. Those “untraceable” diamonds? Very traceable indeed, straight to a terrorist organization. He has bribed a low-ranking diplomatic courier to deliver them along with a “little present, just for you.”

She opens the box, ashen-faced. It’s Donnie’s blue and rotting head. “This one you might find harder to justify,” he tells her.

“My family are gonna hunt you down and feed you your balls,” she spits.

“Probably,” says Harry. “But not before they feed you yours.”

Checkmate, Kat. The black queen has no more squares to go.

But wait … Harry can offer her one square. “I can fix this,” he says.

Here’s what she’s going to do. She’ll resign. She’ll kill the police investigation. The Harrigans’ accounts will reopen and they’ll come off the terror list.

Cut to a London corridor of power, where a faceless civil servant informs DS Kavanagh that “continuation of your terrorism financing line will interfere with an active joint intelligence operation between British and American security services. You will stand down at the close of business.”

And so, full circle. Maeve is electrocuting Frankie’s balls with a car battery. Kevin is building bridges with Bella. Conrad is going madder by the day. And Eddie?

The Semtex he blackmailed Paul for was meant for Harry, at Maeve’s behest. In the hands of any other Harrigan, this would be a doddle. But not Eddie. Not the knifey little twerp, a man who could trip over his own foreskin in an empty car park.

And he does. But first, the hints. Something strange has been going on with Jan all episode: the soppy Damien Rice soundtrack, the wistful dream sequences of the good times she shared with Harry. Her Harry. The one she fell for in the first place. She seems to have forgiven him. They’re even taking a family holiday.

But there’s one last stop before they go: Harrigan HQ, where Eddie is lying in wait. While Harry negotiates the terms of the holiday with Conrad, Gina and Jan stroll over to the lake. It’s Eddie’s moment, his chance to shine. He plants the Semtex in their car, hides around the corner, arms the bomb, finger hovering over the detonator … then a phone call.

By Finn McCool’s cobblers, it’s Kelly “Mad Dog” O’Connor, the lunatic son of the Medusa, whom Maeve sent Eddie to kill. “I’m coming for you,” growls the voice. “The Mad Dog is coming.”

Gina, Jan and Harry return to the car. Only Jan gets in. In his quivering, fearful state, Eddie trips and drops the detonator.

That’s Jan up in smoke, along with Harry’s plans for a quiet life. It may spell the end of Jan and all her problems, but it’s only the beginning of Eddie’s. He now has Mad Dog O’Connor to answer to, and Harry.

Then the credits roll, and what a gloriously cruel place to leave it.

The only other loose end is Frankie. We’re left to assume he met a long, painful death at the business end of Maeve’s car battery, but we never see a body. I truly hope he does a Harry and wriggles out of one last tight hole—Maeve’s hole—with one good knee and a long memory.

Season 3 can’t come soon enough. When Harry works out whose finger was on the detonator, Eddie’ll wish a knifey little twerp is all he could be.

#6733. James Bond’s Favorite Luggage Brand Has Broken Its Own Rules

Globe-Trotter has spent more than a century perfecting its signature suitcase. Now it's trying something different.

James Bond has always travelled in style. And while Daniel Craig's 007 was most immediately associated with his Aston Martins, Omega watches, and Tom Ford suits, his luggage of choice was Globe-Trotter, the Hertfordshire-based manufacturer whose cases have also found favor with everyone from Queen Elizabeth II to Kate Moss.

Now the company has done something completely left-field. After 129 years of making essentially the same kind of suitcase, it has changed the formula.

The new GT897 is Globe-Trotter's lightest case yet, and a significant departure from the design that made the brand famous. Out go the traditional rigid frame and locks, replaced by a zip-around opening and softer, more streamlined silhouette.

That said, some familiar details keep it firmly on-brand: the vulcanized fiberboard, leather trim, nickel hardware, and those trademark exposed rivets. And the cases are still handcrafted in Hertfordshire.

Currently available in two color combinations—bland-and-black and green-and-brown—the GT897 is priced at $1,395. A black-and-oxblood version follows in February.

It's an interesting move for a company whose USP has always rested somewhat on its refusal to change. Whether the new James Bond will follow Craig's lead remains to be seen. But at least there's now a new option for packing his tuxedo.

#6731. Michelle Obama, Hillary Clinton, and Jill Biden Come Together at Gloria Steinem’s Joyous Celebration of Life

The former first ladies coordinated in elegant, high-fashion ensembles to honor the feminist activist

Gloria Steinem’s life, work, and legacy were celebrated this week, as many gathered at a memorial service for the feminist activist in New York City.

The celebration of life took place at the Park Avenue Armory and came two months after Steinem’s passing at age 92.

Among those who came together to pay their respects were former United States first ladies Michelle Obama, Hillary Clinton, and Jill Biden—as well as Chelsea Clinton, Diane von Furstenberg, Meg Ryan, Lucy Liu, Emma Watson, Marisa Tomei, and Mariska Hargitay. In total, there were about 1,000 invited guests, all who were encouraged to donate to Gloria’s Foundation, which aims to preserve Steinem’s Upper East Side brownstone on East 73rd Street as a permanent archive and center for feminist organizing.

Rather than a somber service, the memorial was a joyful, music-filled celebration titled “It’s Steinemite!” Cynthia Erivo sang John Lennon’s “Imagine,” Cyndi Lauper performed “Girls Just Want to Have Fun,” and Kathy Sledge of Sister Sledge closed the event with “We Are Family,” joined by Lauper and Gloria’s Choir.

For the evening, attendees coordinated in all-black ensembles—but it certainly wasn’t your average funeral attire. Clinton wore an elegant pantsuit—one of her favored looks. Obama was so chic and cool in a cropped blazer and a pleated skirt with a silky sash and golden accents. And Biden went for a fun, high-fashion ensemble that included Schiaparelli’s black Corsini suit jacket with asymmetric gold buttons.

If she could’ve looked into that crowd of incredible women, Steinem would’ve been endlessly proud.

#6728. Designers Say This ’70s-Inspired Design Trend Is Making a Glamorous Comeback

Get ready to see this glossy finish everywhere again.

We all know design is reflective. Whether it’s in our personal style, architectural splendor, or cultural ties, great design mirrors the world around us. Now, it’s time for it to mirror itself.

Once dismissed as a tacky, ’70s-era trend (not unlike wood paneling), mirrored surfaces are making a comeback. But don’t worry—today’s mirror craze moves past the floor-to-ceiling panels of the past, opting instead for accents that add light, depth, and dimension.

“I love mirrored surfaces in interiors because they can be so transformative & work in all styles. They can add glitz and glamour, especially when you add beveled details,” says Bridget Tiek of TIEK BYDAY. “I love to see mirrors as a backbar, behind shelves, on doors, ceilings and on walls. Basically everywhere!”

Bolder than traditional ready-to-hang mirrors, mirrored surfaces are often hard to place tastefully. Here’s how designers have brought the striking detail into interiors.

Queer Eye for the Straight Guy star Carson Kressley’s Murray Hill apartment is full of personality, and his sunken living room has several glittering surfaces. The designer embraces mirrored shine as a bold accent, as seen in this disco-ball-tiled end table.

Designer Alexander D. Wilson brought a mirrored backsplash into this Upper East Side apartment to elevate the elegant yet fun palette while creating the illusion of a larger space. NYC apartments often feel like a shoebox. With this classic design trick, they don’t have to look like it.

Interior designer Sarah Vaile brought Manhattan to the Windy City in this punchy coastal home. After coating the walls in Farrow & Ball’s Borrowed Light for a classic Nancy Meyers-esque aesthetic, she framed a nearly ceiling-high mirror in crisp white molding, transforming the panel into a bold decorative piece that amplifies space and cosmopolitan chic.

Bold enough to make a fierce statement in even the smallest piece, interior designer Amy Courtney brought industrial flair to her New York City home with a silvery domed lamp to complement the bold hues of her living room’s palette.

While the glassy, expansive nature of mirrored panels certainly has its charms, if you’re hoping to bring both glossy and textured flair to your design, mirrored tiles are the perfect solution. Designer Shannon Ggem used mirrored tiles in this moody bar design to bring more light to the room without sacrificing the deep-hued palette.

While most rely on millwork to bring style to the walls, designer Michelle Prentice opted for mirror panels on the closet doors of this South Carolina bedroom. Not only do they add depth to the design, but they bring a boutique feel while adding to the visual palette and playing off the centered vanity mirror to unify the wall treatments.

As stainless steel fades from an industry standard to a passing fad, designers are getting creative when it comes to finding chic alternatives. In this monochrome green butler’s pantry, designer Bridget Tiek brought in a glassy paneled door to elevate the appliance design, taking the invisible kitchen trend to new heights.

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.
