#6725. I'm a Mom of Two, and This Ernesta Rug Gives Me That Luxe Look With the Easy Cleanup of a Washable Option

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

I have to admit, I have a bit of a gripe with washable rugs. For years, the design industry has been trying to convince us that the answer to a beautiful, livable home is a washable rug. And while I understand the appeal, especially as a mom to two young kids, a dog, and a household that sees its fair share of spills, muddy shoes, and general chaos, I’ve just never been entirely sold on the idea.

While washable rug options have definitely improved over the years, there’s still something about the weight, texture, and character of a traditional rug that I find hard to forgo for the sake of washability. I want a rug that feels substantial underfoot, looks better with age, and doesn’t require me to wrestle an eight-by-ten-foot piece of fabric into my washing machine every time someone spills a juice box.

When I learned about Ernesta’s protective rug treatment, I was intrigued. The custom rug brand offers an optional treatment designed to help repel spills and make everyday messes easier to clean, without requiring you to compromise on your rug’s style, material, or size. In theory, it offers the best of both worlds: the look and feel of a beautifully made rug, with a little extra insurance against the realities of family life. Plus, it’s a treatment that’s typically hard to access on your own without going custom through a designer.

I actually have several Ernesta rugs in my home (I’m a huge fan of their quality and approachable customization options!), but after swapping in an Ernesta rug treated with the brand’s protective finish in our living room, I don’t think I’ll ever go back to ordering a rug without it. Here’s what you should know about the treatment, how it's held up in my own home, and why I think it may be a smarter investment than yet another washable rug.

Best for: This rug is an excellent option for families, pet owners, or anyone who just prefers not to have to be too precious with their things.

Not ideal for: Anyone looking for a quick-ship rug option.

Bottom Line: The customization of Ernesta’s rug assortment makes it easy to get the exact look and function that serves your home best, including different sizes, non-slip bases, and protections.

One of the reasons I love Ernesta so much is how approachable they make the ordering process. There’s no visiting a showroom or making 400 phone calls to try to get the right custom-cut size. You simply log on to their site, browse the options, then choose up to five samples to try for just $10.

My samples came incredibly quickly—within a few days, they were at my doorstep, ready for review. Because I already liked the look of the rug we had in our living room (more on that later), I knew I didn’t want to stray too far from the neutral, functional foundation it gave us.

When we started, we had Ernesta’s Emerson sisal rug in the space. The nubby texture and color looked beautiful, but it was a bit small (it was originally sized to an old home that we have since moved from), and the deeper grooves of the weave made it hard to clean up spills. I have two young kids and a dog, and truly not a day goes by where someone hasn’t spilled yogurt or cracker crumbs or juice on the rug.

Still, I don’t think you should have to sacrifice beauty for function in a family home, and Ernesta helped us find that balance. After debating between a few different styles, I ended up settling on the Cally wool flatweave rug in Camel. Wool flatweave rugs are notoriously durable on their own, and I knew the protective coating would only help it hold up better. Bonus: The tighter weave meant fewer nooks and crannies for gunk to get into.

Once we decided on our new style, we measured out the size we’d need, which ended up being around 13 x 15 feet. I loved how easy it was to order the exact size we needed for our space, especially given that it wasn’t a standard measurement. If you’re unsure about the right size for your rug (or how many legs of your furniture should be on the rug at one time), Ernesta has helpful guidance on their site.

Placing the order was easy; you can opt into the protective treatment before adding the order to your cart, as well as choose other details, like the type of edge binding and whether you’d like to include a rug pad (we chose the attached version).

Protective treatments for rugs are nothing new; they’ve been available to design industry insiders for years. That said, very few DTC companies offer the treatment, unless you’re buying through a designer or want to seek out a supplier on your own.

That’s what’s so great about Ernesta’s offerings—it’s literally as easy as checking a box before you add your rug to cart. The treatment starts at $62 and increases depending on the size of your rug. Their formula is non-toxic and works by acting as a protective layer between the surface of the rug and any spills or debris, preventing stains from setting in and keeping the fibers intact for longer.

Receiving and installing our Ernesta rug couldn’t have been easier, especially considering its large size. It was delivered and placed in our entryway, where it waited for a few days until my husband and I had a spare moment to lay it out.

The rug was packaged beautifully, with thick plastic wrapping protecting the roll, a firm cardboard tube at the center, and fabric wrapping the edges so the binding didn’t get roughed up in transit. I thought this was a particularly nice touch.

As with most rugs, the corners were a bit flared when we first unrolled it, so here’s my favorite tip: add several ice cubes to any corner you want to lay flat and let them melt. The slow dissipation of moisture will relax the rug's fibers, and by the time it has melted away completely, you should have a totally flat rug.

I was immediately impressed by the look and feel of this rug—the wool was so soft and plush, and the rubber mat integrated on the bottom meant it did not budge once we laid it down (though it did make it difficult to reposition when we laid it a little wonky initially, word to the wise). There was no “chemical” smell from the protective treatments, and the fibers still felt just as soft as they did on the sample I received before ordering.

Aesthetically, I love the vibe that this rug brings to the space. It’s soft and plush and cozy, and finally in better proportion with the rest of the room—which is great, because I have a pair of armchairs I won at auction waiting in the wings for their moment. Before, they were floating off the rug and looked odd, but now, they’ll be perfect.

Logically, the protective treatment was 100 percent the right choice for our family. Not even five minutes after laying it out, my three-year-old squished an applesauce pouch on the rug, and it came up with ease. In the month since having it, we’ve definitely put this rug through the paces of family life, and it still looks as good as the day we opened it. Cleaning it is easy, and Ernesta has a whole page on their site dedicated to care instructions, depending on the material your rug is made of.

Overall, this is the ideal solution to me: a rug that looks as good as it performs, and doesn’t require any design sacrifice (or yelling from mom) in the process.

#6723. Miley Cyrus and Maxx Morando’s Complete Relationship Timeline

Miley Cyrus can buy herself flowers, but we have a feeling that her beau, Maxx Morando, is buying them for her.

Since 2021, the Disney Channel alum has been linked to Morando, a fellow musician who is perhaps best known as being the drummer for the rock band Liily. He was also previously part of another group, called the Regrettes. In 2022, Lilly was invited to play on Cyrus’s TV special, Miley’s New Year’s Eve Party.

Throughout their years-long relationship, the two have preferred to keep their romance largely out of the spotlight. But Cyrus offered a rare glimpse into their life together during her interview for the Harper’s Bazaar’s December 2024/January 2025 cover story. “He’s very similar to me,” she said. “We just don’t take life too seriously.”

Throughout their time together, they’ve also collaborated numerous times in the studio, with Morando serving as a producer and songwriter on Cyrus’s Something Beautiful and Bass Persuades records. In her interview with Bazaar, Cyrus revealed that music was actually how she and Morando first got to know each other. “I’ve always worked with the people that I love,” Cyrus said. “And Maxx just inspires me so much.”

Now, the pair are engaged and reported to be getting married this weekend with an intimate ceremony in Southern California. However, other details are being kept under wraps—for now.

If you’re wondering what else there is to know about Cyrus and Morando’s low-key romance, then look no further. Below, we’ve tracked the couple’s entire relationship from start to finish.

For Cyrus, it was love at first sight, but for Morando, he already had an idea of who he was meeting long before the date even happened.

“We got put on a blind date,” Cyrus reveals in an interview with British Vogue years after the fact, in May 2023. “Well, it was blind for me and not really for him. I thought, ‘The worst that can happen is I leave.’ ”

When he calls mid-interview, her phone starts playing “Tyrone” by Erykah Badu. “I call that ‘boyfriend sexy ringtone,’ ” she says, smiling.

Shane used existing pieces I’ve collected over time and customized Maxx’s cartoon characters [within] his construction. Their collaboration is the perfect example of sustainability becoming the next fashion phase, and it proves that anything can be reinvented, which resonates with me deeply.”

“He’s very similar to me. We just don’t take life too seriously,” she says.

#6717. The 5 Types of Coats Every Guy Should Own

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

A great winter outfit starts with the right outerwear.

Years ago, I was talking to a very menswear-y buddy at a very menswear-y event, and he proposed a theory of outerwear that’s stuck with me: Your coat is your car. Sounds pretty … menswear-y, right? But bear with me here, because I think it actually makes perfect sense, so long as you’re willing to make a couple (major) concessions.

No, your coat cannot help you travel at 75 miles per hour down an interstate. You cannot fill it up with gasoline or top it up at a charging station. It contains, ideally, zero percent steel.

But if you live in a place where you spend more time walking or taking public transit than you do behind the wheel, your coat does indeed become quite car-like. You ensconce yourself in it when you move about the world. It’s the first thing people see when you arrive and the last thing they see when you depart. It shields you from the elements while simultaneously signaling to friends and strangers alike your combined senses of utility and aesthetics. So, yeah, if you squint and look at the situation sideways, your coat is your car. Even if you live somewhere that demands personal automotive transportation, your coat is your second car—unless you’re one of those kooky types who just dart from the interior of a sedan to the interior of a restaurant in nothing but a sweater—for all the reasons stated above.

The point is: Your coat is the most visible and functional part of your winter wardrobe, and you should approach the buying and wearing of said coat with all due respect for its position. Which is exactly why I created this guide. We’ll run through the key styles that every guy should know, where to get a great example (or three!) of each, and how to wear them with style all season long. You won’t find truckers or bombers here—those are jackets—but you will find parkas, topcoats, trenches, and more. All of them will serve you well until the spring thaw, so let’s get into it.

If you’ve been keeping up with Esquire style guides, you’ll know that we’ve covered a lot of ground over the past few years, breaking down everything from the shoe styles you should have in your rotation to the winter essentials you shouldn’t go without. One story that really resonated with readers was about sweaters. In it, we talked about the fact that you can score very good knitwear from a huge range of lifestyle brands these days. We also mentioned the prudence of keeping a few category specialists on your radar. The same advice holds true for coats.

On the lifestyle side, the brands will look familiar to longtime readers. You’ve got Todd Synder and Buck Mason. J. Press and J. Crew. Ralph Lauren and Drake’s and Alpha Industries and Madewell and Billy Reid and Filson ... the list goes on. You’ve also got fashion behemoths like Burberry, which famously started out with the trench coat. And then you’ve got brands that may not just do outerwear but are truly steeped in it. That could be a heritage player like Mackintosh or Schott or Gloverall, a parka expert like Canada Goose, or an upstart label like Wythe, which makes a wonderful range of products but has a cult following for its exceptional overcoats.

As we’ve warned before: This list is not exhaustive. But it’s a good place to start. Now, on to the coats themselves.

This is the dressiest of the bunch, though it wasn’t always. If you’re a real traditionalist, you might have a velvet-collared Chesterfield in your closet to wear with your fanciest togs. Most of us, though, don’t really need something that formal. The topcoat is similar to and derives from the Chesterfield, but it’s less stuffy.

Still, it looks plenty dressed up to modern eyes. It’ll have lapels, which can be notch or peak (for a little extra personality), and can be single- or double-breasted. If you suit up with any regularity, that means it’s likely the most essential for your wardrobe. Look for one made of wool or a wool blend; this is not the time to overspend on synthetics.

Colors are usually pretty conservative—think camel, navy, gray, and black. That might seem limiting, but remember that this thing should be able to play nicely with your whole wardrobe, so opting for a more subdued hue is usually the right move. As for length: Some folks will swear something that hits above the knee looks more modern. We just think it looks off. Go for at or below the knee.

(Oh, and if you’re wondering if a classic polo coat really counts as a topcoat as opposed to an overcoat, well, you might be right to question us. Technically. But considering its cultural associations—rich New Englanders, Wall Street power brokers—we’re giving it a home here.)

Eschewed by many as a bit too fusty and old-fashioned as recently as a decade ago, the raglan overcoat has come roaring back into the spotlight in recent years. We enthusiastically endorse its return.

The style is defined by its raglan shoulder construction, in which a diagonal seam runs from around the collarbone to the underarm, allowing for an uninterrupted panel over the top of the shoulder. Don’t worry too much about the technical details, though. The main thing to remember is that this shape is more relaxed and tends to layer over other garments (a chunky sweater, a sport coat) more easily and comfortably than a traditional “set in” shoulder seam.

The raglan overcoat also tends to be cut wide and long, so you can bundle up underneath it and never worry about chilly knees. It leans more casual than a topcoat, for sure, but you can still wear it with a suit. And it lends polish to something as simple as jeans and a sweatshirt. One of our favorite subgenres of raglan overcoat is the balmacaan, which features a Prussian collar (which looks like a shirt collar instead of a lapel), a “fly front” with a placket that covers the buttons, and buttoned fasteners at the wrists so you can cinch up to keep out the cold.

Wythe Raglan Overcoat in Diamond Ombre Tweed

If you’re into the warm and wooly vibes of the two styles above but simply can’t stand a long coat, the peacoat is for you. Double-breasted and hip length, it’s a shorter—but no less capable—take on the woolen overcoat that dates back about three centuries.

Originally created for sailors of European (and, eventually, American) navies, the peacoat is tough enough to defend from open-ocean winds and sea spray, which means it’s definitely up to the task of keeping you toasty on your way to work. There are a lot of versions out there these days that are done up in frankly wimpy wool blends that might feel nice at first but don’t do the job.

Go for a heavyweight wool like melton, but make sure there’s enough room to layer up underneath it—and keep an eye on the lapels. They should be big. Big enough that you can pop that collar and shield yourself from an incoming gust without feeling even a tickle of wind touch your neck. Plus, it looks cool.

J. Crew Dock Peacoat in Wool Blend with PrimaLoft

Thanks to a quirky little phenomenon we like to call “global climate change,” there’s a very good chance that some of the winter days ahead will feel a lot more like fall or spring. On those days—especially if it’s raining—you should reach for a trench coat or a mac. The two styles aren’t synonymous, but they’re related in both vibe and history, so we’re coupling them here.

Maximalists will prefer the trench, with its storm flap and epaulettes and double-breasted closure. Minimalists will reach for the mac, with its clean lines and streamlined shape. It’s really a matter of preference, because both fulfill the same roles in your outerwear lineup.

They’ll keep you warmish, especially when combined with additional layers. They’ll help block the wind when it kicks up. And although we can’t guarantee you’ll walk away dry if you stand around for an hour during a deluge, they’ll fend off a shower—or a sprint through a thunderstorm—quite capably.

Burberry Long Kensington Heritage Trench Coat

If it’s really fucking frigid, you need a parka. Make sure it’s properly insulated, whether with a synthetic fill or down. And unless you like freezing your literal ass off, get one that’s hip length or longer. You could get something technical and bright that looks like it just stepped off the ski slopes, but we’re more inclined toward rugged-looking styles that take their cues from the military and outdoor exploration.

The N-3B, or “snorkel parka,” is a burly beast that looks absolutely fantastic with a casual outfit and, if you ask us, can be fun with a suit too. (Though if you’re worried about raising eyebrows or breaking a dress code, maybe opt for a topcoat instead.) Something like Canada Goose’s Langdon parka is a little more pared back in looks but overdelivers on warmth, meaning you’ll be cozy in even the worst weather. The trick is finding one that balances form and function so that it looks as though you intended to wear it on the way to the bar—not like you wandered off some snowcapped mountain and forgot to change.

Like the peacoat, the duffle coat is a part of maritime history. It was adopted by the British Royal Navy in the late 19th century and widely issued to British troops during World War II. Thanks to the sheer number produced and postwar surplus, it quickly became a civilian staple and eventually hopped across the pond to become a fixture of the American preppy uniform. Made of thick wool and equipped with a big hood, it’s very warm. And thanks to the large toggle closures, you don’t even need to take your gloves off to fasten it. Is it a little odd? Perhaps. But that’s what makes it so charming.

J. Press Made-in-England Classic Morris Duffle Coat

Gloverall Men’s Original Monty Duffle Coat

#6713. The Watches of Grand Theft Auto 6 (and Their Real-Life Inspiration)

From Rolex Day-Dates to Casio digitals, a guide to the timepieces of Vice City.

For one glorious moment, the world appears to be united. Grand Theft Auto VI has finally arrived, to near-universal acclaim, with millions of people abandoning work, school, and presumably all their other responsibilities to explore the streets of Vice City. In these divided times, it's enough to warm the cockles of your heart.

And amid all the fast cars, guns, nightclubs, and criminal capers, there's something else we pay attention to here at Esquire HQ: the watches!

Rockstar Games' latest blockbuster reportedly features 34 "collectible" luxury watches scattered throughout its fictional version of Florida. But that's only part of the story. GTA VI also has its own watch brands, although some of the designs look ... suspiciously familiar.

There's Crowex (a dead ringer for Rolex), Takio (distinctly Casio-adjacent), and Covgari, whose watches owe more to Audemars Piguet than the Italian-o jeweller its name suggests.

From gold Day-Dates to pocket-money Casio digitals, here are some of our favorite real-world watches that appear to have inspired the game's timepieces.

The gold Crowex is a near dead ringer for the Rolex Day-Date 40 (ref. 228238). The fluted bezel, black dial, and day window at 12 o'clock are all present and correct, although the bracelet differs somewhat from Rolex's iconic President. The Day-Date celebrates its 70th anniversary this year. Evidently, it remains the universal symbol of having made it, even in the virtual world.

The silver chronograph worn by a character overlooking Vice City has more than a passing resemblance to the mighty Daytona. The round case, contrasting sub-dials, and metal bracelet suggest that famous racing chronograph. As we know, the Daytona is one of the world's most sought-after sports watches, making it a natural fit for GTA's world of expensive cars, conspicuous consumption, and bling.

Brian Heder, a veteran drug smuggler, wears a metal sports watch that certainly seems to recall Rolex's Sub. There's the substantial case, dark dial, and the bracelet. The Submariner has been a fixture of luxury watchmaking since 1953, and its more ostentatious steel-and-gold versions would be right at home on the streets of Vice City.

Jason Duval, one of GTA VI's two protagonists, wears a chunky digital watch resembling something from Casio's Pro Trek range. The large circular display, prominent buttons and rugged case certainly suggest its something from the range of the Japanese juggernaut. A more practical choice than the gold watches favored by some of his associates.

Not everyone in Vice City is wearing a gold Rollie lookalike. The Takio F-BLAST is an unmistakable nod to Casio's inexpensive digital classics, particularly the bestselling F-91W. It has the familiar rectangular display, black dial surround, and simple, functional design. There's even a more colorful version, with a red case and two-tone metal bracelet, spotted in the game's Hamlet Thrift store.

As spotted by watch Instagram account @geesgshock.

It seems that the principle of kicking the ball out onto the fairway has many applications beyond the golf course.

Weekly Visit to the Pathé Archives: Here, from 1934, is a dance-band championship from London. Cab Calloway drops by to hand out a trophy. It certainly was a hoppin’ joint, cats ’n’ kittens. History is so cool.

There was a serious tragedy at the Cincinnati Zoo. Fiona, the young hippo who’s been a social-media sensation ever since her premature birth, has died from eating a hat.

(Serious props to the reporter who got to use “pass the hat” in a unique, if unsavory, way.)

But the death of Harambe was nothing compared to the first celebrity animal death at that zoo.

On September 1, 1914, at the Cincinnati Zoo, a bird named Martha, named for the First Lady, passed away, at somewhere between 17 to 29 years of age. There was quite a sensation surrounding her passing. They took her body and froze it in a block of ice. Skinned and stuffed, Martha spent the next 30 years at the Museum of National History before moving to a semipermanent home at the Smithsonian. Martha was the last passenger pigeon.

I treasure my days as a daily—and monthly, and occasionally current—sportswriter. We lost a couple of giants this week. Fred Mitchell was the first African American sportswriter at the venerable Chicago Tribune. He wore his history easily over the 41 years in which he covered the Bulls, Bears, and Cubs until he became a columnist. My old friend from J-school, Dan McGrath, who was Fred’s sports editor, had the right of it when he said:

Mike Wise also passed away. He and I never crossed paths, but his work was full of grace and heart, and it always impressed me. Even though we were a pair of geezers, we met on social media. I was a fan of his writing, and he was a regular at the shebeen. I’m sorry I never got to meet him. May both their memories be the blessings their lives were.

#6706. 32 Gifts for the Woman Who Has Everything

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

When it comes to the woman who has everything (aka your mom, wife, grandma, or mother-in-law), the last thing she needs is more meaningless stuff. What she does need (and definitely wants) is something thoughtful that shows your love and appreciation. And now that it’s almost December, it’s really time to start thinking about the perfect holiday gift.

So what do you get for the woman who already has it all? Good question. Lucky for you, we’ve already handled the hardest part and put together a list of gifts any woman would want, no matter how full her closet already is.

Maybe it’s something romantic and personalized for your wife, like a luxury photo album filled with pictures of the two of you together; maybe it’s a flower or coffee subscription that will keep making her smile throughout the year. Or maybe it’s a new apron for the home chef, a digital photo frame for the faraway grandma, or a cashmere sweater that will keep her cozy for years to come. You could even go all out and get her a sparkly piece of jewelry you know she’ll wear every day.

In short, whichever special woman you’re shopping for, there’s something here that will make her holiday season extra special, all thanks to you.

A pair of statement earrings for under $300 is a no brainer, especially when they look as nice as these suspended round-cut ones from Dorsey. If she wants something more dainty, go with the petite size; for something more bold, you can opt for the oversized version. Or keep it classic with the medium. Whichever you choose, she’s going to be impressed.

If you can swing a tennis bracelet that’s a step up from stainless steel and sapphire, it should be this one from Ring Concierge. With a choice of natural diamonds or lab-grown diamonds and 14-karat white gold or yellow gold, it is truly a luxurious and timeless piece.

A set of luxe pajamas from Petite Plume will give her the sweetest of dreams, and with more than 20 colors and patterns to choose from, there’s sure to be a set she’ll obsess over.

Made from vegan shearling, these fluffy slippers from Rothy’s are so cute and so comfortable, thanks to a cushioned footbed. Every day spent lounging around the house in these will feel like a luxury.

It’s a small but mighty gift that lets her bring a reminder of you wherever she may go. Personalize it with her initials for a special touch.

If she’s astrologically inclined, try this made-to-order birth-date book, which features an illustration of her unique astrological birth chart on the cover and a detailed analysis inside.

The Dyson Airwrap is the hair tool every woman wants. With different options for different hair types and six attachments, she literally won’t ever need another hot tool for the rest of her life.

Everyone is getting into peptides now, and the best introduction to that is with Dr. Barbara Sturm’s new peptide serum, which won a 2026 Esquire Grooming Award.

If she’s been talking endlessly about an upcoming weekend trip, you gotta get her the nicest weekender bag in the world, and that’s the Vérité weekender from Métier.

Don’t just gift her a perfume that smells good; get her something that will look good on her dresser, shelf, or wherever she keeps her fragrance. Bulgari’s new Omnia Amethyste eau de parfum is a gorgeous woody floral blend that comes in one of the coolest bottles I’ve seen.

Whether she’s traveling or just likes to keep her stuff organized at home, Afform’s Traveller is the perfect system to keep all her makeup brushes and products tidy. It looks so nice I actually keep mine on my dresser all the time.

A bottle of vintage Dom is for the lady who knows her bubbles. Like, really knows her bubbles.

So she’s looking for a new laptop? Make it the new MacBook Neo, Apple’s best-looking laptop that comes in at less than $1,000.

Nothing feels quite as luxurious as lathering yourself in Diptyque body lotion after a shower and before bed. This one has the brand’s famous rose scent, which she will fall in love with.

She’ll feel like the most elegant woman in the world lounging in this fluffy robe at home.

You can never go wrong with a diamond necklace.

When in doubt, go with something classic from Tiffany, like this 18k yellow-gold double-link bracelet.

Flowers are beautiful, timeless, and romantic—which is why they always make a great gift.

For a luxury watch that’s especially luxurious, go with Hermès. This dainty silver Nantucket watch will become her favorite new accessory.

If she’s a tea drinker like me, she’ll really appreciate a good tea gift set. We love this one from Forgrave, which includes black, white, green, and herbal blends and comes with the brand’s signature tea infuser.

#6704. 15 Best Beard Oils for Taming Your Stubborn Facial Hair

So, you've realized. Growing a beard was only the beginning: Now you’ve gotta give it some love. Thankfully, the best beard oil will keep that sculpted scruff feeling great! And not just for you, either, because the right beard oil leaves your skin glowing—and stops your facial hair from annoying any partner who gets close to it. Great beard oils also keep coarse hair from getting brittle and wiry, and soften your beard so you’re not scratching your neck or dealing with beard dandruff (yes, it exists). All that from just a couple drops of wonder oil.

I’d go so far as to rank beard oil as the second most important beard care product, just after beard trimmers. And it’s a close second. Because whether you keep your facial hair at a precise 24-hour stubble, go full hermit-mode, or stay somewhere in between, a beard oil does irreplaceable work. (The kind you can’t replace with any old moisturizer or conditioner.) So, if you’re tired of flyaways, coarse beard hair, dandruff, or itchiness, these GQ-tested and approved beard oils will turn your scruff into fluff. Promise.

For the longest time, I thought of Honest Amish as “that Amazon beard oil,” given its enormous popularity on the e-tailer. (At the time of writing, we’re talking nearly 30,000 reviews and a 4.5-star rating.) And often, I'll intentionally seek out an alternative to these Amazon-beloved grooming products—if only because it feels like the equivalent of recommending Applebee's to an out-of-towner. But this time, well: Welcome to Applebee's.

Frankly, I couldn't find a better beard oil. Not only is Honest Amish cost-effective (at the second-best price-per-ounce on my list), but the brand nailed the blend. It's a pure oil recipe (no non-oil ingredients), with seven carrier oils and seven essential oils that give it an earthy scent and help tone the skin beneath your bushel. It absorbs fast, works great to smooth down flyaways, relax stiff bristles, and battle both dry skin and flake-prone beards. And did I mention the value? Six bucks per ounce is a steal.

To be clear, I only use Honest Amish on my beard, largely because I'm not a fan of using any essential oils on bare skin—I particularly find that pumpkin seed oil (a key ingredient here) can be slightly pore-clogging on acne-prone skin. That said: I count myself as acne-prone and haven't had issues with Honest Amish, so I'm probably being over-cautious. It's really just that good.

Despite the name Beardbrand, this company's product line covers the whole body, with a focus on multi-tasking grooming products like dual washes, all-purpose balms, and this utilitarian oil. You'll catch me using it on my hands, elbows, and hair—whether for a dash of shine (to offset a matte hair product) or a protective coat against frizz and humidity. But Beardbrand's Utility Oil is on this list because it works beautifully in the beard.

Sure, it's a touch heavier than some other oils—but that also makes it a terrific pre-shave lubricant. It's important to note that no matter which “flavor” you choose, this oil has a strong dash of fragrance. They're all good, but I'm partial to the brand's new Bold Fortune scent, which oozes a sexy combo of leather, oud, and tobacco. (And pro-tip: The brand's utility wash has the same come-hither scent, if you want to double down.)

Horace's beard oil will make a lot of you happy, though for different reasons. For those overwhelmed by scents—particularly artificial ones—you'll appreciate that its got the subtlest of aromas. I'm partial to the Lemon & Mint one, which reads as “fresh" and never overwhelms. For those looking to stay away from chemicals, Horace's formula is 99% natural—and the 1% is a synthetic grapeseed oil, not something with 12 syllables, which I'm fine with.

The brand gets bonus marks for its easily-understood ingredients labels, which break down these details and err on the side of much-appreciated transparency. Finally, if you're on the fence about beard oils, Horace will make you a believer. It absorbs fast, gives a pinch of shine, and makes just enough difference in terms of taming, moisturizing, and softening that you'll see why a beard oil makes sense.

In the grooming world, Jack Black (the brand) has earned its own fanbase, thanks in large part to one product—the signature Jack Black beard oil. Apply just a few drops and it immediately goes to work softening coarse and brittle facial hair, and without an overpowering fragrance that will distract you all day long. It's one of the most popular beard oils on Amazon, but don't hold that against it. While it softens and adds shine, this oil also delivers a dose of antioxidants and vitamins from natural ingredients such as brown algae and rosemary.

It also uses a unique blend of oils with the subtlest whiff of melon fragrance. Instead of the usual suspects (jojoba, avocado, sunflower seed), this product uses Kalahari melon oil, marula oil, and plum oil, which moisturize your facial hair and skin on contact. Because these oils are all fast-absorbing (my beard hair soaks it up like a sponge), they make this product much less greasy than other beard oils, giving it a more premium feel.

I test literally hundreds of grooming products a year, and I'm so intimately acquainted with brands new and old that I often think about them based on their best product. For Pura d'Or, it's this organic beard oil. It's the best value on this list, at $3 per fluid ounce. Given how quickly you can go through beard oil—just think, if you're applying up to twice a day, that dollar-per-ounce math becomes important. So, in addition to being my budget pick, this also gets my recommendation as the best beard oil for big beards.

Of course, it's only important if the formula measures up—and here, it does. There are two carrier oils, argan and jojoba, along with a bit of grapefruit peel and some sandalwood essential oil for both a natural scent and a touch of antimicrobial powers. It's a great beard oil for anyone, no matter how big your bank account, but if you're bearding on a budget, you'll appreciate how much goodness Pura d'Or packs into a few bucks.

Blind Barber's tonka-tinged oil is wonderful for the guy who wants to moisturize skin and whiskers with the same product. It wears extremely light—so much so that my oily skin barely registers its presence—and I find its carrier-oil-centric and shea-butter-forward formula especially comforting on the skin. It should be noted that this formula includes artificial perfume, so fragrance-averse individuals should steer clear. But for those of us who want a special scent, a flake-free beard, and soothed skin, Blind Barber's beard oil does it all.

While I'd use most of the above options as both a hair oil and a beard oil, Aesop's is one where I think of it as a hair oil first and foremost. It brings control without making things look greasy, while trapping moisture and stopping frizz. I'll use it in short hair to tame down the sides, in medium lengths (mixed with a cream or clay) for added shine, or in long hair to prevent split ends. Then, once my hair's settled up, I'll run my hands through my beard afterward. So, if you want a great hair oil with beard benefits, Aesop's is the winner.

Since the beginning of the men's grooming boom, I've been waiting for the big designer fashion houses to start catering to guys with facial hair. For now, Tom Ford's beard oil stands alone. So, how does the designer oil stack up against our everyday favorites? After trying it out, I can tell you it’s better as a fragrance than a beard oil, but the scent alone earns it a spot in this guide.

The tobacco vanilla fragrance is much more sophisticated than what we’re used to seeing, or smelling, in this space. The scent puts an emphasis on vanilla, and you just need one or two pumps for full coverage. Considering the price and the name on the bottle, we were shocked to find this oil comes in a plastic pump bottle, but that fragrance. If you want to buy beard oil as a gift, this premium conditioning oil is a step up over your average Amazon brand, even if it doesn't soften coarse hair as well as some of our other options.

If you have sensitive skin—or if you're afraid of your beard oil clashing with your daily cologne—then Marlowe makes one of the only fragrance-free beard oils. Most beard oil formulas contain both essential oils and some type of perfume oil (or, parfum, for my fellow ingredients detectives), which can be risky for your skin, follicles, and pores, so it’s nice to have an option for acne-prone and sensitive skin types.

This beard oil couldn’t be simpler—and I mean that in a good way. Marlowe No. 143 has an all-oil formula and just four ingredients: safflower seed oil, sunflower seed oil, corn oil, and soybean oil. That’s it. Considering the average beauty product contains up to a dozen ingredients we can’t even pronounce, it’s a refreshing change. Although, I have to say, if you want the softest possible beard, Honest Amish also makes a fragrance-free oil.

Most guys use beard oil to tame flyaways. But what if you want the thickest, fullest beard possible? In that case, I’d recommend reaching for Bevel’s oil. This brand makes skin and hair products for Black men, but anyone with coarse or curly hair can benefit from their products. GQ contributor Timothy Beck Werth reports that this oil gave him a thicker-looking beard without excess frizz, which isn't easy to do. (BTW, that’s as much as you can expect from any beard oil, so please don’t fall for the “beard growth serums” you’ll find all over the internet.)

For parched facial hair, the grapeseed, macadamia, jojoba, and argan oil will condition your beard and add a healthy shine. For beneath the scruff, the avocado and primrose oil with soothe your skin without clogging up your pores and follicles. Werth says this beard oil instantly softened his facial hair, and he also liked the sweeter smell of the macadamia-based formula. Most beard oils opt for woodsy fragrances, and the more subtle scent profile here is a nice surprise, he explains.

Anthony’s conditioning beard oil is a 2-in-1 product—a pre-shave oil and regular beard oil. That approach can be risky, since a lot of multipurpose products try to be everything and succeed at nothing, but luckily that’s not the case here. If you regularly cycle between clean-shaven, stubble, and full-on lighthouse keeper, then you can still use this product daily. As a pre-shave oil, it moisturizes the skin and softens facial hair for a smoother shave with fewer morning-after red bumps. As a beard oil, it hydrates, then locks in that moisture so your beard stays soft and shiny all day. I found the consistency to be somewhere in between a leave-in conditioner gel and a traditional oil, making it thicker and less messy to apply.

The oil also has an herbaceous, minty fragrance that’s so refreshing it feels like aromatherapy every time you run it through your hair. That’s because the formula is full of ingredients that smell good, feel good, and actually help your skin and hair. It uses carrier oils like soybean and castor to deliver skin-pleasing and antimicrobial botanicals such as rosemary, basil, tea tree oil, eucalyptus, and spearmint. And on the hydration front, calendula and olive oil nourish your whiskers into their softest, fluffiest selves. Because of the more complex fragrance and ingredients profile, this wouldn’t be my pick for guys with sensitive skin. Still, it’s a multi-functional serum you’ll look forward to using every day.

Lots of beard oils promise to get flyaways under control, yet those rogue hairs keep popping up. So, what do you do? Instead of trying yet another oil, try reaching for a product that’s more of a leave-in conditioner for your beard. We’ve previously praised Zeus’s beard brushes, but the California-based brand also makes a beard serum that deserves a spot in your bathroom cabinet. I love that this beard oil has the simplest possible ingredients list: sweet almond oil, soybean oil, argan seed oil, trihydroxystearin, avocado oil, and fragrance.

The only multi-syllable ingredient there is a simple thickening agent, which gives this refined beard oil a gel-like consistency. That thicker formula will also put flyaways in their place. The very subtle fragrance is more Mediterranean than winter pine forest (we tested the Verbena Lime version), but you can also opt for a fragrance-free formula if you have sensitive skin or allergies. The plastic bottle uses an air pump, letting you get every last drop of oil. For clean freaks who hate the messiness and spillage that always comes with beard oil application, this is another bonus.

Tired of being left out of couple’s skincare night just because those stupid sheet masks won’t hold onto your glorious beard? Cardon’s got you, boo. Actually, this bead oil hack is perfect for guys who only sparingly use beard oil on an as-needed basis.

The bamboo and charcoal sheet mask is specially shaped for guys with beards, and each mask comes with a dose of beard oil made with argan and jojoba oil. In the Venn diagram of Korean skincare and beard care products, this sheet mask-beard oil duo is the tiny, microscopic bit of overlap in the middle.

The best best oil should support your skin and your beard, and Baxter of California's sweet-smelling oil doesn't sacrifice one for the other. In his guide to the best beard care products, GQ's Timothy Beck Werth said that “In our testing, no beard oil absorbs as quickly or spreads as evenly as this one.” Squalane helps nourish the most overlooked skin on your face, while avocado oil, vitamin E, and botanical extracts smooth out your beard.

As with anything you put on your skin, the ingredients are what matter most. And with beard oils, there are two categories of ingredients: carrier oils and essential oils. No two ingredients will perform exactly the same. Some will help with very specific situations or conditions; some may actively aggravate your skin. Essential oils in particular can aggravate skin if used by themselves. So, consider the following carrier oils and essential oils, favored both by myself and Alyssa Bachowski, a senior barber at Fellow Barber’s Williamsburg, NYC barbershop.

Carrier oils—often vegetable and see oils—make up the bulk of these beard oil formulas, bringing beard nourishment and skin hydration. The most popular carrier oils are argan oil and jojoba oil (both of which Bachowski recommends), though there are dozens of varieties, from castor to marula to grape seed oils. They're usually unscented, though some—like coconut oil—may carry an aroma. Because carrier oils make up the “base” of the oil, they should be among the first to appear in a product's formula or list of ingredients.

“Jojoba oil is non-comedogenic, which means it will not clog your pores. It is also a lighter oil that is absorbent, so it won't just sit on top of the hair and give you a greasy appearance,” Bachowski notes. Along with argan oil, you’re going to see a lot of jojoba oil in most popular beard oil blends.

“Argan oil is filled with a ton of vitamins and minerals, as well as linoleic acid, sterolins, and squalene. All of these aid in protecting and keeping your skin and beard healthy,” Bachowski says.

“Castor oil is known for promoting beard hair growth,” says Bachowski. It’s hypothetically the best beard-growth oil of the crew, because it acts like a vitamin for your strands—though there's more on that below in the FAQs. But don't expect it to grow more hairs, or thicker hairs. Castor oil does have a smoke-and-mirrors benefit, at least. “It is a heavier oil and dark in color, so it can give the appearance of a denser beard on men with dark facial hair,” Bachowski says.

Grape seed oil can help tone down your skin's oil production, and is extremely moisturizing for scruff and skin alike.

Rich in fatty acids, this is a frontrunner for “most nourishing oil” for your skin and whiskers.

Yes, shea butter is in fact a carrier oil, and it has supremely moisturizing properties that make it great for detangling scraggly beards and softening stiff hairs.

Essential oils are distillations from more potent sources, and they tend to be additive—and active—ingredients in a beard oil. These oils are typically extracted through steaming methods, and their range of benefits can include antimicrobial properties, oil-toning abilities, and soothing superpowers. Typically, any natural fragrance in a beard oil will come from essential oils. But pure essential oils can be extremely damaging to the skin.

“You should not apply pure essential oil to a beard—it will do the opposite of what you are trying to achieve,” Bachowski says. “These [pure oils] are not good for your beard nor your skin. as they can be irritating and harmful. Essential oils can cause dryness, itchiness, and redness—and some people may have allergic reactions to them.” This is exactly why you want beard oils from trusted, tested brand.

Bachowski likes cedarwood oil for its naturally astringent properties. “It helps cleanse the pores and prevent ingrown hairs,” she adds. It also adds a natural fragrance.

“Sandalwood has anti-inflammatory properties that help reduce any skin irritation,” Bachowski says of this deliciously scented oil.

Lavender helps calm inflammation and redness, says Bachowski. Plus it brings lavender's soothing scent.

This antimicrobial, anti-inflammatory, oil-toning ingredient is terrific for acne-prone faces and oily skin types.

Along with its refreshing scent, grapefruit peel oil helps nourish and detangle.

As GQ's lead grooming product reviewer, I've tried just about every beard oil in the biz. The ones chosen for this roundup excel at protecting and moisturizing both skin and hair, with minimal risk for things like inverse reactions and blemishes. They absorb cleanly, unless they expressly choose not to (like those which expressly use silicone), and they smell terrific (with both natural and artificially scented options). I've tested beard oils for a decade, on a beard that's been long, short, and everything in between. I've also scoured online reviews to confirm that these products are backed by throngs of loyal fans, whose collective opinion echoes my own experiences.

What is the best beard oil according to GQ?

With more than a decade of testing beard oils under his belt, GQ’s expert tester chose Honest Amish as the best beard oil. And 29,000 Amazon shoppers have rated this same product 4.5 out of 5 stars.

What is the difference between beard balm vs. oil?

Both beard balm and beard oil are both terrific for facial hair—but they don't do the same job. Beard balm is optimal for taming and styling facial hair, especially at medium and longer lengths. Beard oil provides less control over the shape and style, but is excellent at softening and conditioning the hairs and the skin underneath (preventing beard itch, beard dandruff, and split ends).

After using a beard wash, most beard oils are applied the same way: “Start off with 2-3 drops in the palm of your hand,” says Bachowski. “Rub it into your hands so that it is evenly distributed from fingertips to the heel of your hand. Start by rubbing the oil with your fingertips onto the skin beneath the beard hair, then eventually working the oil from root to tip of the actual beard. Make sure to also apply oil to your neck and cheeks where you may have shaved or trimmed as well. If you feel like the 2-3 drops wasn't enough based on the length or density of your beard, apply another 2-3 drops.”

Preventing beard dandruff starts with changing your skincare regimen. Namely, introducing exfoliation and moisturizing products that can get past a thick nest of facial hair. For exfoliation, look for physical scrubs (even a beard brush will work). For the best moisturizing effects, be sure to apply beard oil both morning and night. The beard oil will also soften and condition the facial hair to prevent itchiness.

No, beard oil is not meant to help beard hair growth, whether that’s to make facial hair thicker or grow faster. To be honest, we don’t know of much that can really change your scruff's density besides a beard transplant and maybe minoxidil. Beard oil is here to make your existing beard hair softer and more cooperative, while also nourishing the skin beneath the beard—skin that’s often prone to dehydration and dandruff. It’s already doing a lot.

Absolutely. Beard oil is a great investment for anyone with facial hair, since it can help soften and tame your scruff while simultaneously moisturizing the skin underneath. This means no more itching, no flyaways, and no beard dandruff.

Alyssa Bachowski is a senior barber at Fellow Barber’s Williamsburg NYC barbershop location.

#6703. Thom Browne and Mr Porter Just Released an Exclusive New Capsule Collection

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

The 17-piece lineup makes its debut on young restaurateur George McNally.

Launching this week on Mr Porter is a 17-piece capsule collection created with New York’s own Thom Browne. Browne is famed in men’s fashion for his conceptual runway shows—staged in Florence, Milan, Paris and, of course New York—but beneath all the elevated showmanship lies a broad and distinctly wearable aesthetic.

Consisting of casual and tailored pieces and extending to luggage, home goods, and even the recently launched Thom Browne x Bose headphones, the collection puts a youthful twist on Browne’s signature style that blends sportswear and suits with a rigorous attention to detail. Browne has acted as Chairman of the Council of Fashion Designers of America since 1923 and won its prestigious Menswear Designer award four times in his 25-year career.

To showcase the collaboration, the capsule was photographed by Ian Kenneth Bird on young New York restaurateur George McNally. George—who, at just 23, has worked pretty much every restaurant position going in New York and London since he was a teenager—is tracing his own culinary path with Faux, his hot new space in Tribeca, serving reasonably priced, loosely French-Mediterranean food, and a space that stays open late for a city that, proverbially, never sleeps.

Go to Mr Porter to see and shop the collection and read George’s accompanying interview.

#6701. 12 Watches That Make a Great Gift for the Man in Your Life

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Whether you’re on a Panerai budget or something closer to Casio.

A watch is a sentimental gift. It’s not something casually given—not a tie or a fancy notebook or even a good pen or a nice bottle of booze. Don’t get me wrong, those are delightful things to receive and all appropriate for a run-of-the-mill holiday or birthday celebration. But some years aren’t run-of-the-mill. Some years require something extra. Some years are watch years—and trust me, you’ll know it when you get there.

But what watch? It’s a personal choice, of course, but a personal choice that you’re making for someone else. Luckily, you likely know them pretty well. Around these parts, Esquire knows watches pretty well. Here are 12 suggestions, in price ranges from midwestern frugal to multigeneration inheritance. No matter how much you’re comfortable spending, you can’t go wrong with any of these watches.

A classic watch for a classic guy. The Khaki Field Expedition is the quintessential field watch. Rich history of American craftsmanship, purpose-driven aesthetics, functional compass bezel (even if I can’t figure out how to use it for the life of me), and in the past few years it’s been available with the always practical NATO strap. It’s an ideal watch for the horologist, who’ll appreciate the history, the movement, and the exhibition case back. Win-win, guaranteed all-star gift.

I’m an unabashed Citizen fan. The Promaster “Fugu” is my favorite watch in the $500 range, and the Japanese brand’s Eco-Drive offerings are even more affordable. The Promaster Sea Eco-Drive is almost criminally underpriced for the watch that you get. We’re talking an emerald-green dial with a matching unidirectional 60-minute bezel, 200 meter depth rating, and a stainless-steel bracelet for well under $300. This is a great gift for the practically driven, classic dive-watch fan. And if the green dial is too swerve-y, the black and the blue are both excellent choices.

Good doesn’t always mean expensive. A big ol’ dive watch isn’t appropriate for every occasion or every guy. Simpler can be better. Like this under-$100 life-proof G-Shock. It treads the line between classic G-Shock aesthetics and little Casio reference. A watch equally appropriate for a man who never thinks about watches or one who is always thinking about watches—Schrödinger’s timepiece of no taste or good taste. And, hot take, it pairs better with a suit than sneakers do. Keep your shoes formal and let your watch carry the high-low combo.

If he’s not a G-Shock man, he may be an Ironman. A Timex Ironman, that is, even if he’s not logging an insane amount of weekend mileage. As the world grows wary of increased technocracy-driven smart designs, there’s a simple nostalgia to the ’90s-tech runner’s watch. Turns out they were right all along. You don’t need a phone on your wrist, just a stopwatch and an alarm clock and a 100-lap memory to boot. I also like the orange accents here. If he is midwestern frugal and would object to a gift over $100, this scaled-back version also plays.

Shinola’s Duck Watch references ’60s and ’70s skin divers, which sounds dirty but really is just a term for smaller, less depth-rated dive watches and the culture of free diving and spearfishing around them. That’s a reductive explanation that some watch nerds will quibble with, but it’ll serve us for now. The two important things to take away are 1) there was a magazine of the same name that you can find on eBay and 2) Shinola’s modern version is a killer watch. I have this one in Canary Yellow, but I recently borrowed the new Mallard Brown colorway for a photo shoot, and I love it. Hated to send it back. In the sun, the dial turns almost iridescent, like the titular duck’s feathers. It’s battery powered, water-resistant, and not fussy at all.

[Editor’s note: The Mallard Brown colorway is currently out of stock, so for the time being we will direct you to the Canary.]

Let’s get fancy. But let’s get fancy with purpose. More on Rolexes soon, but I think Panerai makes the most exciting watches in the $5,000 to $15,000 range. I realize that’s a large range, one encompassing the price of a decent motorcycle or a used Camry, but I’m talking luxury Swiss-made watches here. This is the tip of the iceberg. Panerai has design panache and exquisite movements, a design that’s not too “look at me” but still far enough outside the classic mold that it demands a second look. It’s not a watch for everyone, but man, those who will love it will love it immensely.

Recently I hosted a friend and fellow style writer for a will-not-be-named rival publication at the Detroit headquarters of T-shirt maker Soft Goods MFG. He had a couple enviable pieces (understated Nitty Gritty corduroy jacket, Paraboot derbies, perfectly faded vintage Levi’s), but I kept looking at his watch, a Unimatic Quattro. In the interest of individuality and my own love of a good suede, I’m suggesting this Todd Snyder collab as the next gift for that extra-special man in your life. It’s Unimatic with a twist, Italian design with an Americana POV, like spaghetti western for your wrist. Clint Eastwood fans rejoice.

Timex and J. Crew’s recent trout-forward collab watch sold out in an instant—unfortunately before I could get my hands on one. But there are other options for the outdoorsy dude. Especially one who cares about stopping forest fires. This Vero Smokey Bear option toes the line between campy and classy with aplomb. It’ll look good while you’re chopping wood or a little Wes Anderson–y at the lodge with a glass of bourbon in hand, telling stories about the trout that got away.

TAG can get a bad rap in the watch-collecting community, but ignore the critics, especially if your intended recipient is a racing enthusiast. TAG Heuer has a long history in F1 and Grand Prix, and it looks as much at home in the cockpit of a fancy Brad Pitt–piloted car as it does in the fan stands or the sports bar. If you want to step it up a level, the Monaco is a luxury take on a race-day watch—all European angles and rich leather.

This is the only pilot’s watch on the list—and for good reason. It’s the most handsome pilot’s watch on the market. Marathon developed these in partnership with the Kelly Air Force Base in 1986 and specced it out to withstand high-altitude flight, or even an ejection. If you’re in the market for a classic pilot’s chronograph, Bremont makes the best one. But if you are in the market for a no-frills, all-style, practically designed pilot’s watch, the Marathon Arctic Navigator is your ticket.

Maybe he’s a Rolex Pepsi guy, but you’re on a non-Swiss-made budget. I’ll let you in on a secret: Seiko makes a pretty damn good approximation. I particularly like that this one is PADI co-branded and automatic, with a 200 meter depth rating. Even better, it’s under $500.

For my money, Doxa makes some of the most interesting dive watches out there. Is this on the list because I’d really love if someone took the hint and bought one for me? Hard to say. What’s easy to say is that the orange-dial Doxa Sub 200T is a showstopper. It’s got a slightly smaller profile and a more interesting silhouette than the original 200 but still retains the necessary details, like its unidirectional rotating bezel with its integrated U.S. Navy no-decompression limit table. No rules on whether that’s used underwater or just at the dinner table, either. It’s good to know how long to cook a steak for.

#6700. Everything We Know About Miley Cyrus and Maxx Morando’s Wedding

Miley Cyrus and Maxx Morando just said “I do.” On Saturday, November 21, the former Disney Channel star and her drummer fiancé tied the knot in Palm Springs, surrounded by family and friends.

The newlyweds first met on a blind date back in 2021, and have been linked for the five years since. In the time between, they have kept their relationship fairly private and out of the spotlight, simply popping up for the rare red-carpet appearance or supporting one another at a performance. Behind the scenes, the two have proven avid collaborators, working together on Cyrus’s Something Beautiful and Bass Persuades records.

In December of 2025, Cyrus announced her engagement in the subtle (yet not-so-subtle) manner of debuting a chunky gold engagement ring at the Hollywood premiere of Avatar: Fire and Ash—letting the shimmering diamond do all the talking.

Despite keeping things out of the public eye, Cyrus offered a rare glimpse into her life with Morando during her Harper’s Bazaar December 2024/January 2025 cover story interview. “He’s very similar to me,” she said of her beau, a drummer for the rock band Liily. “We just don’t take life too seriously.”

Ahead, we take a look at everything we know about Cyrus and Morando’s wedding.

Cyrus and Morando’s wedding ceremony took place in Palm Springs, California. More specifically, it was held at Frank Sinatra’s former home, which was transformed with hundreds of white roses for the occasion. Photos obtained exclusively by DeuxMoi showed the couple exchanging vows by the pool, in front of an intimate crowd of guests.

The couple’s official photos showed the “Can’t Be Tamed” star wearing a white long-sleeve Saint Laurent gown with an open back and long train. She paired the look with an updo, drop earrings, and pointed-toe heels, and had her Jacquie Aiche-designed engagement ring on full display. Standing with her at the altar, Morando wore a classic suit—matching the guests, who were all dressed in black.

At the ceremony—which was officiated by Flaming Lips frontman Wayne Coyne—Cyrus and Morando were surrounded by friends and family. Among the guests were Cyrus’s parents, Tish and Billy Ray, with their respective partners, Dominic Purcell and Elizabeth Hurley.

#6699. How Will Robert Pattinson’s James Bond Dress?

When Terence Young, the director of Dr. No, needed a suit for Sean Connery to wear for his debut as James Bond, he took him to see his personal Savile Row tailor, Anthony Sinclair.

“Connery’s suits were considered relatively contemporary, because Anthony always had a very soft shoulder on his suits,” explains Elliot Mason, the director of business at Anthony Sinclair, which is no longer located on Savile Row, but still makes classic British suiting. “Anthony liked to nip in the waist, so it gave that real hourglass silhouette to the suit, which really is quite timeless. And as was the trend at the time, the Connery suits all had a relatively high rise, double-pleated with English forward pleats, and most of his trousers had Daks button-tab adjusters on the side.”

Opting to dress James Bond in a Savile Row suit might seem like a no-brainer in hindsight, but the decision—and the cut of the suits themselves—still resonates more than 60 years later. Sinclair would make all of Connery’s suits during his nine-year run as James Bond, and his blend of classic British tailoring and of-the-moment influences would create a template for every Bond to come. With Robert Pattinson—a Dior ambassador and a guy with a reputation for adventurous style—chosen to take up the mantle in Bond 26, the question now becomes: what will he wear?

Tailor Anthony Sinclair fitting Sean Connery for one of the suits he wore in 1963’s From Russia With Love.

Ian Fleming, the author who created James Bond, spent a lot of time talking about what Agent 007 drank (Wolfschmidt Vodka) what he drove (Bentley convertibles, for the most part) and how he made his coffee (he was a dedicated pour-over guy), but, surprisingly, he never specified where the spy bought his clothes. As a result, for the last six decades, it’s been up to the directors and costume designers of the 25 previous films to create a new look for every successive Bond. In the Connery and Moore eras it was Savile Row suits. Timothy Dalton’s Bond wore Paul Smith. Brosnan was a bespoke Brioni guy, as was Daniel Craig, who also brought Tom Ford into the mix. At 6’1” to Craig’s 5’10”, with a leaner build, and a long track record of fashion-forward dressing, Pattinson has everything needed to reinvent Bond once again.

“I’m pretty sure we’re going to see something quite different from Pattinson's Bond,” says Matt Spaiser, the tailoring obsessive behind BondSuits.com and the author of From Tailors with Love: An Evolution of Menswear Through the Bond Films. “Pattinson is a different kind of actor, and he’s going to wear something that moves differently. Plus, he’s 18 years younger than Daniel Craig.”

The actor playing Bond has a major influence on how he dresses—Daniel Craig, an adventurous dresser himself, was very hands-on with the wardrobe in his films—but Pattinson’s ongoing contract with Dior could also factor in. “Some of his Dior looks look somewhat like James Bond—he wears a great tuxedo—but I don’t think it’s going to tell us what he’s going to wear as James Bond,” Spaiser says. “He’s worn suits from other brands in other roles—like in Tenet, he wasn’t wearing a Dior suit—so I don’t think he's necessarily obligated to be wearing Dior as James Bond.”

Daniel Craig, seen here in 2019 with then-Prince Charles on the set of No Time to Die, mainly sported sharp Tom Ford suiting in his later Bond films.

Another important factor that contributes to a new Bond’s look is what’s happening in fashion whenever the film eventually gets made. “One thing that we've seen throughout most of the Bond eras is that he dresses in suits that are fairly traditional, but there's always some nod to the era that they’re in,” says Spaiser. “Sometimes they lean a little more into fashion, like when Roger Moore was wearing flares. That was something that I think they couldn't avoid without making Bond look too old-fashioned at the time.”

Tone also plays an important role, and historically has ping-ponged between serious (Connery, Dalton, Craig) and more campy (Moore, Brosnan), but with Denis Villeneuve, the auteur behind Sicario, Blade Runner: 2049, and Dune in the director’s chair, the smart money isn’t on hovercraft gondolas and punny Bond girl names. A darker, more atmospheric milieu could make for an interesting complement to Pattinson’s taste for high-fashion.

If Mason were dressing Pattinson for the role, he says, he’d take a lead from his clientele, many of whom, like Bond, are well-heeled Londoners with a taste for expensive sports cars and well mixed cocktails. “The majority of our customers are reasonably conservative, and when it comes to fit, they’re sensitive to current trends,” Mason notes. As such, he’d opt for longer jackets, both single- and double-breasted, that are fuller through the body, and straight-leg pleated trousers. “It’s not that very, very slim fit that we'd seen for so many years, but it still needs to be refined,” he says. “I don’t think it would be appropriate to put him in wide-leg or baggy trousers. I just don't think it’s right for Bond.”

Robert Pattinson, the latest 007, has been the face of Dior’s menswear for over a decade.

Given the intense scrutiny on every relaunch of the Bond franchise, and the tendency of fans to get worked up online over perceived mishandling of a beloved character, Pattinson, Villeneuve, and the film’s yet-to-be-announced costume designers have little room for error. Go too conservative and they’ll be accused of playing it safe; make Bond into a fashion guy, and they’ll incur the wrath of the traditionalists.

“He needs to look current, but also timeless,” Spaiser says. “It’s a hard balance to find.”

Josh Hartnett Just Declared Open Season on Overcoats

Finally, Omega Makes a Watch for the Bond Villains
