#6698. The 30 Thanksgiving Movies Actually Worth Watching

These are the best films to pop onto the TV before you fall into a food coma.

Most people don’t think of Thanksgiving when they think of holiday movies. Halloween and Christmas? of course. Valentine’s Day? You bet! But outside of John Hughes’s 1987 comedy Planes, Trains and Automobiles starring Steve Martin and John Candy, Hollywood still struggles to really capture the feeling of Thanksgiving on film.

That’s not to say that filmmakers aren’t trying. The thirty films below capture all the fall vibes, family squabbles, and strenuous meal planning that come with Thanksgiving dinner—even if some of them don’t technically take place on Thanksgiving. (Still, I tried my best to keep those at a minimum.) If you’re looking for something to watch with the whole family, start an argument, or just feel thankful that good Thanksgiving movies do exist, these are the Thanksgiving movies that are actually worth watching.

You won’t find a Thanksgiving movie list without Planes, Trains and Automobiles. The 1987 John Hughes comedy is a genuine classic—arguably so good that no one’s made a better Thanksgiving film since. How could they? The story follows a marketing executive (played by Steve Martin) who just wants to get home to New York for Thanksgiving on the busiest travel day of the year. Instead, he spends the entire three-day trip across the country with a chatty shower ring salesman (John Candy). Despite Martin’s prickliness, they’re forced to bond over the holiday while sharing the kind of heartfelt laughs that most movies nowadays just can’t replicate with the same sincerity anymore.

This is one of the movies that that you’ll see on the list that is more about fall than it is about Thanksgiving, but You’ve Got Mail does include a Thanksgiving scene. In this 1998 romantic comedy starring Tom Hanks and Meg Ryan and written and directed by Nora and Delia Ephron, two Upper West Side New Yorkers meet in an online chat room and fall in love over a series of months. At one point, Tom Hanks walks into Zabar's on Thanksgiving, which is maybe the most New York thing one can do.

Jodie Foster's finest directorial effort remains this 1995 comedy about the Thanksgiving get-together of Holly Hunter's thoroughly dysfunctional clan, which includes her mother (Anne Bancroft) and father (Charles Durning), her brother (Robert Downey Jr.), and his friend (Dylan McDermott). What ensues is the gold standard for family-gathering holiday films, full of absurdity, pathos, and ultimately a rousing sense of the ups and downs of dealing with relatives.

An ensemble cast of Vanessa Williams, Vivica A. Fox, Nia Long, and Michael Beach star in this comedy-drama film about a Black American family that gets together every Sunday to have dinner. But when Big Mama (Irma P. Hall) falls ill, her three daughters’ strained relationships take on an entirely new form. While the meals never occur on Thanksgiving Day, the family arguments over a mouth-watering meal do.

Remember the Titans isn't technically a Thanksgiving movie either. But it features plenty of those Thanksgiving elements—football, fall foliage, and large amounts of people gathering for meals. The film stars Denzel Washington as a high school football coach who is brought in to teach at a predominately white school in Alexandria, Virginia. And if it’s not playing on cable on Thanksgiving anyway—that is, if you haven’t cut the cord yet—I’m sure a streaming service picked it up for the holiday.

Maybe it's not as iconic as A Charlie Brown Christmas or It's the Great Pumpkin, Charlie Brown, but this Peanuts holiday special is a good holdover between Halloween and Christmas. This Emmy-winning classic sees Peppermint Patty infiltrating Charlie Brown's Thanksgiving holiday, which he naturally scrambles to put together with his trademark anxiety. (Where the hell are these kids' parents, by the way?)

Spike Lee's iconic She's Gotta Have It, following one woman's love square with her three competing lovers, isn't typically included in the wholesome holiday canon. But few films have featured a more memorable Thanksgiving dinner than that of Nola Darling (Tracy Camilla Johns), in which she invites over all three men to celebrate the holiday together as she sits at the head of the table.

Opening and closing with scenes of its characters at Thanksgiving dinner, Woody Allen's 1986 comedic drama—which won him an Oscar for Best Original Screenplay and Best Supporting Actor and Actress statuettes for Michael Caine and Dianne Wiest, respectively—tells a raft of interconnected stories, all in some way related to Mia Farrow's Hannah and her two siblings. Equal parts hilarious and touching, it remains one of the writer-director's crowning achievements.

It’s not the holidays if you haven’t watched a Lifetime movie with the family yet. Still, that doesn’t mean you must subject yourself to the most baseless shlock. How about a simple, magic family film from the creator of the Muppets. Well, posthumously, that is. And yes, that means puppets—but that should only be a plus!

Knives Out just screams cozy with all of its sweaters, wooden decorations, and fallen leaves. Rian Johnson's mystery flick, which is now a trilogy, is a must-watch if you're in a family that loves solving puzzles or escape rooms (or, you know, Daniel Craig). Hopefully, your Thanksgiving isn’t anywhere near as chaotic.

Okay, so, technically this would be more faithful to the Thanksgiving canon if it were Turkey Run. But this feature-film debut from the iconic Aardman animation studio is a Thanksgiving hit for obvious reasons. The stop-motion film, centered on a group of chickens determined to escape execution before being turned into chicken pies, is as hilarious as it is visually spectacular.

Christmas Vacation is in a category of its own, but the entire National Lampoon franchise is usually a crowd-pleaser at family holidays. This one features Malcolm in the Middle–era Bryan Cranston as the eccentric hippie and long-lost cousin of suburban man Mitch Snider. Awkward family antics ensue.

For the Christopher Guest and Eugene Levy fans, this 2006 mockumentary is about three actors played by Guest favorites Catherine O’Hara, Parker Posey, and Harry Shearer, who are making a film called Home for Purim, which is supposedly garnering lots of Oscar buzz. Things come to a bit of a halt when the studio intervenes and renames the film Home for Thanksgiving, changing the entire plot of the film just to chase awards.

This movie jumps around to four different Thanksgiving meals, all thrown by families from diverse backgrounds who have a few things in common: stress, food, and stressing over food. What’s Cooking? also features an ensemble cast composed of Kyra Sedgwick, Joan Chen, Lainie Kazan, Mercedes Ruehl, Maury Chaykin, Julianna Margulies, Alfre Woodard, and Dennis Haysbert.

For a little switch-up, this one is a documentary. Directed by Martin Scorsese, this film follows The Band’s Thanksgiving farewell show, featuring Joni Mitchell, Bob Dylan, and Eric Clapton. Alongside Stop Making Sense, The Last Waltz is often cited as one of the greatest music concert films ever made.

Many films in the ‘40s concerned the difficulties soldiers faced when coming home from the war, but few attempted to bring our heroes home over Thanksgiving. The Very Thought of You, which is more of a romantic drama, stars Dennis Morgan and Dane Clark as they return just for the holiday—before they’re shipped back once the weekend ends. Fair warning: This one’s a bit of a tear-jerker. “You know those advertisements that show pictures of guys in uniform coming back to what they left?,” one character says in the film. “They don’t show what home really means.”

Thanksgiving is anything but jovial in Ang Lee's The Ice Storm, which is set around the holiday in 1973 Connecticut, where two well-off families struggle with all manner of the-times-they-are-a'-changin' upheaval. Adultery and alcohol inevitably play a big factor in their problems, which are dramatized by director Lee—and handled by his cast, including Kevin Kline, Joan Allen, Sigourney Weaver, Tobey Maguire, Christina Ricci, and Elijah Wood—with chilling incisiveness.

In her finest performance, Katie Holmes plays the titular April, who bravely invites her dysfunctional family from suburban Pennsylvania to her tiny apartment on the Lower East Side. Disaster strikes early when her stove breaks and she is forced to find a working oven in her building. Meanwhile, her cancer-stricken mother (Oscar nominee Patricia Clarkson) slowly dreads the trek into the city to see her somewhat estranged daughter.

On Thanksgiving 1983, Marty (Josh Hamilton) brings his fiancée Lesly (Parker Posey) home to meet his family—his dim-witted and horny brother, Anthony; his cold and nosey mother, Mrs. Pascal; and his mentally unhinged and Kennedy-obsessed sister, Jackie-O. All hell breaks loose, naturally, as kitchen knives are hidden, sexual boundaries are tested, and Parker Posey delivers one of the most hilariously intense performances of her career.

Judd Apatow's dramatic comedy stars Adam Sandler as George Simmons, a wealthy movie star who seeks to get back to his stand-up-comedy roots after being diagnosed with leukemia. He meets a young aspiring comic (Seth Rogen) in search of a mentor, and the two tour the country as George performs his new material and reconnects with his ex-fiancée. It's a film about the comedy world and how friendships evolve into familial relationships, highlighted by a Thanksgiving toast George delivers in honor of his chosen family.

Trey Edward Shults made his directorial debut with this indie drama, which stars members of his real-life family—including his aunt Krisha Fairchild in the titular role. Krisha arrives at her sister's house for a Thanksgiving celebration after years of estrangement from her family. Overwhelmed by her return to this seemingly normal life, Krisha starts drinking and popping pills in secret—and all hell breaks loose as emotions fly and family secrets are exposed.

Much like Krisha above, The Humans is for people who really can’t stand Thanksgiving. Based on Stephen Karam’s Tony Award-winning play, the film stars a family played by Richard Jenkins, Jayne Houdyshell, Amy Schumer, Beanie Feldstein, Steven Yeun, and June Squibb as they all just tear into each other over Thanksgiving dinner.

This sequel to the big-screen adaptation of the classic TV sitcom finds the spooky-ooky Addams family once again up to their weird, gothic ways. But their family is thrown into turmoil when a new nanny, Debbie (a pitch-perfect Joan Cusack), has her sights on Uncle Fester—and plans on marrying him for his riches before killing him off. Wednesday and Pugsley know something's up, but Debbie convinces Gomez and Morticia to send them to summer camp—where they are forced to participate in a completely bonkers musical rendition of the first Thanksgiving.

Folk rocker Arlo Guthrie (son of Woody) plays himself in this offbeat comedy film inspired by his song of the same name. Guthrie, a long-haired draft dodger visiting some friends in an uptight Massachusetts town for Thanksgiving, thinks he's doing his hosts a favor by filling his Volkswagen minibus with their garbage and taking it to the dump. A mix-up ensues, Guthrie is busted for littering, and he seeks to prove himself unfit for combat when the draft comes calling.

Nobody's Fool is one of the last great headlining vehicles for Paul Newman, who stars here as an upstate New York construction-worker hustler in constant conflict with a contractor (Bruce Willis) whose wife (Melanie Griffith) he fancies. His routine is upended by the arrival of his estranged son (Dylan Walsh) around Thanksgiving, leading to an amusing (and disarming) holiday-set character study about loneliness, reconciliation, and the unexpected ways people achieve contentment.

Over the course of a Thanksgiving weekend, Chris O'Donnell's prep-school student comes of age while caring for a blind retired Army lieutenant (Al Pacino) in Martin Brest's acclaimed Scent of a Woman, which finally earned Pacino his first-ever Academy Award (for Best Actor). Amid all of its star's blustery hoo-ahing, it's a surprisingly tender tale of an unlikely friendship between two strangers.

Jack Lemmon and Walter Matthau are two bitter lifelong rivals who engage in ludicrous warfare over the affections of the new resident (Ann-Margret) of their Wabasha, Minnesota, neighborhood. That conflict includes an amusing Thanksgiving dinner, in which the two do their best to act like feuding, immature children.

Another cozy classic. Wes Anderson’s stop-motion film about a family of foxes with a killer voice cast—George Clooney, Meryl Streep, Jason Schwartzman, Bill Murray, Wilem Dafoe, Owen Wilson, Brian Cox, and Adrien Brody—couldn’t be a better film to watch with your family this holiday.

There’s never been a Thanksgiving-based superhero that I can recall—nor should there be—but the original Spider-Man film with Tobey Maguire features a particularly memorable Thanksgiving scene. Peter’s Aunt May invites the Osbornes over for Thanksgiving when Norman (Willem Dafoe) notices one of Peter’s injuries and deduces that he’s Spider-Man.

Eli Roth turned an actual joke project into a feature film in 2023 when he released Thanksgiving, a Halloween-esque slasher horror film about a killer let loose on Turkey Day. He wears a John Carver mask, carries an axe, and absolutely hates Black Friday. On the latter, we can all agree.

#6697. Miley Cyrus Turns to Saint Laurent’s Anthony Vaccarello for a Backless Wedding Dress

The star married Maxx Morando in Palm Springs

Miley Cyrus is a bride—a Saint Laurent bride, that is. Yesterday, the “Wrecking Ball” singer married longtime partner Maxx Morando at an intimate, flower-covered Palm Springs ceremony.

In new photos shared directly by Cyrus, the woman of the hour was seen wearing a timeless white gown, designed by Anthony Vaccarello for Saint Laurent. The whole dress was crafted from silk, and featured long sleeves, an open plunging back, and a train that pooled at her feet. Styled by Bradley Kenneth, it was paired with a matching set of silk slingback heels.

The decision to work with Saint Laurent was not unexpected, given Cyrus’s long-standing partnership with the house. Most recently, the star was seen in Vaccarello’s designs at the 2026 Golden Globes and during her Hollywood Bowl shows in October. The wedding dress, however, was likely one of the creative director’s final big moments with the label, as his departure was announced last month.

For glam, Cyrus went bold, styling her hair in an updo and giving her eyes a smokey shadow. Her stack of diamonds was the perfect blend of classic glamour and rockstar edge (the best of both worlds, if you will), with drop Chopard earrings, studs, and a crawler that curved upward along the ear.

Morando, too, wore Saint Laurent. Sitting next to his wife on a leather chair, he was seen in a black single-breasted tuxedo with a silk-satin tie. He added in his own rockstar edge with a pair of grained-leather sneakers and sunglasses.

A first look at the ceremony, obtained exclusively by DeuxMoi, showed the couple—who first started dating back in 2021, after being set up on a blind date—standing amongst white roses. The nuptials took place at Frank Sinatra’s former home, with Flaming Lips frontman Wayne Coyne reportedly serving as officiant. All of the guests appeared to be wearing black, including Cyrus’s parents, Tish and Billy Ray, and their respective partners, Dominic Purcell and Elizabeth Hurley.

#6696. 15 Best Zip-Up Hoodies for Men 2026, According to GQ Editors

Surveying the best zip-up hoodies for men takes me back to my younger days as a hopelessly romantic high school kid. My formative years were marred by angst, soundtracked by pop punk, emo, and indie rock. The piece of clothing that got me through it all? A zip-up hoodie. It was core to the indie aesthetic, with every forlorn frontman sporting one over a thrashed band tee, skinny jeans, and shredded Chucks. But nostalgia never seems to stay in the past, especially when fashion is involved.

Gen-Z has revived every minute piece of 2000s pop culture, which means zip-up hoodies are back and have all but usurped the pullover hoodie as the sweat du jour. The zip-up is inherently versatile in both practical wear and sheer styling dexterity. With the flick of the zip, you can put your beloved Death Cab for Cutie tee on full display while letting your pits breathe, or keep it zipped up when it's just a bit too nippy out. To find the best zip-up hoodies on the market, I dusted off Transatlanticism and went on a deep dive. The good news is that the old classics are still good. The better news is that there's a lot of great competition to choose from.

Uniqlo’s no-nonsense clean aesthetic and low price point has made it a go-to for any menswear enjoyer looking for good style on a budget. While the brand excels at cashmere sweaters, T-shirts, and a slew of homerun collabs, the Japanese label’s sweats are criminally underrated. We’d go so far as to say that they should be talked in the same breath as brands like Champion and Sunspel. This zip up hoodie took us by surprise thanks to its comfy midweight, all-cotton fabric and solid construction. But the main selling point is the impeccable fit.

So many other brands cut their hoodies too long in the body and leave you looking like Jack Skellington’s pubescent brother. Uniqlo managed to cut its French terry hoodie to sit right at the hips which makes for a more classic and natural silhouette that’s neither lanky nor sloppy. Because of the fabric, the feel, and the overall fit, this is my favorite zip-up hoodie by a sizable margin. It’s my most-worn hoodie and has yet to show signs of any fraying.

Hoodies were originally meant for sportswear, as warm up gear before a grueling game of football. These days, they’re more often worn for lounging on the couch to watch the kickoff. And if you want to kick it in comfort while saving your pennies for booze and amuse bouche for the big game with the boys, the best budget zip up hoodie to buy is Hanes’ EcoSmart zip hoodie. The fit is spot on—relaxed, but not sloppy, comfy in the body and the sleeves without swallowing you like a sand pit.

The fit is impressive for the price, and the overall quality means you’re getting a little more than what you pay for. It’s not a hoodie that’ll last you for too long, but it is a good quality piece for just 15 bucks. And when other hoodies offer lesser quality and worse-fitting options for about the same price? The answer is clear.

It’s hard not to sing the praises of Reigning Champ, a brand that built its fleece empire on sweatpants and hoodies. The Canadian label’s flagship midweight terry fabric has drawn the attention of streetwear royalty and menswear buffs alike and its dedication to quality has elevated the humble hoodie to oxygen-deprived heights. We named Reigning Champ’s crewneck sweatshirt and pullover hoodie as among our favorites in their respective categories, and the same goes for its zip-up hooded sweatshirt. It’s made of the same 100% cotton French terry fabric as its superlative-earning brethren and is cut with high-quality flatlock stitching for a smooth, un-bulky feel.

I had high expectations for Reigning Champ, given my previous experience, and I’m not surprised at all with how nice this zip-up is. And while I'm partial to the standard fit, it’s also available in a slim fit version, too.

Hoodies live in a universe well beyond the sportswear world from which they originated. And while we still enjoy the OGs and the classics that made the hoodie a bona fide menswear must-have, we also love seeing all the ways the style has been pushed, tweaked, and freaked. Our Legacy's Level Hood has been a successful avenue for the Stockholm-based brand to explore the limits of the hoodie and this particular version strikes a delicate balance between the freaky and the fashion-curious. It's made of an English wool knit into an all-over ribbed texture for a throwback '90s feel. The cropped, boxy silhouette and elongated sleeves harken back to the grunge-y era, while the sultry dual-tone nightshade colorway throws you off in the best way possible.

Menswear influencers will be happy to regale you with their TedTalk on the golden ratio of clothing proportions. Essentially, that means your outfit should be divided into three equal sections where the top takes up one third of the outfit. Basically, your tops should be cropped and sit closer to your natural waist. Follow it up with some high-waisted pants with a wide silhouette and you’ve instantly garnered 300K views on TikTok (RIP). With so many indie brands and influencers scrambling to make their own versions of “the perfect cropped hoodie” you’re probably tired of wading through all the choices. Do yourself a favor and go to Abercrombie.

The Moose’s version is backed by actual trend forecasters, actual designers, and actual fit models. What does that mean, exactly? It means that its cropped zip hoodie fits really well and uses surprisingly beefy fabric. It was a revelation for me, too—Abercrombie wasn’t a name I associated with hoodies, but after I saw this in person, I had to readjust my menswear word cloud.

Some of the best zip-up hoodies for men are made by a brand you never see in brick and mortar stores. Camber has been crafting the most durable sweats for decades, right here in America and have been the shining example of the difference between good quality and great quality. Sure, you have to order through an old-school web 1.0 website and wait for several weeks to actually get your hoodie. But trust us when we say that the relatively minor inconvenience is completely worth it.

The heavyweight fleece is almost as thick as a leather jacket and can stand in as outerwear if you wanted it to. While other hoodies are washed to hell in giant industrial washing machines to reach a super soft texture by the time it gets to you, Camber’s actually require you to do the work yourself. It sort of feels like you’re being swaddled by an anaconda, which means if you’re a proponent of hard clothes like raw denim, you’ll like Camber’s hoodie a lot.

Cashmere zip hoodies, once the exclusive province of Silicon Valley unicorn founders and PJ-flying nepo babies, have been democratized, none better than Naadam's take. You know the brand for bringing thoughtful, sustainable cashmere pieces at “really, only that much?” prices. Its zip hoodie is as soft as it gets, with extra credit points awarded for coming in a classic fit (too many cashmere hoodies err on the side of slim) and six colors, including this decidedly handsome olive. Wear it on weekends, work-from-home Fridays, and long flights—even if it's just Economy+.

Shoutout to brother in arms Louis Cheslaw, GQ Recs contributor, whose writing for the newsletter Magasin put us on to young brand Cease. From seed to sewing, it's beefy 20-ounce cotton is fully American-made. Assembling the fabric vertically means the hoodie will shorten up to a slightly cropped, oversized fit. Act fast if you want some of the less-boring colors—they move fast.

We can’t deny Russell Athletic’s contributions to the hoodie space. And while its formula has changed over the years, the new hoodies are still pretty good for the price. The fabric is soft and lightweight, and it’s available in a rainbow of colorways. Plus, if you’re looking for a hoodie with a little more length, this is a great option.

Bold graphics aren’t usually deemed classics, but Gap’s retro-leaning collegiate-style block logo has staying power almost as much as the Levi’s red tab. Add to that a wonderfully fluffy midweight fleece material and a tailored straight fit and you’ve got something you’ll see on moodboards for generations to come.

Champion’s Reverse Weave fabric is iconic for its nearly shrink-proof quality and almost unsettlingly rough exterior. Flip it to the inside and you’ve got a luxuriously brushed interior that’ll have you feeling like you’re melting into a cloud. Heads-up that pricing varies kinda wildly by size on Amazon.

The secondhand market is chock full of navy-blue hoodies that have been sun-faded and beaten down with time. They'll cost you just as much as this Mfpen version, but will also fall apart faster than you can zip 'em up.

Les Tien's dedication to all things sweats results in a catalog of luxurious fleece and terry cloth with a modern approach. Featuring high-end Cobrax hardware, RiRi zippers, raw-edge hems and sleeves, and a heavyweight brushed fleece, no detail is phoned in.

The Real McCoy's is considered by many to be Americana repro wizards. This revival of an early 20th-Century hoodie is just one small piece of the brand's preservation prowess. It's made using beefy sinker-knit fleece, old-school Talon-style zippers, and vertical patch pockets.

A.Presse's chokehold on the menswear illuminati refuses to let up and it's pieces like this vintage-inspired hoodie that keep the pressure on. Based on very rare “after-hood parkas", this made-in-Japan hoodie features an idiosyncratic attached hood, super dense and lofty fleece, with artfully distressed details throughout.

Think of the zip-up as the pullover hoodie's elder brother, but with way more layering potential. In its most natural state, it can cuddle up to a matching set of sweatpants and a baggy tee with flair. (Top it off with a swanky necklace, crisp sneakers, and some stellar shades and you’re left with a fit on par with Shai Gilgeous-Alexander at his tunnel-trotting best.)

Unzipped and left open, it can also take on the role of a picture frame ready to display your sickest graphic tee—much like the cardigan, another treasure of 2000s fashion. Or make like a mid-Aughts garage band ironically donning corporate attire and wear your zip-up over a dress shirt and baggy tailored trousers, a high-minded fashion move with a jolt of kooky Scandinavian cool.

Style is subjective, we know—that’s the fun of it. But we’re serious about helping our audience get dressed. Whether it’s the best white sneakers, the flyest affordable suits, or the need-to-know menswear drops of the week, GQ Recommends’ perspective is built on years of hands-on experience, an insider awareness of what’s in and what’s next, and a mission to find the best version of everything out there, at every price point.

Our staffers aren’t able to try on every single piece of clothing you read about on GQ.com (fashion moves fast these days), but we have an intimate knowledge of each brand’s strengths and know the hallmarks of quality clothing—from materials and sourcing, to craftsmanship, to sustainability efforts that aren’t just greenwashing. GQ Recommends heavily emphasizes our own editorial experience with those brands, how they make their clothes, and how those clothes have been reviewed by customers. Bottom line: GQ wouldn’t tell you to wear it if we wouldn’t.

We make every effort to cast as wide of a net as possible, with an eye on identifying the best options across three key categories: quality, fit, and price.

To kick off the process, we enlist the GQ Recommends braintrust to vote on our contenders. Some of the folks involved have worked in retail, slinging clothes to the masses; others have toiled for small-batch menswear labels; all spend way too much time thinking about what hangs in their closets.

We lean on that collective experience to guide our search, culling a mix of household names, indie favorites, and the artisanal imprints on the bleeding-edge of the genre. Then we narrow down the assortment to the picks that scored the highest across quality, fit, and price.

Across the majority of our buying guides, our team boasts firsthand experience with the bulk of our selects, but a handful are totally new to us. So after several months of intense debate, we tally the votes, collate the anecdotal evidence, and emerge with a list of what we believe to be the absolute best of the category right now, from the tried-and-true stalwarts to the modern disruptors, the affordable beaters to the wildly expensive (but wildly worth-it) designer riffs.

Whatever your preferences, whatever your style, there's bound to be a superlative version on this list for you. (Read more about GQ's testing process here.)

#6694. What to Watch After Finishing MobLand

Though season 2 of the much-loved crime series has come to an end, we've got some shows that'll fill the void.

MobLand season 2 has come to an end. (For now, at least—Tom Hardy and his motley crew have already started working on the third season.) Fortunately, there's an excellent selection of shows on Netflix that'll fill that gory Guy Ritchie–crime thriller–shaped void in your weekend watchlist. Here's our pick of the five best ones that you should stream now that season 2 is over. And if you haven’t yet, make sure to read our breakdown of MobLand’s explosive season 2 finale—and that cliffhanger.

The obvious choice, if you haven't seen it already, is the other Guy Ritchie series. The Gentlemen similarly revolves around a large mob family, has a lot of gore, and has the very same wicked sense of humor.

When an aristocratic army officer returns home after unexpectedly inheriting his family's estate instead of his older brother, he quickly discovers that it's at the heart of a notorious drug empire. While he initially tries to shrug it off, he quickly becomes more involved in the criminal underworld than he imagined he would ... and it seems it comes more naturally to him than he'd have hoped.

The Gentlemen is twisty, wildly entertaining, and will be the closest you'll get to something that feels like MobLand, even though the shows are narratively quite different.

Dept. Q is another Netflix original that packs a punch – adapted from the Department Q novel series, it's a crazy crime thriller that only has one season so far, so is easy to catch up on (though a second is on the way).

After a top-rated detective returns to work from an attack that left his partner paralyzed and another policeman dead, he finds himself caught up in the investigation into a cold case that completely takes hold of him.

The plot, the sharp sarcastic writing and the way the whole show looks are enough to make Dept. Q an excellent watch, but it's the cast (led by an outstandingly complex performance from Matthew Goode) that makes this one particularly special.

The spy thriller with the sharpest sense of humor, the most enthralling fight scenes—and, somehow amidst all the explosions—a whole lot of heart. It can be none other than Black Doves, Netflix's original espionage series that has quickly become one of the streamer's best-loved shows.

Helen Webb, a spy working for an organization called the Black Doves in an undercover marriage to a politician, has her position compromised, and mostly simple”'spy wife life” upended, when her lover is assassinated. In order to protect her and the secrecy of the Black Doves, her supervisor calls in an old friend to save her and sort out the mystery. However, the web of deceit proves unexpectedly complex and the three are soon in over their heads.

Other than being excellently acted (thanks to the legendary Keira Knightley, Ben Whishaw, Sarah Lancashire, and the supporting cast) and delightfully explosive, Black Doves is also surprisingly heartfelt, and you just can't help but root for its kooky cast. Even if they are all a bit nuts.

Once you're on board the Harlan Coben train, it's hard to hop off. I Will Find You, led by Sam Worthington and Severance's Britt Lower, is certainly one of the better adaptations of a Coben novel. Ridiculous, for sure, but undeniably entertaining the second you suspend your disbelief.

Five years after a man is wrongly imprisoned for the murder of his son, he receives information from his ex-wife's sister that his son might still be alive.

If you've read the book (or seen enough Coben shows that you know what's coming), then maybe you won't be quite as surprised by the twists and turns in this one. That being said, it's breezy, easy-watching, and it's intensely bingeable if you're into that kind of thriller.

Aside from the sense of humor, the thing we all love most about Guy Ritchie's films and others in that realm is the stylised action sequences. If that's what you'll be missing when MobLand is over, then give Gangs of London a watch.

Based on the video game of the same name (but fortunately much better), the series revolves around the power struggles of rival gangs across London, all of which have different allegiances and international connections. When one young undercover police officer is tasked with infiltrating one of them to exploit a moment of weakness, things get ... very messy. Very fast.

It's perhaps not as funny (read: not at all comedic) as some of the other entries on this list, but it's classically gritty, gory British crime drama at its finest, and a must-see for fans of the genre.

#6693. How a GQ Editor Is Wearing Abercrombie & Fitch YPB This Fall

Abercrombie & Fitch’s resurgence has been well documented, and the brand shows no signs of slowing down. Alongside its familiar mix of timeless staples, contemporary silhouettes, and archival-inspired pieces, Abercrombie has introduced YPB, or Your Personal Best: a dedicated line of performance apparel designed to bring the same considered approach to the gym as it does to the rest of your wardrobe.

Comprising everything from tees and tanks to shorts and jackets, YPB reflects Abercrombie’s ability to evolve while staying true to its heritage. The collection makes a convincing case for performance wear that goes beyond the usual sea of uninspired basics and head-to-toe Lycra. It’s also versatile enough to seamlessly move from a strenuous workout to the everyday wardrobe rotation. To put the collection through its paces, we’ve styled three looks that show just how far YPB can go.

I am a chronic overheater so my gym outfits always reveal a bit of skin. (It’s also a vanity thing so I can see my muscles at work when I’m getting after it.) This vintage-inspired muscle tank looks like some of the actual vintage tees that I’ve taken a pair of scissors to without a stranger’s set-in stains. I have a lot of hair that gets in my way so headwear is a must when working out. A bandana is a nice throwback to the classic bodybuilder era and unlike a hat, it does a better job at soaking up the sweat so it’s not dripping down my face.

The standout of this outfit is the shorts. Compression lined, they’re about as long as I’d go for shorts clocking in with a five-inch inseam. They have a discreet pocket in the liner to store my phone, and they're tight enough so my device doesn’t go flying out when I’m doing high knees. Plus, they’re stretchy enough for hitting depth on a squat without risking a NSFW tear between the cheeks.

I’ve come to learn that recovery days are not only a blessing but a necessity. But they don’t necessarily mean you have to waste away on the couch all day. My active recovery days involve a lot of walking, and that means my workout shorts make an appearance once again. I can’t deny their comfort factor, and it’s hard to beat a sweater-and-shorts combo, an outfit beloved by my favorite A-listers.

The sweater was a quarter-zip with an elastic waistband for a more streamlined, tailored fit. And if I’m walking a little too briskly, the top easily comes off to reveal a staple graphic tee in my wardrobe.

Finally, I put on some pants! I don’t make it a habit to wear sweatpants out casually, but sometimes the weather calls for it. Hike ‘em up a bit and show off some crisp white socks, a style swerve I saw once and couldn’t help but copy.

The staple of this outfit is the heavyweight jacket. Its water-resistant lining keeps me dry when the meteorologists get the forecast wrong, and it’s lined to keep me warm as the days get shorter and the nights get longer. The two-way zipper also allows for some extra mobility, and it won’t bunch up when I sit down like so many other parkas.

#6692. Winter Essentials Worth Giving—and Keeping

Bring warmth and classic style to city days and outdoor adventures with gear from The North Face.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

It’s an oft-expressed sentiment, but it’s true: The holidays turn people’s attention toward friends, family, and community. There’s a beauty in the selfless, caring gifts people give to others. And while all that giving feels great, the holidays are also a good time to treat yourself. Plus, it’s getting colder.

Whether you equate The North Face with everyday city wear or outdoor adventures, it’s a solid, time-tested spot to get a gift for a loved one (or yourself). The brand has jackets and accessories with familiar winter looks, as well as products with less expected silhouettes that still offer protection from the elements in an understated style.

Here’s a look at some of the best cold-weather gear that will have you wrapping up the gift of warmth and durability… even if that gift is for yourself.

This is an easy gifting win because the warm, hooded, insulated jacket is a recognizable The North Face staple, evoking an unpretentious spin on the brand’s iconic Nuptse Jacket.

It’s familiar, but it’s also practical—the kind of gift you feel good about because it’s going to get a ton of use. (And it can withstand the repeated wears.) The attached hood offers protection from biting winds, and the straightforward color options make it easy to pair with a variety of outfits, whether it’s something fun or part of a heavy bundling during the coldest days of winter. This is the perfect type of winter coat: warm but not too puffy.

The Zaneck Jacket is a great alternative to the Aconcagua, stepping away from the outdoorsy look the brand is best known for. It’s still waterproof and insulated, but it has a longer, more modern cut.

The Zaneck comes with a removable faux fur lining for the hood, which, in addition to being warm, means there are a couple of different looks that can be teased out of it. The straight cuffs add to its stylish feel in a way that doesn’t scream “I’m dressed for warmth and not fashion,” even though it’s doing both.

The Lone Peak Triclimate 2 has a lot in common with the Zaneck. From a distance, they cut a similar silhouette. However, the Lone Peak adds a little versatility if you’re looking for a jacket that can handle a variety of climates.

A removable, insulated inner jacket is attached to a removable, insulated top layer, which is fit for rainy conditions or as a spring or fall jacket. The insulated layer converts the whole thing into a winter-ready jacket. It also sports underarm vents capable of releasing heat in the middle of a long hike, ideal for outdoorsy types. The hood is removable and it’s got adjustable cuffs and an adjustable hem, making this a do-it-all jacket.

It might be easy to ignore the Pertex Jacket. It’s a bit unassuming. It doesn’t cut as stylish of a figure as the Zaneck, but it’s endlessly useful. The lightweight Pertex fabric will keep you dry in a downpour, but it also offers warmth without marshmallow-like bulk.

The lightweight aspect makes this the kind of coat you toss in the backseat on the way to a hike, just in case. It’s also great for layering, whether it’s tossed on over a sweater or layered under an overcoat to make a favorite jacket more winter-ready.

This edition of the classic Base Camp Duffel echoes the design details we love in The North Face’s jackets. While it’s got enough style and a heap of color options for daily use, it’s built for adventure.

Made with water- and tear-resistant fabric, it boasts 71 liters of storage space, a roomier option to the similar 31-liter version. Like all of the brand’s products, it’s built as if the designers have been through the rigors of outdoor adventures (and they probably have). It’s got a sweeping D-zip opening, backpack straps, mesh pockets, and compression straps in case you’re curious if you can pack 73 liters worth of gear into the 71-liter space. (You know you’ve tried.)

The North Face has a lot of hats, none more iconic than this. The Salty Lined Beanie has a classic feel, fitting snuggly over the head with rib-knit texture, a cuffed shape, soft fabric, and ample color options to ensure it will match any jacket or accessory. The Salty Lined Beanie encapsulates the entire The North Face vibe in a small way: It’s stylish enough for a subway ride to work and practical enough for an outdoor adventure. Best of all, it’ll keep your ears warm in either instance.

Often, the best glove is the one that disappears into the outfit. It looks seamless, keeping hands warm without drawing too much attention. The Etip Recycled Glove is about functionality first and foremost, but the simple design and understated colors makes it look good with a variety of winter outfits.

While we all like to pretend we aren’t attached to our phones, the Etip makes a great modern gift because its stretchy fleece delivers a snug fit and the palms have touchscreen compatibility. That means you won’t have to take off your gloves to respond to that important text while the snow is falling.

Andrews, like Swift, is a WAG. The designer is married to former NHL center Jarret Stoll. #6686. Well, It Sure Seems Like Mitigating Climate Change Is No Longer a Concern for Democrats

Conservative Republicans and their oligarchical allies are getting everything they want out of a bipartisan bill that could walk back important environmental laws.

Last Thursday, and on a bipartisan basis, the Senate approved the so-called Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act (BAAJA), an alleged effort to “reform” the federal permitting process for things like energy projects, hard-rock mining, and, yes, data centers. At the very least, the bill lived up to one part of its clumsy title; its passage through the Senate truly was bipartisan. It passed by a 62–33 vote, and liberal Democratic senator Sheldon Whitehouse of Rhode Island was one of its strongest advocates.

It’s also a very big apple with more than a few razor blades concealed inside. Environmentalists have been giving BAAJA the side-eye for a number of reasons. Out in Nebraska, Democratic state chair Jane Fleming Kleeb suspects the bill is connected to a sudden revival of interest in restarting our old friend, the Keystone XL pipeline, the continent-spanning death funnel meant to bring the world’s dirtiest fossil fuel from Alberta ultimately to refineries around the world.

My problem with this whole thing is that this “kind of civil war” is taking place exclusively on the Left. Conservative Republicans, and their oligarchical allies, are forcing the action before the new Democratic majorities take over in January. They know exactly what they want out of the bill and what they don’t want in it. The president already has said there won’t be any wind farms under the provisions of this bill on his watch. (The birds! The whales!) The bill now is with the House of Representatives. May I suggest a discreet dragging of the feet? It is the holiday season, after all.

#6685. Gordon Ramsay’s Routine for a Six-Pack at 60 Includes Daily Crunches and Red-Light Therapy

Gordon Ramsay has earned 17 Michelin stars throughout his career and still oversees nearly 100 restaurants. He also fronts prime-time TV franchises on both sides of the Atlantic and holds a couple of casual Guinness World Records (call him if you need to fillet a 10-pound fish in 1 minute five seconds).

This is a man who’s never met a goalpost he didn’t want to surpass. So it wasn’t entirely surprising when he posted a bare-chested shot of his six-pack to the grid earlier this month. What generated headlines was the occasion: his 60th birthday. “Gordon Ramsay thirst trap was not on my bingo card,” reads one comment. “My wife is on this app sir,” reads another.

At home in London, he shows me the Insights section on his Instagram account, which reveals the photo has gotten over 62 million impressions. “It’s very strange,” he says. “I’ve had a lot of ribbing from my eldest daughter, Megan. She’s a police officer. She was like, ‘Dad, how can you do that to me?’ All her senior sergeants and officers were laughing and telling her to tell me they’re going for a run. They’re in their early 40s. And then my Royal Marines Commando son just sent me a message with: ‘WTF dad, what are you doing?’”

Ramsay has been a pretty active guy for a while now, despite working in an industry typically associated with chaotic diets and zero time for exercise. He was a youth soccer player back in the day, before becoming a chef and then, in his 40s, going from weighing 252 pounds to competing in marathons and Ironmans.

All very impressive, but it’s even rarer to be this chiseled in your 60s (maintaining a low body fat percentage becomes harder as your testosterone and growth hormone production slows down). Read on to learn more about his preferred forms of cardio, his favorite pre-workout treat, and why he's established a daily cryotherapy routine. “I call it my freezer,” he says.

“15 minutes a day. It starts off with a quick two-minute stretch and 100 sit-ups every morning. Precore lifestyle is my ab cruncher thing—these things are fucking amazing, by the way. If I miss a day, then I have to punish it and do double the next day.”

“It’s not physically hard with big weights. These are 25 pounds. I’ll do a strength class once a week, 45 minutes. That’s it. Occasional push-ups and squats. No personal trainer.”

“What’s helped tremendously, with no impact on any of the joints, is a lot of swimming. So I have an injury in my patella, my right knee, currently. So I just increased the swimming because that’s the only sport that can use every muscle in your body with zero impact. But cardio is off the charts.”

“I have a Zwift bike, so I jump on that for 45 minutes. There’s no impact on that. I’ll do that Monday, Wednesday, Friday and gear up for 90 minutes on a Sunday. If I wasn’t injured, I’d be doing three 5K runs again, Monday, Wednesday, Friday, 20-minute runs. I’ve got my 5K back down to 21 minutes. The dream is under 20. I was running at 19 and a half back in July. A 5K run is easy to do in the morning before work, it’s done within half an hour. Once I do that, I can gear up for either a two-hour bike on a Sunday or a 90-minute run. I alternate. So, one Sunday’s run, one Sunday’s bike.”

“10 years ago, I ruptured my Achilles. I invested in an AlterG, a beautiful running machine that takes your weight, so you get all the cardio you want and less impact on the joint.”

“From the chef’s perspective, I call it my freezer. It’s the cryochamber. I do two sets of three minutes every morning. I try to go seven days a week in that thing, straight after [working out]. That is incredible. That’ll get up to like minus 160. For six minutes.”

“I’ve invested in a tiny little red-light therapy sauna outside in the garden, and it seats two of you, and so [I’ll do] half an hour of that, every other day. This doesn’t need to be done every day. Three times a week’s fine. So when I’m not running, the other day I’ll sit in that red-light therapy and spend 20 minutes, 30 minutes in there, which is brilliant.”

“The older you get, the more you have to stretch. I use a lot of bands. And so after sitting and stretching, I had those incredible bands where you lift your feet up and pull and stretch. 5 minutes is always a nice stretch before you [work out] and then 10 minutes stretch after that. I’ve never been to a yoga class in my life.”

“I’m big on oatmeal for breakfast, Monday to Friday. I indulge Saturday, Sunday. I will do [oatmeal with] blueberry compote, caramelized banana for my potassium, and then fresh blueberries on top with some—it sounds weird, but it’s delicious—hot honey.”

“I have my friends at Precision. I’ll do one of their gels before I start that 5K, a protein gel, 30 grams, and then that sees me through the whole 45-minute routine. Coffee is just a full-fat, flat white, extra hot. Extra hot means I can sip it slowly. I cut out a lot of dairy, a lot of butter, a lot of cream. And so that treat in the morning with a flat white is that sort of, ‘I’ve earned this shit.’”

“I don’t overstress on that because I stay fit. I monitor what I eat, but if anything, I’ll do smaller portions now. I’ll involve a lot of spinach, involve a lot of salmon, chicken, especially thighs. We’re just smart with less cream, less butter intake. And then when we travel, we just keep it light.”

“I have a red wine with dinner, Californian, something light, something delicious. In 20, 30 years running restaurants, I’ve never gone around the room holding a glass of wine, holding a glass of champagne, sitting down, drinking with customers. Again, discipline.”

“Just a couple of spoons of creatine, five mornings a week. A little scoop in my water.”

“One thing I’ve learned in my 50s and my 60s—everyone said the older you get, the less you sleep. I think it’s the other way around. I like to be in bed by 11, and I’m up at 6. My Oura ring for me has become my best mate, because it just gives me the proper day-by-day indication and then keeps you on track for sleep.”

“Sign up for something. Have a target because that gets you out of bed, with a bigger, incentivized plan. I need that target twice a year, every six months. And I’ve done that since my 40th birthday. Whether it’s half an Ironman, whether it’s a marathon, half a marathon, I’ve always got something to focus on.”

“No, no, no, no, no, no. Definitely not. No. I mean, no. My meditation is Kitchen fucking Nightmares. Dealing with some of those contributors on there and having to sit with them and talk with them. But no, not very good at sitting still.”

A version of this story originally appeared in British GQ.

#6681. A Brief Guide to Grand Theft Auto VI’s Nike Sneaker Dupes

GTA 6 is finally out, which means we’ve got some in-game sneaker shopping to do. We’d already spotted Air Force 1 dupes in the trailers, but now that we can finally play the long-awaited masterpiece itself, we’re taking a closer look at what else is on the shelves. There are tons of different sneaker stores sprinkled throughout the game, and Eris—Rockstar’s fictional answer to Nike—has given us a lot to work through.

Even the name is a joke. Nike takes its name from the Greek goddess of victory, while Eris is the goddess of strife and discord—an appropriate choice for kicks you might buy with money from an armed robbery. The Swoosh has been replaced with a pointier, upside-down logo, and plenty of the sneakers baring the mark are instantly recognizable.

The Eris Rise Low is the obvious starting point. It’s the game’s AF1 equivalent, complete with the familiar paneled upper, perforated toebox, and a big, beefy sole underfoot. The Eris Strife 360, meanwhile, looks straight out of the Air Max 97 catalog. There’s the rippling upper, rounded toe, and long cushioning window underneath. Designed by Christian Tresser and released in 1997, the IRL sneaker’s silhouette took inspiration from water ripples, while the OG “Silver Bullet” finish drew on mountain bike components. Eris keeps enough of that shape for you to be able to spot it from across the shop.

Some versions get even more specific. There’s a white-and-orange Strife 360, for example, with oversized branding, contrasting lace loops, and printed side text that recalls Off-White’s own Air Max 97s. It isn’t a straight one-to-one recreation, but the reference feels pretty clear. An apparent take on the Off-White Air Force 1 Mid also appeared in the teasers. Someone at Rockstar is clearly a fan of Virgil Abloh’s work.

Then there’s the Eris Strife 90, with pairs that borrow heavily from the Air Max 90 “Infrared.” The bright red accents and sharp, aggressive lines are all there, with one colorway even adding black scribbles across the overlays.

Arguably the most iconic signature shoe of all time, the Air Jordan 1, gets a look in the game as well. The Eris Rook 1 High comes in leather and suede variations, with a tall ankle collar and laces running almost all the way up. There’s a black-and-white pair that swaps Jordan branding for an Eris basketball graphic on the tongue. You’ve probably got something quite similar in your closet at home.

The Eris Cross Trainers are, well, harder to pin down. Available in several colorways, they have a mesh base and leather overlays. One white-and-gray version gets bright green hits that nod to Nike’s signature “Volt” hue. The simpler construction puts it closer to an older Nike runner, somewhere around MD Runner 2 territory, than a direct Zoom Vomero 5 or V5 Rnr copy. And for those times that tying laces feels like too much work, there are the very appropriately named Eris Basic Slides. The broad branded strap and flat footbed are very Nike Benassi.

There are different versions to consider, too. “Origins” go all-white, while “Elite” options are matte and all-black. So if you‘ve found a silhouette you like, you can still spend another 10 minutes deciding which version works with your trousers. A problem we were hoping to leave outside the game.

It gives you an idea of just how much there is in GTA 6. The older games let you change your clothes, but here you’re choosing between different colorways, materials, and versions of individual shoes. And Eris is only one part of it: There are clothes and accessories that look like takes on Stüssy, Loewe, Chrome Hearts, and Louis Vuitton, too. You could spend a fair bit of time getting dressed before you get anything else done.

We’ve waited this long to play the game. A few more minutes in the sneaker shop before you really get going on missions won’t hurt.

A version of the story originally appeared on British GQ.

#6678. What’s a Vintage Car Rally Really Like? Inside the Louis Vuitton Dolomites Classic Run.

How I got mixed up in a race with a bunch of priceless historic automobiles and views of Italy’s spectacular Alpine scenery—and won.

In vintage rally driving, there are generally two types of competitor. Both share a passion for keeping priceless and nearly century-old vehicles in use on the open road rather than displaying them under glass. But some see the drive itself as challenge enough, while stalwarts adhere to what’s called the road book. This is a hundred or so pages of complex navigational directions that the copilot uses to navigate instead of a map, maintaining a constant flow of instructions to the driver. It is this book and how closely you follow it that decides the eventual winner.

Gianandrea Redaelli, a car nut whose family has long roots in the Italian printing business, is firmly in the “road book” camp. In early September, I climbed into the copilot seat of his 1939 Aston Martin C-Type Speed Model Roadster Bertelli—just one of two ever built, with an experimental aerodynamic body that earned it the nickname “the Flying Pea”—and stayed there as he drove six hundred kilometers of “left at the traffic lights, right at the cow” for the Louis Vuitton Dolomites Classic Run. The rally started in Venice and ended, after a scenic but challenging detour high into the Dolomite mountains, at Monza, the home of Italian Formula 1, just in time for practice day of the Grand Prix.

Venice might not seem the most logical starting line for a car rally given the proverbial wetness of the streets. But the city was chosen this year because it was also the end point of the last LV rally of its kind: the La Serenissima Run from Monte Carlo to Venice in 2012. Gianandrea won that one as copilot, with his father, Giuseppe, at the wheel. The pair also won the one before it—the Boheme across Hungary, the Czech Republic, and Austria—in 2006. This time, however, the pressure was on me too.

I have been a copilot before, and I can tell you it’s no picnic. Rally racing in vintage cars is less about speed and more about timing. It’s an exercise that requires pinpoint accuracy at the time-stamp points in the overall course and, most critically, in the regularities. These are fiendishly short time-over-distance sections in which crossing the line just a few hundredths of a second late—or early—earns strict penalties. Sensibly, the day before the race began, Gianandrea took me out in the Aston in the Veneto countryside to practice, especially those critical regularities.

I could have opted for an easy life. The rally allowed competing cars, dating from 1931 to 1969, to use the road book or follow the course using a map in a dedicated phone app. But where’s the fun in that?

“If you join a competition, you follow the regulations and strive to do your best,” Gianandrea says. “As an Italian, if you paint numbers on the door of my car, I will want to win.”

Every car in a rally like this comes with its own idiosyncrasies. On flat ground, someone driving a souped-up 2026 sports car would find these tests difficult to nail. In the reality of race conditions, the regularities often involve steep climbs or descents and unexpected corners, alling for an expert hand at the wheel—and foot on the gas pedal. The tendency of some car doors to swing open at hard bends (ours has the “suicide” doors with hinges on the back instead of the front, beloved by early carmakers) only adds to the excitement. The purpose of driver and copilot is to battle and overcome all the mechanical and computational challenges—and get our ride to the finish line bang on time.

Louis Vuitton has been involved in these glamorous and exacting vintage rallies since 1993. The Dolomites Run represents an enthusiastic return to the genre after a fourteen-year gap and, perhaps tellingly, the company’s global sponsorship (since 2025) of Formula 1. Unconfirmed rumors circulated in the field that the choice of ending the rally at a Grand Prix could well be repeated—and soon—in other countries in the future.

It’s easy, after all, to see why a fusion of style and cars with six- and seven-figure price tags might appeal to Louis Vuitton and competitors alike. Not surprisingly, at every coffee and lunch stop, the LV effect sprinkled the race with an added sense of luxury. In between, our snaking caravan carried the glamour of a century of motoring along with it. At every hairpin you got a glimpse of a gull-wing Mercedes, a 1931 Bugatti two-seater (my personal fave), and a shimmering behemoth of a Bentley in quick succession. These cars would turn heads anywhere. But in Italy, arguably the home of the sports car, they brought smiles, applause, and gasps in even the tiniest village that passed by in our slipstream. I swear I even saw a nun with a glint in her eye at one set of traffic lights.

Navigating, I was quickly learning, is not so much about providing a constant babble of information as it is knowing how to give clear and simple instructions—and only when strictly needed. As the finish neared in Monza at the end of two long days on the road, I was able to give Gianandrea the most helpful instruction of all: “Nothing for 15 kilometers.”

“That,” he very kindly replied, “is the mark of a pro.”

Even after the engines cooled, we were still clueless about how well we’d done overall. That changed at the final event, a dinner inside the Sforzesco Castle in Milan. Gianandrea stepped up, beaming, as winner of the race, and for the first time as driver. I stood somewhat sheepishly behind him as copilot as he received the trophy from Pietro Beccari, CEO of both Louis Vuitton and Louis Vuitton Fashion Group. It was an impressive swirl of Murano glass created by Venini, housed in its own equally impressive traveling case, made (naturellement) at Louis Vuitton’s atelier in Asnières.

Gianandrea has promised me visiting rights.

#6675. As an Awkward Teen, Grand Theft Auto Was My Escape. Now It Feels Like Real Life.

This morning, I woke up at 7:00 AM to play Grand Theft Auto VI just hours after its release. It’s the first new installment of the franchise since 2013; the one whose first trailer was released three years ago; the one that probably cost over a billion dollars to produce; the one set in a satirical, fictionalized south Florida; the one described in this very magazine as “the peak of entertainment.” During my first four hours with the game, I made a few hundred dollars doing odd jobs, performed several repairs on my swamp-front fixer-upper, knocked out a few sets of kettlebell swings on the deck, did favors for two friendly neighbors, and ran a mile to retrieve a freshly repainted car I left behind at a fishing spot just outside of town. I’m writing in vague terms not only to avoid spoiling the game’s plot but because, so far, it hasn’t been all that exciting. The absurd and hyperrealistic mundanity of its gameplay is, simply, astonishing, and more than I ever could have wanted from a franchise I’ve been playing for nearly a quarter of a century.

Grand Theft Auto, first released in 1997, and GTA 2, which followed two years later, were top-down driving games whose mechanics and visuals never intrigued me as a child. I wasn’t even aware of their existence until learning of the franchise’s third entry, and the first in 3D. I discovered GTA in a hotel room in Wichita Falls, TX, a small city just a stone’s throw from Oklahoma, because a classmate named Zach (or Zack) risked damaging his PS2 by toting it along on a school trip. We were there for the annual Texas Thespian Festival, an opportunity for dramatically inclined teenagers to perform plays and musicals in front of strange kids from strange schools and compete for prizes that, like most awards, quickly lost meaning after they were bestowed. After attending the required main stage productions, many of which were watered down to their school-friendly “Junior” adaptations—Into the Woods without the second act deaths and darkness, Guys & Dolls with considerably less gambling—we returned to the nearby hotel where students slept three to a room and were told to behave. For several nights we took turns playing this impossibly violent and vulgar game, five or six of us huddled around the television parked on a dresser no one used. We stole cars, killed non-player characters with assault rifles, and even completed an occasional story mission, most of which required, well, stealing cars and killing non-player characters with assault rifles. We were actors, and wasn’t gaming just another form of performance?

I don’t remember much about that particular ThesFest, but I do remember being grateful for Zack or Zach’s generosity. Before that moment, he was simply an intimidatingly charming and friendly older boy I knew best for playing Romeo in a production that our school’s theater director chose to set during the Vietnam War for reasons he was unable to articulate. (In that, my first stage role, I was cast as Juliet’s unnamed little brother, a role that doesn’t exist in the original text.) Of course I’d played video games before that weekend—Myst, Rollercoaster Tycoon and a Titanic-themed mystery were my favorites at that point—but never before had I experienced something so immersive or graphically impressive. It was loud and exciting, a simulation of real life at its most extreme and chaotic. When I got home, I knew the console–and GTA 3–had to be mine. My life, laser-focused on math homework and our theater director’s post-apocalyptic interpretation of Antigone, could have really used a thrill.

I traded in my PlayStation, a Christmas gift from years earlier on which I played mostly rented or borrowed adventure games, and used money I’d saved from mowing lawns (my family’s own, and those of a few elderly neighbors), to pay the balance. The only game I wanted was GTA III, and it’s the only one I played for months. The novelty was the thing. There was a story, of course: some nameless protagonist with a chiseled, low-polygon face committing crimes and various acts of violence to achieve a goal of… staying alive? Perhaps that’s the goal of most games, maybe even the goal of life itself. I skipped cutscenes in favor of driving from one glowing cylindrical marker to the next, shooting police officers in the head and picking up sex workers who did unseen, noisy things to my character that made the stolen cars bounce up and down like a lowrider with hydraulics. I’d largely missed the groundbreaking graphics of the N64 era—having never played Zelda or Mario Kart or GoldenEye outside the bedrooms of childhood friends with bigger houses and never-ending supplies of soda in the fridge—which meant my first truly passionate dive into console gaming was filled with manic, gratuitous violence. The original PlayStation was for catching troublemaking monkeys in nets and flying around gentle landscapes as a purple dragon. The PS2, though, was for imagining a life of crime. That my parents allowed me to play these games at all still surprises me, but I suppose to them anything was better than me getting drunk at parties I knew were happening but was never invited to.

By the time Grand Theft Auto: Vice City was released the following year, I had a part time job at a local AMC, so I bought the game with money earned selling popcorn and cleaning that same popcorn off the floor. Playing it was a reward for completing an essay on Ernest Hemingway or telling multiple moviegoers that we couldn’t give refunds simply because they “didn’t like” Men In Black 2. The game’s soundtrack introduced me to songs that still activate memories sitting cross-legged on the carpet of my bedroom, turning the volume down as low as I could to prevent my father from stepping inside and saying, gravely, “Am I going to have to turn that off?” To this day, I can’t hear Laura Branagin’s “Self Control” without conjuring Tommy Vercetti, the protagonist of Grand Theft Auto: Vice City, driving a motorcycle down a sparsely populated road flanked by neon-lit palm trees. For months, I listened to more in-game radio than the local Top 40 station, unaware at the time that its curated period playlists would shape my musical appetite for the rest of my life.

The third installment of that PS2 trilogy, Grand Theft Auto: San Andreas, came out two years later, when I was a freshman in college. I was 18, adjusting to life in a new city, and trying desperately to make friends. The game was less a reward for hard work than a balm when I didn’t feel like socializing, a distraction from my countless first semester anxieties. By the time I completed its main story, life away from home had become normal. I had new friends, new obligations, new problems. I used my PS2 as a DVD player almost exclusively, watching several movies a week, all borrowed from Netflix (during its original red-envelope form) and the school library. By the time GTA 4 was released, I’d lost interest in gaming altogether. We were in a recession, and I was hellbent on keeping my very first real job. In fact, I didn’t play another Grand Theft Auto game until 2013, on a PS3 Slim (the console’s discounted, late-generation entry), weeks after my now-husband and I moved into our first apartment together. In the time since my last GTA experience, I’d come out of the closet, moved across the country, started a new career, adopted my first cat, and made an entirely new group of close friends. A franchise that was once a distraction from teenage malaise was now pure, unadulterated fun. Gone were the days of playing it at a low volume–I turned it all the way up, and didn’t skip cut scenes. I was there for the story as well as the spectacle.

13 years later, the longest ever interval between new installments, my relationship with gaming has evolved alongside the games themselves. It’s not enough to be distracted; being distracted is now our default state. Disturbances arise, whether seeking them or not: a text message in the middle of a terrible Peacock show; a news alert regarding a global calamity about which you can do nothing but panic; the jittering beeps of FaceTime call while reading a wonderful novel, or that mindless, habitual scroll through a feed that shows videos meant to be seen but not remembered. Lately I’ve turned to gaming for total immersion, for sensations of genuine awe that might keep me from succumbing to the dopamine rush of my phone. I spent much of the summer replaying Kingdom Come Deliverance and its sequel, a pair of life simulators set in medieval Europe. In those games, every action has a consequence, and every success is hard-fought and requires patience.

After several hours in its impossibly dense world, GTA 6 seems to be encouraging a similar sense of duty. My real life is fine, often even good. It’s the kind of life I thought impossible when the franchise first entered my life nearly a quarter century ago. I’m not bowled over by the game because it’s better or even more fun than the life I live outside Vice City—there are gun-toting mass murderers in both, after all—but because it’s totally different. Even when you’re comfortable in your own life, isn’t it extraordinary to enter another? The games have always been about adults, and for adults. It says so right on the box. Since 2002, their goals have fundamentally remained the same: get a job, make money, stay alive. As a teenager, its simulated depravity was funny and exciting, with graphics and gameplay so vivid I could feel like a kid again. But now, at 40? With 20-plus years of GTA under my belt and a life 16-year-old me would find more unbelievable than the functionalities of the PS5 Pro? No game has ever made me feel older. What a privilege that is.

#6673. The Art of the Winter Layer

Use these cold-weather staples from The North Face for practical warmth with polished style.

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Like any specialized skill set, winter layering is one that must be continually practiced and perfected, lest your talents wane. It’s not as easy as throwing on a pair of cashmere leggings and a coordinating hoodie—that’s only the beginning. From there, it takes a careful curation of hero pieces (like puffer jackets and parkas), as well as a cast of supporting details (hats, gloves, and high-performance footwear), to layer effectively and aesthetically in the harshest conditions.

That said, even the most adept artist is nothing without an arsenal of trusted creative tools. And in these bitter temps, you’ll want to reach for the best, which essentially means any piece of gear with The North Face’s iconic rectangular logo attached to it. The storied brand has dominated the outdoor space for decades, having perfected the art of elevated style at elevated altitudes.

As we head into the chilliest season of the year, look to The North Face’s vast selection of dependable staples for all your layering needs. Designed both for performance and appearances, these pieces are in it for the long run.

Whether painting or dressing, it’s essential to start any creative endeavor with a blank canvas made of quality materials. A good base should be versatile and simple to pair with a host of helpful staples. Long-sleeve tees, cashmere turtlenecks, and crewneck sweatshirts, for example, are all good jumping-off points.

If you’re looking for a high-performance piece with enough style power to stand on its own, we recommend the Women’s Chabot 2.0 Mock-Neck. This cozy layering top offers the same reliable comfort of a traditional insulated thermal with a few modern updates. Though understated by design, its delightfully oversized fit and a funky waffle knit texture make the basic feel intentional and interesting.

For a closer-fitting base, the Women’s Golden Ridge Ribbed Long-Sleeve Tee layers easily under other pieces. Its slim fit and ribbed texture give this practical layer a polished look, while thumbholes at the cuffs add a functional detail. Bonus points for the tee’s anti-odor technology that helps keep the fabric feeling fresh.

Women’s Golden Ridge Ribbed Long-Sleeve Tee

After you’ve selected the first layer, the next stop should be your coat closet. This is where you’ll select the hero of your look. While each piece of the layering puzzle is uniquely important, the power jacket is a non-negotiable. The right iteration should supply a thick layer of warmth, while also doing its part to make the outfit, as a whole, feel cohesive.

Those who crave a more subdued winter aesthetic will love the classic appeal of the Women’s Arctic Bomber. The waterproof winter jacket boasts all the utility of a parka, with the sleek design details most commonly found in trend-focused outerwear. In four comforting colorways, this winter coat is an insulation powerhouse in a chic, wearable package.

Look to the Women’s Hydrenalite Down Jacket for a more classic puffer. This work-horse is a versatile down layer that can be slipped on at any time to keep you warm for any duration that you spend outdoors, whether you are on the mountain or running around town.

For the more artistically inclined, try the Women’s Yumiori Off Peak Full Zip Jacket. This fleece outer layer offers unfailing warmth and a distinctly sporty flavor. Its two-tone color palette, meanwhile, adds a fun, whimsical feel to the utilitarian, zip-up-style silhouette.

During the summer months, it’s all about the mani-pedi. But once that icy weather blows in, self-care starts to look a bit different. The North Face’s Etip Gloves are basically a hand treatment on the go. Crafted from soft, stretchy fleece made with 93% recycled polyester, these tech-friendly gloves will keep your hands comfortable on cold-weather outings. They’ll also keep you connected: Touchscreen compatibility lets you check your phone without taking them off, while silicone grip dots on the palms help you hold onto your device as well as smaller essentials like keys and credit cards.

For footwear, the Women’s THERMOBALL Traction Mule V will quickly become the standout of your seasonal lineup. These cloud-like slip-ons pair warm insulation with a quilted upper that gives them a casual, streetwear-cool vibe. The rubber outsole adds traction for quick trips outdoors.

The minimalist philosophy requires you to take off one item before leaving the house. But during the cold weather season, it’s in your best interest to do the opposite. As you walk out the door, make sure you grab a beanie since most of your body heat can escape from your head.

Beanies are simultaneously the most underappreciated outfit addition and the most indispensable. The North Face’s Wool Blend Beanie, for instance, pairs the warmth of its 65% merino wool knit with a refined, metropolitan style. Its classic cuffed design and textured knit add visual interest, while its deep fit offers roomier coverage. If you prefer the rectangular logo with the same cuffed look, you can never go wrong with the rib-knit and soft lining of the Salty Lined Beanie. On the slopes or on your morning commute, either will add a polished finishing touch.

The importance of a functional winter hat cannot be overstated—think of it as the final brushstroke in the art of winter layering.
