#6671. This Piping Hot Zenith Collaboration Explains the Future of the Watch Market

This is an edition of the newsletter Box + Papers, Cam Wolf’s weekly deep dive into the world of watches. Sign up here.

In early July, I met with Zenith’s chief product officer Romain Marietta over Zoom to get a sneak peek at a watch that wouldn’t come out until November. The latest Zenith G.F.J., made in partnership with the Los Angeles-based independent watchmaker J.N. Shapiro, is a gorgeous timepiece that combines the former’s mechanical know-how with Shapiro’s meticulously handmade dials. Over four months before the watch was even unveiled to the public, Marietta shared that 20 of the 25 pieces produced had already been sold. Of all the releases from the major Swiss brands in 2026, none better represent the future of luxury watchmaking than this high-end, fussily made, independent-adjacent, sold-out-before-you-ever-heard-about-it G.F.J.

Zenith recently announced that it’s laying off roughly half of its current workforce as it attempts to shift its position in the market. “All of us in our segment, which is described as mid-range, are going through a very difficult period,” Zenith CEO Benoit De Clerck told staff as he explained the restructuring, according to Le Temps. The brand has been shifting upmarket since last year, when it released a rainbow gem-set version of its flagship sport watch. The G.F.J. is the cornerstone of that new business. As the economy tilts even further in favor of the uber-rich, it’s also altering where the bulk of the profits come from for watchmakers. “Wealthy people are still wealthy, and even more wealthy,” Marietta said. “It’s easier [now] to sell high-end watches than watches for 3,000 to 5,000 Swiss francs.”

That’s true. Lorel Monroy, a sales associate at LA’s Feldmar Watch Company, tells me there’s “definitely been an uptick” in interest around Zenith this year. Not for the brand’s flagship Chronomaster, which remains firmly in the starter luxury watch zone at $12,300, but for the far more expensive G.F.J., which starts at $49,900. “The luxury direction is the game changer,” Monroy said. Feldmar only received three G.F.J.s to allocate in 2026 and they were all quickly snapped up. There’s now a waitlist building at the retailer for the upscale model.

When it comes to the G.F.J., Zenith has acted like MacGyver, finding endless uses for its smash-hit model. First released in the spring of 2025, the watch received two very pretty, well-received sequels earlier this year. In March, I got to test out the new onyx-dial version for just a few hours before I had to send it along to Hollywood stylist Fabio Immediato, who promptly strapped it on Simu Liu’s wrist for the Oscars.

Over the summer, Zenith also launched this double-signed program, which allows the brand to work with independent makers like J.N. Shapiro. Before this latest release, the Swiss brand linked up with Japan’s beloved Naoya Hida.

That watch was a fascinating representation of the project—the spotlessly clean dial was straight out of the latter’s workshop. Zenith, a historic brand backed by LVMH, ceded control entirely to the small Japanese indie. It was a massive success. To allocate the pieces, Marietta said they narrowed the list to 10 clients, mainly from the Naoya Hida side, then sent an email that included the price tag of 60,000 Swiss francs but zero images of the watch. “They said yes right away, trusting us with the design,” Marietta said. “And that’s a great testimony of how the trust can be at that level when you buy a piece for [$75,000] without even seeing it, not even a picture.”

In the topsy-turvy world of watches in 2026, it’s much sexier to be an independent brand than one in the stable of the world’s largest luxury conglomerate. Marietta calls the G.F.J. “an independent brand within our global lineup,” pointing to the high-level finishing and historic movement inside the piece—the type of nerdy details that appeal to the most seasoned collectors driving the industry. “The G.F.J. collection itself is bringing us new clients,” Marietta said. “It's attracting clients who did not really have Zenith on their radar. These guys were more A. Lange & Söhne clients or with independents.”

Zenith created irresistible bait for this new stripe of collectors. The new J.N. Shapiro-designed G.F.J. is absolutely gorgeous. The partners leverage the LA independent’s dedication to creating its dials by hand through a technique known as engine turning. I got to visit Shapiro’s workshop a few years ago and was charmed by the contrast of the brand’s cavernous industrial warehouse and the row of almost medieval-looking engine-turning machines lined up at the front of the room.

The dials of the new collaborative watches were painstakingly made on those very machines—on which a single mistake means tossing the whole thing in the bin—and then shipped off to Switzerland, where Zenith watchmakers installed them into cases. There are three different patterns on the dial, including a basketweave at the center and Shapiro’s signature invention, the Infinity Weave, inside the subdial. Much like on the Naoya Hida version, the dial is all Shapiro. Marietta notes, however, that it already resembles the brick pattern seen on the original G.F.J., which is based on the exterior of Zenith’s manufacture in Le Locle.

Zenith plans to release two collaborative versions of the G.F.J. annually moving forward. According to Marietta, a couple of years down the road, the brand may allow their indie partners to go beyond the dial and play with the case and movement as well.

With the G.F.J., Zenith has created something that is tailor-made for today’s collectors. Seven-figure pieces are currently booming at auction, and it would take Moses himself to part the money flooding into the market right now. These deep-pocketed collectors are most interested in watches from independent makers: Auction houses have sold more million-dollar F.P. Journe watches this year than they have from either Rolex or Patek Philippe. As collectors search for the next hot watchmaker, they’re finding exactly what they’re looking for in a 161-year-old Swiss luxury brand.

#6670. ‘The Gilded Age’ Just Used a TV Trope We Really Need to Retire

Spoilers for The Gilded Age season 4 ahead.

Oh, Marian. Marian, Marian, Marian. We finally got to see her letting loose, and then it all came crashing down. Hard. As you may have seen coming from a mile away, on Sunday night’s fourth episode of The Gilded Age season 4, Marian realized that she was pregnant (at least she assumes she is). After a one-night stand. I think I’ve seen this show before—and it was created and written by the same person.

The season 4 premiere shows Marian Brook going out on the town with her ex-fiancé Larry Russell following Enid and Oscar’s wedding. They have a great time dancing, and then they have a great time at a hotel after bad weather means they can’t make the trip back uptown. Marian literally lets her Victorian-era hair down and loses her virginity to the man she loves—or at least, loved.

The aftermath is not fun, though. Marian thinks of herself as a ruined woman for having premarital sex, and Larry offers to get re-engaged to her—a fine idea considering they both still have feelings for each other—but she sees it as a pity re-proposal and refuses him. A few episodes later, she’s having nosebleeds all over the place and admitting to a colleague at the settlement house that she hasn’t had her “monthly courses.” (We’ll get to more on why I don’t like this storyline, but I will give the show a shoutout for using the underrepresented pregnancy symptom of nosebleeds rather than the classic barfing everywhere.)

This could have been different. Marian, a character who has had happiness snatched out of her hands time and time again, could have just stubbornly continued to turn down Larry and devoted herself to her work, only to realize by the season finale that she actually is still in love with him. I wouldn’t have minded a good old slow burn here! Much of this show moves so fast. Marian and Larry’s romance could’ve kept things more grounded.

But, instead, we get the classic TV trope of “woman gets pregnant after having sex with someone once.” And this particular situation has the added bonus of “and she was a virgin.” Shows as wide-ranging as Friends, Mad Men, The Secret Life of the American Teenager, Reign, Ginny & Georgia, and The Originals have featured one-night stands that lead to pregnancies to varying levels of success. The trope is supposed to make us gasp, but at this point, it’s just kind of cheap and predictable.

Most notably, this already happened on Downton Abbey, which, like The Gilded Age, was written and created by Julian Fellowes. The character Edith—who is very similar to Marian in that she repeatedly gets into doomed relationships with men and devotes herself to work at a time when that’s not the norm for aristocratic women—gets pregnant after sleeping with her love interest, Michael Gregson, one time. As with The Gilded Age, given the time period of the show, this was not happy news and was just another blow to a character who kept getting the short end of the stick. (Speaking of the short end of the stick, Michael Gregson goes out of the country following their lovemaking and dies while away, so Edith never even gets to see him again!)

The whole “Poor Edith” thing became something of a running joke for Downton fans and on the actual show, which was entertaining, but seeing something so similar happen again on The Gilded Age just makes me wish this had gone in a more creative direction. Maybe Marian could’ve kept hooking up with Larry despite the side of herself that knew it was a bad idea. Maybe Marian could’ve sworn off of men altogether. (Honestly, it’d make sense at this point! I mean, damn, that’s three failed engagements!) Anything but pregnancy.

So, where does Poor Marian go from here? My guess, it’ll either be: 1. She isn’t actually pregnant, but the stress from her guilt caused the nosebleeds and missed “monthly courses.” 2. She has the baby and secretly puts it up for adoption a la Edith from Downton. 3. She gets back with Larry, has the baby, and Bertha does some of her conniving to get society to not ask questions about the timeline. 4. She has a miscarriage.

Are any of these options that great? Is this going to be worth it? A miscarriage would mean it was just for the shock (or, “shock”) of the episode 4 cliffhanger. A baby with Larry would be forcing them together more than the guilt of the one-night stand.

Despite my critique of this particular storyline, I love this show, so I am interested to see how it turns out. The creativity might not have been there when it came to using this trope again, but hopefully, it’ll be there in the outcome.

#6669. Bella Hadid’s Radical Vulnerability

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

How do you stay open in an industry that demands toughness? Bella Hadid embarks on a new chapter with a renewed sense of optimism and her boundaries firmly in place.

I first met Bella Hadid on a fashion shoot I was styling more than a decade ago. We went on to shoot several stories together, including her first major American magazine cover. On one of those shoots, she arrived upset because she had just found out she didn’t get cast in the Victoria’s Secret show. I will never forget telling Bella that one day, Victoria’s Secret would be begging her to walk for them. Cut to today, and Bella is not only one of the most in-demand models, but she opened their most recent show. When I saw her hit the runway in a beaded floral bra and matching wings, I let out a scream because I knew what that moment meant to her.

A few days after the Victoria’s Secret show in October, Bella and I sat down to reflect on her career so far, forgiveness and success, turning 30, and where she goes from here. I have always felt connected to Bella because she, like me, is a heart-on-her-sleeve kind of gal. She is deeply vulnerable and human, and it’s what makes her one of the most compelling figures in fashion today.

Samira Nasr: This is a full-circle moment. On one of our first shoots together, you were really upset because you didn’t get the Victoria’s Secret show. And I said to you, “One day, they will be begging you to be in their show.” And I could tell you didn’t believe me. Your whole body shut down. And three days ago, you opened Victoria’s Secret. Holy shit.

Bella Hadid: I still don’t believe you, to be honest. I feel like people are just AI-ing me onto the runway. I think that it’s so crazy how time passes and you still have some kind of impostor syndrome. No matter how hard you work, there’s always another girl who is more deserving, more beautiful, and has worked so hard to be able to get that spot. Ten years ago, I remember that moment too, where I never thought I would get the show, but I think that God always has a plan, and I truly believe at that time I wasn’t ready. I don’t think I’ve been ready until this year, to be honest. In past years, I was going to my horse-show finals in the morning, flying straight to the Victoria’s Secret show that day, doing rehearsal right before, and then pulling what we call a Bella, and Belinda comes out, and we just act. But this year, I felt really strong.

I have had to make a decision for myself to have boundaries with my own health. And I think it’s gotten me to this point now where I’m able to do the jobs that I do want to do with people that I love, that I can feel inspired by. And that’s truly what [working in] fashion is about: being around people you feel inspired by. Until you find that inspiration, it’s hard to go back into our industry. I think I needed a little bit of time just to be able to be like, “Let me just remember why I’m doing this again,” because I know I’m more than just a face.

SN: We don’t really support a culture of care. I have always believed that boundaries are bridges.

BH: They really are. We know so many people who have been working for 30, 40, 50 years in this industry with zero boundaries. I’ve watched people over the years just get more depressed because they don’t know themselves and they don’t know who they are. And that’s how I was for 10 years, when I didn’t know what my favorite color was, what I liked to eat. I stopped listening to music. To not be able to know yourself in an industry that tells you how to be every single day is really hard.

SN: Going back to Victoria’s Secret, did you feel empowered, or was that your alter ego? What is her name again?

BH: Belinda. You know what? Fortunately and unfortunately, I think it was Bella this year.

BH: I made up my alter ego, Belinda, a long time ago to deal with the intensity of everything happening in my life. So she could deal with that, and I can deal with this, and we can do this together, which I’m realizing I should probably go to therapy about again. Belinda is the one that people are like, “Wow, this, that.”

I have POTS [postural orthostatic tachycardia syndrome], and I have a lot of PMDD [premenstrual dysphoric disorder], endometriosis, plus my Lyme and things that affect my body very deeply. Right before I walked out on the runway for my opening look, I went to go stretch and my whole clavicle completely … I don’t even know what it was. My lungs almost collapsed, and I couldn’t breathe, and then it was like, “Five, four …” So my body goes through these different motions of feeling really good and then all of a sudden crashing on me at the worst moments. I need to realize maybe my body can’t handle all that I have going on. Because I know how to walk. One thing I can do is I can strut. But it’s a lot of pressure; we all have our own insecurities. Sometimes my body just can’t connect with my brain. My brain wants to do it, and my body is like, “No, we can’t do that today.” And I’m like, “Okay, great. So let’s figure out another way.” But I think people don’t realize how much I struggle with the fatigue and the body pain, and so I just try my best.

SN: You’ve achieved so much in your 20s. You just turned 30. What are you looking forward to in your 30s?

BH: It’s funny, today I woke up with—I wouldn’t say the word is anxiety, but this feeling of “Oh my gosh, my life is starting.” I want to get rid of what doesn’t bring me joy and bring in what does. I’ve tried to put myself in positions for work for a very long time that have made me feel out of body or like I don’t belong. Years ago, I was just grateful to get a job, and I would say yes to everything. I lacked boundaries then, but I do believe that that gave me the strength to be able to have the confidence in my own boundaries now. I’ve achieved pretty much all the goals that I wanted to when it comes to fashion as a model. And now I do want to go into other ventures to eventually get to peace and happiness and love within this universe that God has put me in.

In the next couple of years, it’s just about getting back to community, getting back to using my voice again. I got beaten down pretty heavy for a long time, which pushed me into this very big struggle with my physical and emotional health, which makes my brain turn off and makes me not be able to write or read. When you’re not around enough people that are like-minded, you feel really ostracized. That’s almost where I got to. Of course, I always want to speak from my heart, which I hope will never disrespect anybody, but still be able to have the confidence to speak my truth and hope that people will walk beside me along the way when it comes to that.

SN: Is there something you’re most proud of achieving?

BH: Well, first and foremost, starting [my fragrance line] Orebella was something that I truly needed for my own creativity and to be able to build community. I am not a 50/50 person. I’m an all-in type of girl. Being able to move forward into my 30s, really focusing on building the things that I’ve started—Orebella was something that I pushed so hard for since 2018, and the fact that it’s really now coming to fruition makes me so proud.

When I was 17, going to my first go-see with a stylist I will not name, I was basically told I was too big to wear Saint Laurent and needed to lose a couple of pounds. And that’s when I was just a baby coming out of high school. Twelve years later, now I’m the face of Saint Laurent. I’m the face of Prada, which is incredible and insane. Times have changed so much, and the people that have come into the industry to make it a better place for us as models are so important. Not that that comment ever impacted me. I went through enough struggles with my body before that, so by the time she said that, I was like, “Okay, girl, I’m actually coming out of something that I dealt with very heavily when it came to my body. And now I look at food in a different way, and I love my body, and that’s okay if you think that I don’t look the way that you think I should look.”

I just would love for everyone to think about if every day they had to walk outside and be photographed, what that would feel like, because it’s an uncomfortable feeling for anybody. My face would change so much. I would be super puffy one day, and one day I would look yellow and emaciated, and people would think I was on drugs or doing this or that. And when anybody knows about, one, hormones, just as a woman in general, and two, about immune problems and how weight fluctuates—again, I just think that there needs to be more sensitivity in our industry.

SN: I’m curious if you’ve always been this person—so sensitive, empathetic—from a little girl.

BH: I’ve always felt like my sensitivity and my openness were a disservice to myself because I felt like no one else was like me in those ways, so I shut it down. Now I realize that there is a generosity of thoughtfulness that is so much deeper than just buying somebody an expensive gift. I think sitting on the side of the street with somebody, even if you don’t have any money, is more important than just walking by without caring.

SN: You’re able to access compassion when a lot of people can’t, and that is a superpower. I want to talk about style because you mentioned your archive. I remember you once mentioned you collect Gaultier. Do you still collect?

BH: I do collect. Behind me are bags to sell. But downstairs, I have my Gaultier dress that the atelier made. It was a Naomi Campbell dress that she had worn in one of their shows, and I was obsessed with it. I called their PR, and within a week and a half, because that dress was in a museum, I had it made and sent to Cannes, and that was that white-and-black dress that I wore on the carpet. And so, that’s here. My Coperni dress is here. I have a whole section for Virgil [Abloh].

SN: The last time I saw Virgil was with you, actually.

BH: I don’t think I’ve done a real, full Fashion Month since he passed away. And Vivienne [Westwood] and Karl [Lagerfeld] passed, and I felt like all of my idols were leaving, and it felt really scary. I have all the art Virgil gave me, his letter he wrote me, his bags, his shoes he made me. And then I have my Prada area, my Cavalli area, my Bob Mackies that I’ve been collecting over the years.

BH: Hopefully for a future wedding. There are just some dresses you can’t find again. And Bob did it best.

SN: He’s an American icon. I wanted to talk to you about Cannes. You’re such a queen of Cannes now, and I look forward to seeing you there. Is it fun?

BH: This past year was fun. I felt grounded. To do the red carpet and feel, in those moments, sober and happy and healthy and strong, and go to sleep not having anxiety about going out or how I’m going to look the next day, and just being with loved ones. It did really well for me this year.

Cannes has always held a very special place in my heart. It begins long before I arrive. Working closely with designers on custom gowns, exchanging references, and watching those first sketches come to life is such a beautiful part of the experience.

This year, being there to support Marie-Clémentine Dusabejambo’s Ben’Imana, the first Rwandan film in the official selection, was especially meaningful. It explores memory and reconciliation after the genocide against the Tutsi through women whose lives carry its legacy. That’s what I love about cinema: It brings us closer and gives stories too rarely heard the attention they deserve. You can enter a theater knowing nothing of someone’s world and leave carrying a little of it with you.

SN: I look at you and how hard you’ve worked for so long, from our first job together. To me, it feels like you’ve achieved so much. But what does success look like for you? Do you feel you’ve achieved it?

BH: I talked to my business manager yesterday, so that made me feel a little bit more successful. But when you don’t think about the money part of it, I think success truly comes from peace within. At the end of the day, I really don’t need much. That’s why I do give back so much, because a lot of the money that I do make, I don’t feel deserving of. There are so many people in the world, whether it’s doctors or teachers, that I think deserve my pay rate a lot more than I do.

From an external space, success is about how known you are, how much you make, how many times you can be on TV, how many covers you’re on, how many fashion shows you do. And that is really important in our industry, especially when you’re building. But for me, I am different. Success, for me, can be living in peace, in a small town, with a man that I love, with my child, having my animals. I’ve always wanted to start a farm-to-table bakery, with flowers, and everything comes from the community. I want to do things that are fulfilling for me in my soul: having a greenhouse that I can garden in, having my glassblowing and ceramic studio, and having lots of children and being a mother. It wasn’t a dream that I’ve always had, but it’s something that now is so intense for me. My intuition is screaming so loudly at me about it.

I think knowing there is a reason behind everything is so important. My sister and I donated a million dollars to Palestine last year [the donation was split between HEAL Palestine, Palestine Children’s Relief Fund, World Central Kitchen, and United Nations Relief and Works Agency], which we could have never been able to do without the success that we’ve had.

God gave me the platform to be able to speak about the things that I hold dearly to me. And when it comes to Palestine or Sudan or Congo or Rwanda or places that mean so much to not only myself but the people around me—to give people a voice in any capacity, that’s success for me as well. To make my family wide and far proud, or to see themselves in me, it gives me strength.

#6668. Martina Mondadori on the Secret to Hosting a Truly Memorable Dinner Party

#6667. I’m Sure Jensen Huang’s Reinstatement Has Nothing to Do with His $25 Million Donation to Trump’s Ballroom

Why else would the president involve himself so conspicuously?

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Remember, people. You can’t spell invidious without Nvidia.

Well, I’m certainly reassured. What the hell? Bring back the sex machine in the leather jacket.

And the conspicuous involvement of the president in this whole thing makes the cynical mind think of cheap shenanigans at the highest level, conducted under the first rule of the president’s career: “Me some too, yes?” I’m sure the $25 million Huang donated to the ballroom had nothing to do with the president’s patronage.

That’s very sweet and all, but the fact remains that the chips are still in China, where they can be used in weapons and Christ knows what else. The same thought seems to have occurred to Senator Professor Warren, soon to be chair of the Senate Banking, Housing, and Urban Affairs Committee.

The president marked his latest triumph by posting an AI-generated adaptation of the famous picture of him from Butler, Pennsylvania, with Jensen Huang in his place. Instead of a raised fist, Huang is raising a red brassiere.

#6666. So Many Dyson Hair Tools Are on Sale Right Now—These Are Cosmo Editors’ 6 Favorites

4 beauty editors. 6 Dyson tools. Here’s our official ranking.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

As we haircare lovers all know, Dyson has truly solidified itself as the crème de la crème of buzzy hair tools. From the viral Airwrap i.d. to the OG Supersonic Hair Dryer, Dyson = high-tech hairstyling. Despite the higher price points, these tools are everywhere these days, from your favorite creator doing their hair on your TikTok feed to your hairstylist doing your hair in the salon chair. It seems like everyone loves a Dyson product (and that includes pretty much every Cosmo editor too). They’ve won tonssss of Cosmo beauty awards, including a 2025 Holy Grail Beauty Award and 2026 Reader’s Choice Beauty Award for the Airwrap, back-to-back wins in the Reader’s Choice Beauty Awards in 2024 and 2025 for the Supersonic, and many, many more.

However, at $250-$500 a pop, it’s not like most people can afford to own every single Dyson tool out there. So which ones are the most worth the hundreds of dollars they cost? Luckily, we’ve here at Cosmo have tested all of ‘em—these are the ones we truly recommend spending money on.

Why we love it: Despite so many Airwrap dupes out there, us Cosmo editors keep going back to the original Dyson hair styling tool that changed the game forever. Talk about true innovation—the Airwrap created a whole new category of styling products that dry, curl, volumize, straighten, and blow out hair all in one handy tool that, oh by the way, uses hot air instead of high heat that could potentially damage your hair more.

It’s also on rare sale right now—23 percent off—for Black Friday and Cyber Monday, so if you’ve been thinking of scooping one up, this is your time to shine. Run, don’t walk!

One Cosmo editor says: “If the Airwrap has zero fans, I’m dead. For years, I didn’t know how to style my hair. The round-brush-and-dryer combo was a lot for me, I didn’t know how to curl my hair, and quite honestly, all I ever did was straighten it with a flat iron (which was perfect for the mid-2010s style at the time). But when I got my hands on my first Airwrap, that all changed. Suddenly, I could do it all, and I was imparting wayyy less heat damage onto my strands than if I had been using traditional hot tools. The i.d. is great because the it has all the attachments I need, it dries and styles my thick, wavy hair super quickly, it eliminates frizz, and keeps my style in place all day.” —CATHARINE MALZAHN, CONTRIBUTING BEAUTY WRITER

Why we love it: The one that started it all and turned hair dryers from a humble styling tool into a must-have item... Dyson’s Supersonic dryer launched 10 years ago in 2016, yet it still remains one of the best hair dryers out there. And honestly, it’s not hard to see why. Beyond the basics you’d be looking for (long cord, negative ions, multiple heat and speed settings, relatively lightweight, that kind of thing), its powerful airflow dries hair noticeably quickly while the dryer monitors the temperature 40 times a second to prevent extreme heat on your strands.

It’s pretty straightforward and easy to use, making a salon-worthy blowout at home feel a whole lot less intimidating. A decade later, the Supersonic is proof that some beauty investments really do stand the test of time.

One Cosmo editor says: “This is the first Dyson hair tool I ever used, and I still think it's probably the most worth-it tool price-wise if you wanted to splurge on one thing. I used to hate blow dryers until I tried this one. It's quick and powerful without sacrificing the health of my hair. This fully dries my hair in under two minutes (yes, two), but it doesn't leave my hair crunchy or fried either.” —BETH GILLETTE, BEAUTY EDITOR

Why we love it: Straightening without hot plates? And you can use it on wet hair? Sign me up. The Dyson Airstrait uses airflow to dry and straighten hair at the same time, meaning you can skip the blow-dryer-and-flat-iron combo entirely. Perfect if you’re trying to cut down on styling time in the morning!

Unlike traditional flat irons, the Airstrait doesn’t use heated plates. Instead, it uses airflow to smooth and dry hair, delivering a soft, naturally straight finish with movement rather than that super-flat, pin-straight look. Plus, it has a dry mode for touching up your style between washes. When our editors with varying curl patterns (from wavy and fine to 4b curls) tested it, it straightened with ease and speed.

One Cosmo editor says: “I have an interesting hair type because while it’s curly and fine, I have a lot of it. It can take a very long time for me to dry my hair, but the Airstrait cut my styling time dramatically. This tool takes my hair from damp to fully dry in under 10 minutes. However, it did take a slight learning curve at first.” —MARY HONKUS, CONTRIBUTING BEAUTY WRITER

Why we love it: Meet the Dyson hair dryer that had three Cosmo beauty editors with three different hair types completely obsessed. The Dyson Supersonic r takes everything we love about the original Supersonic and makes it lighter, smaller, and easier to maneuver, thanks to its curved, ergonomic design. In fact, it’s so lightweight that it weighs less than a pound, making it perfect for professional stylists as well as anyone who doesn’t want an arm workout just to dry hair.

Speaking of which, your arms probably won’t get super tired because of how quickly it works. Beauty director Lauren Balsamo said it dried her soaking-wet hair in just six minutes(!!!). Add in the intelligent attachments that automatically adjust heat and airflow and remember those settings for the next time you use it, the multiple attachments, and the compact size, and it’s easy to be willing to overlook the hefty price tag.

One Cosmo editor says: “The first thing I noticed when I picked up the Supersonic R was the weight, or rather, the lack thereof. It seriously feels like you're holding a bottle of hairspray—that’s how airy and easy it is to handle. Then came the assembly. The magnetic attachments snapped on effortlessly. And as a wavy-haired girl who can either lean curly or reach for a round brush for a bouncy blowout, I really appreciated the range of attachments for everything from creating curls to smoothing flyaways.” —JASMINE HYMAN, ASSISTANT BEAUTY EDITOR

Why we love it: Dyson finally made a one-step blow dryer brush, and at $250, it’s the brand’s lowest-priced hair tool (though it’s still an investment!). Like the Airwrap, the Dyson Airsmooth is another wet-to-dry styler, using powerful airflow to dry and smooth strands without extreme heat. Its teardrop shape helps you get closer to the root for more lift and volume, while the bristles are designed to minimize snagging, pulling, or breakage. It can smooth thick, wavy hair in under 15 minutes, leaving it sleek, shiny, and noticeably less frizzy.

If you’re shopping for a new blow dryer brush, this is a great pick. It’s perfect for anyone who loves a smooth, bouncy blowout but doesn’t want all the bells and whistles of an Airwrap.

One Cosmo editor says: “I was thrilled to find out Dyson had created a one-stop tool for drying and styling, complete with upgraded bristle technology. I want to preserve as many of my precious strands as possible, and what I love most about this tool (besides the low-for-Dyson price) is that its bristles aren’t your basic plastic teeth that tug at your hair. Instead, they bend with your strands as you pull the brush through, releasing them rather than fighting against the tension. I usually finish styling with a solid clump of hair left behind on my tool, but with the Airsmooth, there were truly fewer than five strands caught in the bristles.” —JH

Why we love it: An update from the iconic and original Supersonic, this even-more-high-tech dryer features a Scalp Protect mode that uses sensors to detect how close the tool is to your head and automatically adjusts the heat to help maintain a scalp temperature of approximately 131°F. Translation: You can dry your roots without blasting your scalp with so much heat that you flinch when your dryer gets too close.

It also features intelligent attachments that remember your preferred settings, plus a Pause Detect function that reduces airflow when you put the dryer down. And, of course, you still get the powerful, fast-drying performance Dyson is known for.

One Cosmo editor says: “I have the Supersonic at my parents' house (I gifted my old one to my mom a few years ago), but the Nural is at mine. This is the more high-tech version of the Supersonic, which automatically shifts air flow and heat based on how close the tool is to your scalp. If blow dryers often feel like they're burning off your scalp, this one will turn that around.” —BG

Dyson hair tools are definitely not one-size-fits-all, and what works wonders on someone’s fine, straight hair might not do the same for thick curls or coils.

Before dropping hundreds of dollars on a Dyson, ask yourself: What do I actually want this thing to do? If you’re constantly switching up your hairstyle, the Airwrap’s versatility might justify the splurge. But if your goal is simply to dry your hair quickly, you probably don't need all those attachments—a Supersonic will do the trick. Love a sleek, straight finish? Try the Airstrait. Simply consider which tools you’d actually see yourself using every day before taking the plunge.

Short answer: Heck yeah! There’s a reason Dyson hair tools have earned cult-favorite status among us beauty editors, hairstylists, and pretty much everyone on TikTok. We’ve tested practically every Dyson out there, and we’re consistently impressed. For thick, long, or doesn’t-want-to-stay-styled hair, these can be especially worth the money.

Of course, there’s no denying that they’re a serious investment. So if you’re looking for a budget alternative, there are plenty of options out there too. But in our experience, the Dyson’s quick-drying capabilities sans excessive heat damage, customizable settings, and long-lasting results make them worth the splurge—especially if you’re someone who styles their hair regularly. And while you certainly don't need to own every Dyson tool (your bank account can thank us later), finding the right one for your hair type and styling goals can be a total game changer.

Catharine Malzahn is a contributing beauty writer at Cosmo. She’s written about all things hair, from cool hair color trends to the best haircuts worth trying. She’s an avid Airwrap user and has tested several versions of the hair styler over the years.

Catharine Malzahn is a contributing beauty writer at Cosmopolitan and was previously the beauty assistant at Good Housekeeping, Woman's Day, and Prevention.

#6664. Sisley Paris Matriarch Madame Isabelle d’Ornano on Hosting, Family Traditions, and Her Beloved Cushions

The beauty icon talks grand dinners, decorating rules, and Christmas.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Welcome to In Good Company, where we ring in the festive season with fashion-insider entertaining tips, expert etiquette advice, and the perfect editor-approved hostess gifts.

Madame Isabelle d’Ornano’s early gatherings at her family’s Loire Valley estate were dressy affairs. Over time, the Sisley Paris matriarch—whose plant-based approach helped redefine the beauty industry—let the formality fall away. Grand dinners became generous buffets of moussaka, poached eggs over lentil salad, and even “pizza à la d’Ornano.” The mood is more relaxed now, but the thoughtfulness remains—a sensibility she shares in her new book, Imagine: Words & Cushions, and has passed on to her children, including Countess Christine d’Ornano.

“I started to embroider the words ‘love’ and ‘peace.’ People wanted to buy my cushions. I said no,” she remembers with a laugh. “My cushions are much, much too precious.”

That sense of personal attachment runs through d’Ornano’s home, too. At 89, she lives on Paris’s Left Bank in a maximalist duplex layered with Henri Cartier-Bresson photos and objects by Jean-François Fourtou. (She shared the home with her late husband, Hubert d’Ornano, with whom she co-founded Sisley.) It’s also where she welcomes family for the holidays, a tradition that reflects her easy approach to entertaining: She likes a mélange of guests, from the glamorous to those for whom an invitation “feels like a treat.”

Her family knows just how much the cushions mean to her—and still hopes to get one. “My daughter-in-law and my granddaughter say, ‘If you give me one present, give me a cushion,’” she says. “Because I’m quite stingy with my cushions.”

If it’s a big party, the more glamorous people you have, the better. But you also need people who really like you, and some for whom it’s a treat to be there. It’s a mixture.

I would not be able to live in a home that has been created and decorated by an architect, because I like having my mess and books around. You must not have too many rules.

Christmas is a wonderful tradition in our home. I made a cushion embroidered with “Noël”—it’s such a lovely word in French. Our family has come together every Christmas since I got married. And of course, we love presents, too.

A version of this story appears in the December 2026/January 2027 issue of ELLE.

#6663. Why Peptide Skincare Might Actually Be Worth Your While, According to Experts

The recent buzz around peptides came to a head last month when U.S. Secretary of Health and Human Services (HHS) Robert F. Kennedy Jr. announced that six unregulated injectables, including the highly controversial “Wolverine stack” BPC-157 and TB-500, will now be available for broad use throughout the United States. Meanwhile, unsurprising to derms and estheticians everywhere, the rising tide of interest in peptides has spilled over into skincare, where the molecules have long served as an active ingredient.

“I’ve had a lot of patients come in since the HHS ruling,” says Erik Peterson, MD, a board-certified dermatologist in New York City, who likens the recent policy change to “opening the floodgates,” resulting in a surge of interest—particularly from patients who’ve come across claims of peptide-based skincare being like ‘botox in a bottle.’

“It’s interesting how they are suddenly so popular, because peptides have been around for like 30 years in skincare,” says Malika Sloan, medical esthetician and founder of Sloan Studio in New York City. “They’ve been incorporated into cosmetic formulations for decades, with ingredients like matrixyl helping to popularize them in the early 2000s. What’s exciting now is how much more sophisticated the formulations have become.”

If you’re curious about peptide-based skincare products—what they potentially can do, what they definitely cannot—and which products experts recommend for the best results, here’s everything you need to know.

Peptides, regardless of whether they’re injected via syringe or applied topically with a serum or cream, are short chains of amino acids that instruct your body to execute specific functions. Insulin, for example, is a peptide that helps the body regulate blood sugar, and it’s been in use since the 1920s. More recently, GLP-1 drugs (short for glucagon-like peptide-1) have been found to curb food cravings and slow stomach emptying, making them highly effective for weight loss. The list of uses for peptides is virtually endless, ranging from muscle growth and wound healing to balancing hormones and managing inflammation.

In skincare, topical peptides work much the same way as their injectable counterparts, but on a more localized level, directing your skin to carry out specific functions, “the first of which tends to be increasing production of collagen and elastin, which are two of the major determinants of skin quality,” says Dr. Peterson. They’ve also been shown to help even out skin tone, repair the skin barrier, and reduce the appearance of fine lines and wrinkles.

The world of peptides is still a bit of a Wild West. A quick Google search for peptide skincare will yield dozens of results for unresearched compounds promising you flawless skin. And then, of course, there are the influencers. “You see all these kids on TikTok who are talking about their beauty routines, and they’re like 19 years old,” says Peterson. “It’s like, ‘Amazing, your skin obviously looks great, because you’re a child.’” In reality, the list of peptides that you can truly depend on to move the needle (without an actual needle) is surprisingly short, at least until more research is done.

“The two peptides that are out right now that have any sort of viable data on them are palmitoyl pentapeptide-4 and GHK-Cu,” says Peterson. Signaling peptides, as they’re called—such as palmitoyl pentapeptide-4, better known as matrixyl—have some of the strongest clinical and anecdotal evidence for stimulating collagen and reducing the appearance of wrinkles. Although, as Peterson points out, much of the scientific data has been industry funded, “so we always have to be a bit skeptical.”

Carrier peptides—like GHK-Cu, a popular copper peptide—on the other hand, “have some good lab data, but very minimal human data,” Peterson says. Nonetheless, products containing GHK-Cu are widely heralded for their ability to repair damaged tissue, reduce inflammation, and stimulate production of collagen and elastin. “I find copper peptides particularly interesting because of the research surrounding tissue repair and collagen-related remodeling pathways,” says Sloan.

As for acetyl hexapeptide-8, or argireline, as it’s more commonly known—aka “botox in a bottle”—don’t waste your money. “From a physiological perspective, it can’t possibly work,” says Peterson, who explains that the molecules of this particular peptide are simply too large to pass through the surface of the skin. He compares it to skincare products that contain collagen rather than spur the body to produce its own. “Collagen is a massive molecule,” he says. “It would be like putting cow’s milk on your face—it’s not going to do anything.” Sure enough, a 2023 study found the effects of topical argireline to be “not statistically significant.”

Instead, for those looking for the most noticeable results, Sloan recommends seeking out products that combine proven peptides with complementary, similarly validated ingredients—like SkinCeuticals’ P-TIOX Anti-Wrinkle Peptide Cream. “I like that the cream also incorporates ceramides, niacinamide, and moisturizing ingredients, making it an interesting option for someone looking to address both hydration and visible signs of aging,” she says. Peterson’s pick, The Rich Cream from Augustinus Bader, flanks signaling peptides with hyaluronic acid and vitamin E to improve moisture retention and bolster the skin’s protective barrier.

Peptides are a relatively low-maintenance addition to most skin regimens. “One of the reasons I love incorporating peptides into at-home skincare is that they’re generally well tolerated and relatively easy to combine with other ingredients,” says Sloan, who notes that peptides can typically be used alongside other popular actives like vitamin C, niacinamide, hyaluronic acid, and retinoids. “I typically recommend incorporating a peptide serum after cleansing and before moisturizer,” she says.

Just keep in mind that peptides alone won’t take the place of a well-rounded skincare regimen. “The best, most robust data still comes from the tried-and-true favorites of all dermatologists—protecting your collagen with consistent sunscreen use, and the best signaling molecule of them all: topical retinoids,” Peterson says.

And, of course, don’t expect overnight results—despite what social media might have you believe. “Peptides are just signals for your skin, not magic in a bottle,” Peterson says. “The few studies that there are show at least three months of consistent usage before results are seen, so stick it out if you want to give it a real try.”

#6661. Is Social Media Really the Great Equalizer? Here’s What Hulu’s ‘The Circle’ Host Tinx Has to Say (Exclusive)

I sat down with Christina Najjar to talk alliances, authenticity, and the responsibility of being the “internet’s big sister.”

After a seven-season run on Netflix, The Circle has found a new home on Hulu, and things look very, very different. Rather than “normies,” the cast is now made up of familiar faces from the Walt Disney Company reality TV ecosystem and micro-influencers disguised as civilians. (Even the player with the most modest initial following, Cyrus Irani, had over 10,000 followers at the time his casting was announced. Is it a problem? Not necessarily. But it’s certainly a switch-up from season 1’s social media skeptic Shubham Goel.)

The revival series is also airing in near-real time, with new episodes dropping Tuesday through Thursday and audience voting being used to shape the process. And perhaps the biggest change: stepping into hosting duties in place of comedian and actor Michelle Buteau is SiriusXM’s Christina “Tinx” Najjar, who rose to fame on social media during the COVID-19 pandemic.

As a longtime Circle devotee, I was...curious about what these changes might mean for my favorite competition series. So when I got the chance to fly to Atlanta to visit the set (and meet Tinx) myself, I took it. There I chatted with her about the power of social media, the potential problem of having celebs in the Circle, and the player she thinks might win it all.

I truly couldn’t believe it. I know how many loyal fans The Circle has, so when I got the call that I got it—I mean, I’m still pinching myself every day. It’s a really big moment for me, and I know I have big shoes to fill. And I really just want to make everybody proud.

I remember watching the very first episode and thinking, “Wow, this is such a fresh concept, and it’s truly a social experiment for the modern age.” So the fact that I’m here all these years later is truly a pinch-me moment.

I relate to it so much, because in COVID, I was living in a studio apartment in L.A. I had just moved. I didn’t have a lot of friends, and all I had was my phone. That’s why I initially picked up TikTok and thought, “Who knows what’s going to happen in the world? I might as well make a few videos and try to make friends online.” And as soon as I started creating content, I just realized, “Wow, I love connecting with people. I love entertaining people.” It’s been a wild six years ever since.

I grew up doing theater in high school, but honestly, no. I truthfully had a lot of limiting beliefs about myself. I just thought there was no way I could ever be an influencer. I didn’t feel pretty enough. I didn’t think I was put together enough. And I think one thing COVID taught us is that anybody who has a dream can go for it. Social media is really the great equalizer in that sense, because if you are funny, if you have something to say, you can get online and you can connect with people.

It’s such an honor, and I love that people look up to me for advice. It’s the honor of my life that people trust me enough to call me on my radio show for advice or write to me, and I just try to be a voice of reason and remind women to know their worth. I feel like this is a journey I was meant to be on, and all of the opportunities that being online has afforded me, because it really has given me so much. The fact that I’m sitting here as the host of The Circle is—again, I can’t believe it.

It’s so different because I’m used to just holding up my phone, recording, cutting quickly, and pressing post. This is a totally different operation, but the crew is so cool, production is so cool, and the writers that I work with are just so smart and so funny. I was actually shocked when they explained to me the process of how close to the wire we are. It is as close to real time as you could ever think.

Because I have lived online for six years, I post in real time myself. I’m someone who wakes up and I’m like, “Hey guys, I’m going to the gym. Here’s what I’m eating for breakfast.” I don’t know if I would know how to do it any other way, because I’m just, like, an oversharer, so I think I would just be myself and see what happened. But I can be strategic. I feel like I would make a lot of alliances. I’m good at that.

I honestly think maybe the celebrities are at a slight disadvantage because, as you say, people have perceptions about them. I feel like they also have more of a target on their backs.

He was shocked. He had such a good strategy. I was honestly shocked he got out, too. He’s so likable, and everyone knew it was really him. He got got. He got got by Justin [Hinsley], if I’m being honest, who has very strong strategy. I think Justin is incredibly strategic and knows how to play the game.

I mean, Hannah [Brown], obviously. I don’t think she knows how to play any other way. She’s so genuine, and she’s definitely playing with her heart. So we’ll see how that fares and whether it’s strategy or heart that wins.

I think it’s totally fine, because there’s a lot on the line here. This is a popularity contest, but there’s a big prize at the end. So I think it’s fun when people are ruthless. Why not? It’s a game, and games are meant to be won.

#6658. 10 Sophisticated Gourmand Colognes Perfect for Winter

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Sugar and spice and everything nice—but not too sweet.

If there’s one thing you can trust us on, it’s that we’re always keeping our noses on top of the latest fragrance trends. Right now, one is undeniably taking over, just in time for the cold weather: sophisticated gourmands. If “gourmand” brings to mind the sugary cupcake body sprays your sister bought at the mall as a teenager, you’re not alone in that. But these grown-up “neo-gourmands” take the comforting, familiar sweetness of edible notes and balance them with dry woods, spice, or smoke. The result? A little less syrup, a lot more intrigue and sophistication.

Instead of buttercream and chocolate sauce, think tonka bean and smoke, bitter cacao and rum, toasted sesame and musk, or vanilla and cardamom. Even coffee-scented colognes are taking off right now. There are so many delicious new gourmand combinations out there that narrowing them all down to just ten was no easy feat. But we did it, and these are our favorites right now. Consider it your invitation to smell very good (and maybe a little mouthwatering) all winter long.

When it comes to gourmands, easily one of the most common base notes is vanilla. And in fragrance, vanilla definitely does not mean boring—quite the opposite, in fact. Henry Rose’s Dave fragrance combines vanilla and tonka bean at the base for a creamy foundation that’s sweet, but not overwhelmingly sweet.

At the top, we get some nice earthy, slightly spicy notes of tangerine, neroli, and pink pepper, and its heart is softer with florals like orange flower and almond blossom. Base notes of vanilla bean, cocoa, and creamy tonka bean leave you with a smooth, velvety finish.

The great thing about Solférino’s Lumière d’Automne is that it’s both sweet and smoky, but not too much of either. It’s the perfect blend of light gourmand notes (namely vanilla and benzoin) with the hint of smoke that comes from notes of roasted chestnut. The name literally translates to “Autumn Light” and evokes the cozy feeling of wearing a sweater on a chilly day while colorful leaves fall to the ground.

For the past two years, Chai Fleur has been my go-to scent the moment the temperature drops below 50 degrees. I’m not a coffee person, but I do drink a chai latte every day of my life (honestly, I don’t know how I would get through my day without it), and somehow Belnu has captured the scent of chai and turned it into the perfect winter fragrance. It’s sweet and creamy thanks to the base of vanilla and woods, and it has the perfect amount of spice from nutmeg, cinnamon, and green cardamom.

While not technically a gourmand fragrance, Fulton & Roark’s Calle Ocho has notes of bitter chocolate and tonka bean, which give it that velvety smooth feel that many gourmands have, even if it doesn’t smell quite as sweet as a true gourmand. When blended with cinnamon, rum, florals, smoky tobacco leaf, and musk, the chocolate and tonka bean are transformed into something dark, boozy, sexy, and very sophisticated.

Sometimes, you don’t have to spend more than $100 to smell like you have your life together. In fact, the whole goal of House of Twist is to make affordable fragrances (under $50!) that smell just like your favorite luxury colognes. Liquid Amber No.173 is a spicy gourmand akin to something like Parfums de Marly’s Althaïr (which we also absolutely love), with spicy cinnamon and cardamom, creamy bourbon vanilla, woods, and musks.

Almond is another one of those creamy, nutty scents like pistachio or sesame that thrives in gourmand fragrances. My favorite example of this is Nicolaï Paris’s Macaron Bourbon, an ambery gourmand centered around bitter almond, sweet rum, davana, and patchouli. It’s smooth and creamy without being too rich, so it doesn’t overwhelm you with sweetness. The bitter almond and spices also add an interesting nuttiness to the fragrance, which we love.

My favorite dessert has got to be a warm sticky toffee pudding with a scoop of vanilla ice cream on top. I never thought there would be a fragrance that captured the warm deliciousness of those exact flavors, but D.S. & Durga, one of our favorite niche fragrance houses, has done just that. Ain’t That Sweet is inspired by a slice of malty date cake and combines notes of cinnamon, date, praline, condensed milk, bourbon vanilla, and nutmeg for an addictive gourmand scent that we’re frankly obsessed with.

You may not expect floral and gourmand notes to go together, but they actually balance each other out quite nicely. In Van Cleef & Arpels’ Orchidée Vanille, the light floral notes of rose and violet lend a nice airiness to the rich gourmand notes of bitter almond, vanilla, and tonka bean. The effect is something sweet but delicate—it’s not the “manliest” of scents in the traditional sense, but we’re very pro-florals when it comes to men’s cologne.

Another one of my favorite new gourmands that launched this year is Abel’s Double Scoop, which truly evokes a sweet, nutty ice cream (my favorite kind). But it’s more nuanced than that—it starts off with herbaceous, slightly woody rosemary before revealing the rich notes of toasted black sesame, butterscotch, and vanilla. The dry down is nice and musky, which gives it a sweet but muted lingering aroma.

Finally, if you do want something rich that still feels grown-up and not too sugary, Free Yourself’s Savor is the way to go. It’s a true gourmand with notes of bittersweet chocolate, pistachio, toasted almond, tonka bean, vanilla, and even marshmallow—but it never feels like an overly sweet, juvenile scent in the way you might think marshmallow would lend itself to. That’s because all these rich, creamy, sweet notes are balanced out by plum, rose, and tobacco, which add a more sophisticated twist to the rich gourmand.

#6657. Exclusive: Shohei Ohtani Just Signed a Historic Deal With New Balance

Shohei Ohtani is used to making history. In addition to his four MLB MVP awards and two World Series rings, the Los Angeles Dodgers two-way great is the first member of the 50/50 club (having racked up 54 home runs and 59 stolen bases during the 2024 season), became the first player to hit three homers and record 10 strikeouts in a single game (which he managed in a 2025 NLCS contest against the Brewers), and earlier this year maintained simultaneous streaks in games on base (48) and innings without an earned run (32 and two-thirds).

Now, the 32-year-old Japanese icon is adding to those accolades with a pioneering endorsement deal, officially extending his relationship with his longtime partner New Balance through to the end of his baseball career. (While a rarefied group of athletes have signed lifetime sponsorship deals—including LeBron James and Cristiano Ronaldo at Nike, and Damian Lillard at Adidas—Ohtani’s new contract appears to be both the first to exclusively cover a star’s playing days and the first of its kind in MLB.)

“There’s a lot of trust,” Ohtani tells me through an interpreter during a sit-down in Boston of his decision to commit to the New England sportswear stalwart for the long haul. “We’ve obviously built friendships, as well. They’ve been able to accommodate my requests, the things I’ve asked for, and, like I said, that takes the involvement of a lot of trust. That relationship has been very, very good and smooth. I had no reservations about extending our relationship.”

Earlier in the day, I shadowed Ohtani at New Balance’s headquarters, where, as part of the agreement, the company is renaming its on-site think tank as the Shohei Ohtani Sports Research Lab. A team of specialists led the Dodgers superstar through a slew of shoes—including his on-field Ohtani 1 model, which will receive new editions in 2027—and then a rack of branded apparel. All of it falls under his Shohei Ohtani Signature Collection, which New Balance launched in 2024 (about a year and a half after the athlete first signed with the company). The collection has grown deliberately and unhurriedly ever since—mirroring his patience in the batter’s box; Ohtani is methodical when it comes to product development and uninterested in rolling out rushed goods with his name slapped on them.

While conducting a kind of footwear postmortem on his 2026 season—which started well, but ended with a rare slump (and left knee and right biceps injuries that kept him from pitching after July 3)—Ohtani noted there’d been some moments of slippage on the heel of his landing foot when pitching. On a printed-out diagram, he used a pencil to sketch ways in which the shoe’s spikes might be slightly reconfigured to find a sweet spot for stability on the mound and speed around the bases. The question of switching footwear between pitching and batting comes up, but Ohtani has not yet done this (and it seems the consensus is to stick with one shoe only).

“I have a lot of direct input into the footwear and apparel,” Ohtani says. “That includes my style, which I like to keep simple, as well as performance-based requests, [like with my] spikes. Some years we make minor changes, and sometimes we’ll make a big change. They understand what I want.”

Ohtani’s apparel line runs across three categories: performance, lifestyle, and fanwear. His custom logo—a striking ink drawing of his figure, designed by the Japanese manga artist Takehiko Inoue—is featured subtly on the bulk of the lineup. “Shohei doesn’t see himself as a glamorous player,” Chris Davis, New Balance brand president and chief marketing officer, tells me. “He sees himself as a gritty, hardworking athlete who wants to get better every day, and his logo reflects this—it has this kind of quiet intensity.”

While some of the future apparel designs boast cool technical features—like detachable, two-in-one, and reversible elements inspired by Ohtani’s two-way play—the man himself is only concerned that his products “fit comfortably and to be easy to wear.” When asked if there’s anyone whose style he likes or is influenced by, Ohtani shrugs. “Honestly, not really. I just like to wear what I like to wear.”

“[Shohei] is very quick and clear with his input,” says Masahiro Uchida, New Balance’s general manager of athlete signature product, who has worked alongside Ohtani for years. “On the performance side, he always prefers softness and a soft hand feel. For the lifestyle side, he prefers clean and simple. He also understands the occasion. He always asks us, ‘Where would I wear this?’” One major goal for Ohtani with his apparel, Uchida adds, is to “support the expansion of baseball culture and show his respect for its heritage. He is a super geek about MLB. He knows so much.”

At 32, the duration of Ohtani’s career—and, now, his New Balance deal—remains an open question. That age can be considered as getting toward the older end in pro baseball, but elite pitchers have a habit of playing well into their 40s—Justin Verlander, for example, just pitched his final season at 43.

Ohtani still plans on being a full two-way player in 2027, with hopes for a Cy Young Award—MLB’s top pitching honor, one of the few laurels he has yet to win—still alive. When the Dodgers’ season ended on October 15, Ohtani told the press that he was “not quite satisfied with how things shaped up on the pitching side.”

What words of advice or motivation does he have, I ask, for the legion of kids hoping to follow in his footsteps? His answer feels like something he might be saying to himself as the next season approaches.

“If you want to become successful, baseball is a sport that takes a lot of repetitive practice,” he says. “You always have to work on your fundamentals. Once you reach a certain level, the sport becomes very, very hard. At that point, you need to remember why you’re playing. Are you playing because you love the sport? If you have that drive, that love for it, then even when it’s tough, you can overcome it.”

#6655. Can 4 Minutes of Exercise a Day Really Improve Your Fitness?

Brief strength training can boost balance and leg strength.

Committing to any level of exercise isn’t easy, even whether you’re a gym regular. But a recent Penn State College of Medicine study may be music to the ears of anyone looking to get active with minimal effort—especially older adults. The findings suggest that four minutes a day of strength training helped improve leg strength and balance in adults 65 and older. The benefits are significant for study participants, but whether the results translate to younger folks is up for debate. Dr. Harry Pino, director of human performance and exercise physiology at Yunara, unpacks if four minutes of exercise can help make gains or if it’s all too good to be true.

Time may not matter as much as you think. But before diving into the details of your workout, Pino suggests figuring out your goals and assessing your current fitness level. If you’re unsure which exercises work best for your end results, Pino offers this breakdown:

“The best four-minute workout is one that challenges your body appropriately. Intensity matters, but consistency and progressive improvement are what drive long-term results,” Pino tells ELLE.

It may seem insignificant and the warm-up may take longer than the exercises themselves, but Pino says four minutes can make a measurable difference, especially for someone who’s been sedentary. “Research shows that brief daily strength workouts can improve balance, leg strength, and mobility in older adults. These improvements directly support physical function and independence as we age.”

The good news is yes, absolutely. According to Pino, exercise benefits are cumulative, and even brief sessions can stimulate positive adaptations when performed consistently. “Four minutes won’t replace a comprehensive fitness routine, but it’s an excellent starting point. Small, sustainable habits often produce the greatest long-term impact,” he says.

Focus on exercises that match your goals and fitness level. Pino’s recommendation is to start with a simple routine that might include 30 seconds each of squats, push-ups, step-ups, and resistance band rows, alternating with 30 seconds of recovery. “Prioritize proper form and gradually increase the challenge as your fitness improves,” he specifies.

Even a short workout benefits from a warm-up, particularly at higher intensities. Start with gentle movement to prepare your muscles and joints. Afterward, prioritize hydration, adequate nutrition, and quality sleep. Pino notes that exercise provides the stimulus, but recovery allows the body to adapt.

There is no one-size-fits-all workout routine, no matter your body type or how active you are. “Fitness level, strength, and mobility are much more important than body shape when designing a workout. Beginners may benefit from modified movements, while more experienced individuals can increase resistance or intensity. The goal is to challenge the body safely and progressively," says Pino.

The Penn State study only studied an older set of adults, primarily to understand how to prevent falls and balance concerns that can come with age. While Pino explained that these findings can have a positive impact for anyone looking to get active, he agrees that short workouts can be especially valuable for older adults. “They’re accessible and easier to maintain consistently. Even a few minutes of daily strength training can improve balance, mobility, and functional strength. These improvements are critical for preserving independence and quality of life as we age,” Pino says.

#6654. Bella Hadid Opens Up About Being Rejected From the Victoria’s Secret Fashion Show

Bella Hadid has become one of the biggest supermodels in the world, and this year, she even opened the 2026 Victoria’s Secret Fashion Show—a runway on which she has become a regular. However, her success is the result of a lot of work, and many rejections.

In her December 2026/January 2027 cover story for Harper’s Bazaar, Hadid opens up about a time, over a decade ago, when she was rejected after auditioning for the VS show. In a conversation with Bazaar’s editor in chief, Samira Nasr, Hadid is asked how it felt to come full circle. Nasr recalls: “On one of our first shoots together, you were really upset because you didn’t get the Victoria’s Secret show. And I said to you, ‘One day, they will be begging you to be in their show.’ And I could tell you didn’t believe me. Your whole body shut down. And three days ago, you opened Victoria’s Secret. Holy shit.”

Hadid admits that she often feels like she’s in a daydream. “I still don’t believe you, to be honest,” the Orebella founder says to Nasr. “I feel like people are just AI-ing me onto the runway. I think that it’s so crazy how time passes and you still have some kind of impostor syndrome.”

She goes on to add: “No matter how hard you work, there’s always another girl who is more deserving, more beautiful, and has worked so hard to be able to get that spot. Ten years ago, I remember that moment too, where I never thought I would get the show, but I think that God always has a plan, and I truly believe at that time I wasn’t ready. I don’t think I’ve been ready until this year, to be honest. In past years, I was going to my horse-show finals in the morning, flying straight to the Victoria’s Secret show that day, doing rehearsal right before, and then pulling what we call a Bella, and Belinda comes out, and we just act. But this year, I felt really strong.”

Over the years, Hadid has opened up about turning to an alter ego, named Belinda, to handle the pressures of her career. However, when Nasr asks if she feels like it was her or Belinda walking the Victoria’s Secret runway this time, Hadid responds: “You know what? Fortunately and unfortunately, I think it was Bella this year.”

#6653. Most Black Friday Sales Are Fake. These Ones Aren’t.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

The Internet is a deep, dark place this week, but we found where the deals are worth it.

How do you even define a good deal anymore? The whole point of Black Friday was for retailers to make us wake up at the crack of dawn the day after Thanksgiving, wait in line, and then fight someone over a TV that was 50-percent off at Sears or Best Buy. How did they do that? They would sell a few products (door busters) at a loss just to physically bring people into the store. Those deals were good, and everything else, which you would buy because you were already there, was where the money was made.

That is now a thing of the past. Lines and physical altercations aside, in the past 20 years there has been a real swing in the favor of big retailers. First, we created Cyber Monday (though most people do their Black Friday shopping online already). Then the deals started a week earlier. Now, they last all month. And instead of having door busters to get you in the door, retailers just have very aggressive online marketing programs. In fact, it turns out that most Black Friday deals aren’t even deals at all. In a recent study, it was found that one in three Amazon Black Friday deals provided no true savings at all—prices were marked up earlier in the year, only to be brought back down to a standard retail value. The big retailers like Amazon and Walmart are the most to blame for these tricky strategies, but you should still be judicious when looking at any so-called Black Friday sales.

The good news? Black Friday is still filled with great deals—you just have to know where to look and what to expect. A lot of big wins revolve around private sales at more niche, direct-to-consumer stores. For example, Filson, one of our favorite outerwear brands, is having a 25-percent-off sale for Black Friday. That’s not the 75-percent-off you might have wanted, and there are other times that Filson goes 25-percent off. But the best strategy these days is to think about holiday shopping: Just buy the things you already would, but do it while they’re on sale now.

You should be especially skeptical of tech products. The increase in all tech prices has made it easy for those brands to push a 10-percent-off sale when products where 10-percent-off the current price. This is the sort of stuff we’ve been watching as the sales roll around. Based on this and years of covering sales and knowing what’s good and what’s not, we determined these are the best deals of the weekend. These are the deals our editors think are actually worth shopping this Black Friday.

Mr. Porter has an absolute goldmine of a sale. Like sure, you can grab this Brunello Cucinelli Jacket for $6,500 at 50-percent off. But it’s also filled with basics from brands like On that rarely go on sale.

These Nike socks are more fun than the pairs they sell on amazon. And they’re $6. Also better than Amazon.

$644 for a cashmere-blend overcoat that’s normally $1,298 is a real-life steal.

A few days ago, the Samsung Frame Pro was 50-percent off. That deal was real, and it sold out. Today the Hisense Art TV (which we’re fans of) is 50-percent off. These deals vary daily, but today, this is the one we’d buy.

Dyson deals are confusing as fuck. You need to be knowledgeable about the model you’re buying into, and here that number matters. You will be able to find models like the V8 and the V10 for super cheap this week—but that’s because those models are old and outdated. Right now, the best and newest Dyson cordless vacuum is the V16—with all the bells and whistles it retails at $1,100, but it’s $200 off right now.

But as someone who has used every Dyson, I know the V12 the best. And while it isn’t the newest, it still has lots of the same features as newer models and will last you years. So the fact that it’s on sale for $400 this weekend makes it a truly good deal. Also, I can’t find this deal on Amazon. For the best deals from Dyson, I would always check its actual site.

As previously mentioned, we love that many of Filson’s jackets are 25-percent off this week. This is a good example of when a brand just wants to participate in a sale, so they’re willing to mark down popular items. This jacket is so good our commerce editor Luke Guillory endorsed it last week, calling it the best $250 jacket. Guess what? Now it’s only $187. We know for a fact that this deal won’t last past Black Friday.

It’s expensive, yes, but Begg x Co. is one of the best cashmere brands anywhere in the world. $500 off a new cashmere sweater is something that doesn’t happen until the end of the season. But if you buy it now, you still have six good months of wear to get in.

Water filtration has become very expensive, especially when it comes to reverse osmosis and a countertop version that looks sleek. We have been recommending this one for over a year at $500, so the fact that it’s $150-off for once makes it a pretty good deal.

Furniture on sale is a whole category we can’t ignore. Furniture markups are a real thing, and a lot of it has to do with quality and materials. Marble is overpriced for sure, but good quality marble is worth it. We love Soho Home’s marble, and this dining table has been one of its most popular pieces for a while. Getting it for almost $3k-off when you’re not a member is a big deal.

If you’re on the home redo kick, then a brand like La Mae, which is offering some its best wood pieces at 50-percent off, is a great place to start. Made with high quality materials and oak, this is one of those furniture pieces you will own forever.

If you’re a camo guy or do any non-deal hunting, then you know that Tom Beckbe jackets never go on sale—but this is your lucky week.
